ABIM Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Histamine mediated vasodilation and increased vascular permeability throughout the body): histamine released from mast cells and basophils is the principal mediator of the rapid vasodilation, increased vascular permeability, and hypotension seen in anaphylaxis. Interleukin-5 and interferon gamma act over hours to days and do not cause acute hypotension. Tumor necrosis factor contributes to inflammation but is not the primary driver of immediate hemodynamic collapse. Complement C3a can trigger mast cell activation but is not itself the direct mediator of the acute vascular changes.
  2. The correct answer is A (Deficiency or dysfunction of the C1 esterase inhibitor protein): hereditary angioedema most commonly results from a genetic deficiency or dysfunction of C1 esterase inhibitor, leading to unchecked bradykinin generation and nonpruritic swelling without urticaria. IgA deficiency causes recurrent infections and is unrelated to angioedema. Complement factor B deficiency is not a recognized cause of angioedema. Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency causes liver and lung disease, not angioedema, and mast cell tryptase elevation reflects mast cell activation rather than bradykinin-mediated swelling.
  3. The correct answer is D (Hypersensitivity pneumonitis caused by thermophilic actinomycetes in moldy hay): exposure to moldy hay causes farmer's lung, a classic hypersensitivity pneumonitis with delayed fever, cough, and dyspnea that improves after removal from exposure. Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis presents with chronic wheezing and mucus plugging rather than acute post-exposure fever. Occupational asthma from isocyanates is linked to industrial chemical exposure, not hay. Chronic eosinophilic pneumonia and Churg-Strauss vasculitis have more indolent, systemic presentations unrelated to acute organic dust exposure.
  4. The correct answer is B (X-linked agammaglobulinemia caused by a Bruton tyrosine kinase defect): a Bruton tyrosine kinase defect halts B cell maturation, causing absent tonsils, pan-hypogammaglobulinemia, and recurrent sinopulmonary infections typically in young boys. Common variable immunodeficiency usually presents later in life with a different genetic basis. Selective IgA deficiency spares other immunoglobulin classes. DiGeorge syndrome involves T cell deficiency from thymic aplasia, and severe combined immunodeficiency involves both T and B cell defects with earlier, more severe presentation.
  5. The correct answer is E (Systemic mastocytosis with abnormal mast cell proliferation and mediator release): flushing, diarrhea, urticating skin lesions (Darier sign), and elevated tryptase reflect abnormal mast cell proliferation and mediator release characteristic of systemic mastocytosis. Carcinoid syndrome causes flushing and diarrhea but not urticarial skin lesions or elevated tryptase. Chronic spontaneous urticaria does not typically cause flushing or diarrhea. Pheochromocytoma causes episodic hypertension and flushing without skin lesions, and hereditary angioedema does not present with urticaria or elevated tryptase.
  6. The correct answer is A (Interstitial infiltrate containing eosinophils and edema on light microscopy): the clinical picture describes drug-induced allergic interstitial nephritis, classically from agents like vancomycin, and biopsy shows interstitial edema with eosinophilic and lymphocytic infiltration. Diffuse basement membrane thickening suggests diabetic nephropathy. Crescentic glomerulonephritis with linear IgG deposits describes anti-GBM disease. Mesangial IgA deposition indicates IgA nephropathy, and amyloid deposits reflect amyloidosis, none of which fit this drug-hypersensitivity presentation.
  7. The correct answer is C (Dense eosinophilic infiltrate exceeding 15 per high power field): esophageal rings, furrows, and dysphagia in an atopic patient are classic for eosinophilic esophagitis, diagnosed by a dense eosinophilic infiltrate on biopsy. Neutrophilic infiltrates suggest infectious or inflammatory bowel processes. Granulomatous inflammation suggests Crohn disease. Barrett metaplasia results from chronic reflux rather than allergic inflammation, and a lymphocytic or plasma cell predominant infiltrate does not define this eosinophil-driven condition.
  8. The correct answer is E (Allergic transfusion reaction triggered by donor plasma proteins): isolated urticaria and pruritus without fever or hemodynamic compromise during transfusion is typical of a mild allergic reaction to donor plasma proteins. Acute hemolytic reactions cause fever, back pain, and hemoglobinuria. Febrile nonhemolytic reactions present with fever and chills rather than hives. Transfusion-related lung injury causes acute respiratory distress, and circulatory overload causes dyspnea and hypertension, neither matching this isolated cutaneous presentation.
  9. The correct answer is B (Intramuscular epinephrine injected into the anterolateral thigh muscle immediately): this patient has anaphylaxis with hypotension, and intramuscular epinephrine is the first-line, life-saving treatment that must be given immediately without delay. Oral antihistamines act too slowly and do not address hypotension. Intravenous corticosteroids may have a role later but are not first-line acute therapy. Albuterol alone does not treat systemic anaphylaxis, and diagnostic testing has no place during an acute life-threatening reaction.
  10. The correct answer is D (Refer for penicillin skin testing to clarify true allergy status): a vague childhood rash history with subsequent tolerance of a related cephalosporin makes true penicillin allergy unlikely, and skin testing can safely clarify this before using a first-line agent. Permanently avoiding all beta-lactams unnecessarily limits treatment options. Giving a full dose without any evaluation risks a reaction if allergy is real. Choosing a fluoroquinolone without assessment promotes unnecessary antibiotic substitution, and prophylactic antihistamines do not reliably prevent true allergic reactions.
  11. The correct answer is A (Serum specific IgE testing or skin prick testing for peanut): after a reaction suggestive of IgE-mediated food allergy, specific IgE or skin prick testing helps confirm sensitization and guide further management. Total serum IgE is nonspecific and not diagnostic for a particular allergen. Home oral food challenges are unsafe given the risk of a more severe reaction. Empiric elimination without testing is overly broad and unnecessary, and stool eosinophil counts are not used to diagnose IgE-mediated food allergy.
  12. The correct answer is C (Omalizumab added as an anti-IgE biologic therapy option): in patients with allergic asthma poorly controlled on standard therapy who have elevated IgE and confirmed perennial allergen sensitivity, anti-IgE biologic therapy is an appropriate add-on. High-dose oral steroids carry significant long-term toxicity and are not the preferred next step. Discontinuing inhaled corticosteroids would worsen control. Empiric antibiotics are not indicated without evidence of infection, and a leukotriene antagonist alone is insufficient for this level of severity.
  13. The correct answer is B (Perinuclear antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody directed against myeloperoxidase enzyme): asthma, chronic sinusitis, eosinophilia, and mononeuritis multiplex describe eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis, which is frequently associated with perinuclear ANCA (anti-myeloperoxidase). Anti-CCP antibody is associated with rheumatoid arthritis. Anti-double stranded DNA is specific for lupus. Anti-Jo-1 relates to inflammatory myopathies, and anti-GBM antibody is associated with Goodpasture syndrome, none of which fit this vasculitic eosinophilic presentation.
  14. The correct answer is D (Immunoglobulin replacement therapy to restore protective antibody levels): recurrent infections with low IgG and impaired vaccine response indicate common variable immunodeficiency, for which immunoglobulin replacement is the mainstay of treatment to reduce infection risk. Bone marrow transplantation is reserved for more severe combined immune defects. High-dose corticosteroids would further impair immunity. Splenectomy increases infection risk rather than reducing it, and prophylactic antifungals are not the primary therapy for this antibody deficiency.
  15. The correct answer is E (Hereditary angioedema occurring despite a normal C1 inhibitor level): recurrent angioedema unresponsive to antihistamines and steroids, a strong family history, but normal C1 inhibitor level, function, and C4 is characteristic of the normal C1 inhibitor variant of hereditary angioedema, often linked to factor XII mutations. Acquired angioedema typically presents later in life with low complement and no family history. ACE inhibitor angioedema relates to a specific drug exposure. Classic type I hereditary angioedema shows low C1 inhibitor and C4, and chronic urticaria would show wheals, which are absent here.
  16. The correct answer is A (Bradykinin mediated swelling independent of mast cell degranulation): ACE inhibitor angioedema results from impaired bradykinin degradation rather than mast cell or IgE mediated pathways, explaining why epinephrine and antihistamines are ineffective. IgE cross-linking would typically respond to these standard anaphylaxis treatments. Complement mediated hemolysis and eosinophil degranulation are not the mechanisms behind ACE inhibitor angioedema, and opioid receptor stimulation is unrelated to this drug class.
  17. The correct answer is D (Systemic glucocorticoids given as the first-line induction therapy): IgG4-related disease affecting the pancreas, biliary tree, and salivary glands with characteristic storiform fibrosis on biopsy is typically treated initially with systemic glucocorticoids, which produce rapid symptomatic and radiographic improvement. Endoscopic stenting alone addresses mechanical obstruction but not the underlying inflammatory process. Proton pump inhibitors and ursodeoxycholic acid do not target the immune-mediated fibroinflammatory process. Pancreatectomy is not indicated for this medically treatable condition.
  18. The correct answer is C (Allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis with IgE mediated Aspergillus sensitization): the combination of asthma, migratory infiltrates, central bronchiectasis, elevated total IgE, and Aspergillus-specific IgE is classic for allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis. Chronic eosinophilic pneumonia does not typically show central bronchiectasis or Aspergillus-specific IgE. Invasive aspergillosis occurs in immunocompromised hosts with tissue invasion, not this allergic pattern. Loffler syndrome is self-limited with transient infiltrates, and organizing pneumonia does not feature elevated IgE or Aspergillus sensitization.
  19. The correct answer is E (Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis unmasked by steroid tapering): leukotriene receptor antagonists can allow tapering of systemic steroids that had been masking underlying eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis, unmasking vasculitis with neuropathy, purpura, and marked eosinophilia in a patient with asthma and sinusitis. Drug-induced lupus presents differently with different serologies. Simple allergic vasculitis does not typically cause this degree of eosinophilia or neuropathy. Polyarteritis nodosa and granulomatosis with polyangiitis do not typically feature this striking eosinophilia or the classic asthma and sinusitis history.
  20. The correct answer is B (Type IV delayed hypersensitivity reaction causing allergic contact dermatitis): a sharply demarcated, weeping rash confined to the area of contact with a new metal watch band reflects T cell mediated delayed hypersensitivity to nickel, distinct from the patient's baseline atopic dermatitis pattern. IgE mediated immediate hypersensitivity would cause urticaria rather than a localized eczematous eruption. Stress-related exacerbation would not be so sharply localized. Staphylococcal infection would show crusting and pustules rather than this pattern, and photoallergic reactions occur in sun-exposed areas, not under a watch band.
  21. The correct answer is E (Stage 2 hypertension at or above 140 over 90 mmHg): Under the current ACC/AHA classification, a blood pressure at or above 140/90 mmHg on repeated measurement is stage 2 hypertension. Elevated blood pressure and stage 1 hypertension refer to lower ranges. Hypertensive urgency requires a much higher reading, typically above 180/120, and normal blood pressure is below 120/80.
  22. The correct answer is C (Coarctation of the aorta narrowing distal to the left subclavian artery): Upper extremity hypertension with weak, delayed femoral pulses relative to radial pulses and an interscapular murmur is the classic presentation of aortic coarctation. Renal artery stenosis and aortic dissection do not produce a radial-femoral pulse delay. Patent ductus arteriosus causes a continuous murmur without this pulse discrepancy, and Takayasu arteritis typically affects older adolescents or young women with more diffuse findings.
  23. The correct answer is C (Acute pericarditis causing positional pleuritic pain with diffuse ECG changes): Pleuritic, positional chest pain that improves leaning forward, along with diffuse ST elevation and PR depression, is the classic presentation of acute pericarditis. Myocardial infarction produces regional rather than diffuse ST changes and is not typically positional. Aortic dissection causes tearing pain unrelated to position, pulmonary embolism causes pleuritic pain without this ECG pattern, and costochondritis does not cause ECG changes.
  24. The correct answer is B (Beck triad of hypotension, distended neck veins, and muffled heart sounds): Hypotension, jugular venous distension, and muffled heart sounds together constitute Beck triad, the classic bedside finding of cardiac tamponade. The other listed symptom groupings describe unrelated conditions such as pneumonia, endocarditis, or constrictive pericarditis, not the acute tamponade triad.
  25. The correct answer is A (Kussmaul sign, a paradoxical jugular rise seen in constrictive pericarditis): A paradoxical rise in jugular venous pressure with inspiration is Kussmaul sign, classically seen in constrictive pericarditis where the rigid pericardium prevents the right heart from accommodating increased venous return during inspiration. Pulsus paradoxus is an exaggerated fall in systolic pressure with inspiration, seen in tamponade, not a jugular venous finding. Water hammer pulse and Corrigan sign describe the bounding pulse of aortic regurgitation, and the Levine sign refers to a clenched fist gesture associated with angina.
  26. The correct answer is D (Stable angina with a consistent, predictable exertional symptom pattern): Predictable, reproducible chest discomfort with exertion that resolves with rest and has remained unchanged in pattern over months is the definition of stable angina. Unstable angina involves new, worsening, or rest symptoms. Prinzmetal angina occurs at rest from coronary vasospasm, non-cardiac chest pain is unrelated to exertion in this pattern, and silent ischemia by definition lacks symptoms.
  27. The correct answer is C (ST elevation myocardial infarction affecting the anterior coronary territory): New ST elevation of 1 mm or more in two contiguous leads, here 3 mm across the anterior precordial leads with reciprocal changes, meets criteria for STEMI. NSTEMI and unstable angina do not show ST elevation. Pericarditis produces diffuse rather than localized ST elevation without reciprocal depression, and a baseline bundle branch block would not typically show this focal pattern with reciprocal change.
  28. The correct answer is B (Ischemic cardiomyopathy from prior infarction causing reduced ejection fraction): A depressed ejection fraction with regional wall motion abnormalities corresponding to a prior infarct territory defines ischemic cardiomyopathy, the most common cause of heart failure with reduced ejection fraction. Hypertrophic and restrictive cardiomyopathies have distinct structural patterns unrelated to infarct territory, stress-induced cardiomyopathy is triggered by acute stress with a different wall motion pattern, and myocarditis is inflammatory rather than related to prior infarction.
  29. The correct answer is A (Gastroesophageal reflux disease causing postprandial and nocturnal burning pain): Postprandial and nocturnal burning chest discomfort relieved by antacids, with a normal cardiac workup, is characteristic of GERD. Microvascular angina and pericarditis would not typically respond to antacids, pulmonary embolism causes acute pleuritic symptoms rather than a chronic postprandial pattern, and costochondritis is reproducible with palpation rather than meal related.
  30. The correct answer is E (First degree AV block with a fixed prolonged PR interval): A prolonged PR interval above 200 milliseconds with every P wave conducted to a QRS complex defines first degree AV block, which is often benign and asymptomatic. Mobitz type I and II involve intermittently dropped beats, third degree block shows complete dissociation between P waves and QRS complexes, and bundle branch block affects QRS morphology rather than the PR interval.
  31. The correct answer is B (Physiologic sinus bradycardia reflecting high resting vagal tone): A resting heart rate in the 40s in a well trained, asymptomatic athlete reflects increased vagal tone and is a normal physiologic adaptation, not pathology. Sick sinus syndrome and pathologic sinus node dysfunction imply symptomatic or abnormal conduction, complete heart block shows AV dissociation on ECG, and a junctional rhythm would show absent or abnormal P waves rather than normal sinus rhythm.
  32. The correct answer is A (Atrial fibrillation with an irregularly irregular rhythm and absent P waves): An irregularly irregular rhythm without discernible P waves is the hallmark ECG finding of atrial fibrillation. Atrial flutter typically shows a regular sawtooth pattern, multifocal atrial tachycardia shows at least three distinct P wave morphologies with variable rates, sinus arrhythmia is a regular variation tied to respiration, and premature atrial contractions are isolated early beats rather than a sustained irregular rhythm.
  33. The correct answer is E (One hundred to one hundred twenty compressions per minute): Current resuscitation guidelines recommend chest compressions at a rate of 100 to 120 per minute with adequate depth and full recoil between compressions. Rates below 100 or above 120 per minute are associated with worse outcomes and are outside the recommended range.
  34. The correct answer is D (Atrial septal defect causing a fixed, widely split second heart sound): A fixed, widely split S2 that does not change with respiration, along with a pulmonic flow murmur, is the classic finding of an atrial septal defect due to chronic right ventricular volume overload. Ventricular septal defect produces a holosystolic murmur without this fixed splitting, patent ductus arteriosus produces a continuous murmur, bicuspid aortic valve produces an ejection click, and pulmonic stenosis produces a harsher ejection murmur without fixed splitting.
  35. The correct answer is C (Ascending aortic dilation commonly associated with bicuspid valve disease): Bicuspid aortic valve is strongly associated with aortopathy, including dilation and increased risk of dissection of the ascending aorta, so imaging of the aorta is recommended in these patients. Renal artery stenosis, pulmonary arteriovenous malformations, coronary ectasia, and vena cava duplication are not established associations of bicuspid aortic valve.
  36. The correct answer is A (Ventricular septal defect with left to right shunting and overcirculation): A harsh holosystolic murmur at the left lower sternal border with signs of pulmonary overcirculation and failure to thrive is classic for a ventricular septal defect with left to right shunting. Atrial septal defect produces a softer flow murmur with fixed split S2, tetralogy of Fallot and pulmonic stenosis produce ejection type murmurs, and Ebstein anomaly has a distinct tricuspid regurgitant murmur pattern.
  37. The correct answer is E (Aortic stenosis with a delayed carotid upstroke and carotid radiation): A crescendo decrescendo systolic murmur radiating to the carotids with a delayed, diminished carotid upstroke (pulsus parvus et tardus) is classic for aortic stenosis. Mitral stenosis produces a diastolic rumble, mitral and tricuspid regurgitation produce holosystolic murmurs, and aortic regurgitation produces a diastolic decrescendo murmur, none of which match this description.
  38. The correct answer is D (Water hammer pulse describing the bounding pulse of aortic regurgitation): A rapidly rising and quickly collapsing peripheral pulse from a wide pulse pressure is termed a water hammer, or Corrigan, pulse and is classic for chronic severe aortic regurgitation. Pulsus alternans reflects alternating pulse strength in severe systolic dysfunction, pulsus paradoxus is an inspiratory drop seen in tamponade, pulsus bisferiens describes a double peak, and pulsus parvus et tardus describes a weak, delayed pulse in aortic stenosis, the opposite pattern.
  39. The correct answer is C (Mitral regurgitation with a holosystolic murmur radiating toward the axilla): A holosystolic murmur at the apex radiating to the axilla with an enlarged, laterally displaced apical impulse reflects volume overload from chronic mitral regurgitation. Aortic and pulmonic stenosis produce ejection murmurs rather than holosystolic ones, tricuspid stenosis produces a diastolic murmur, and mitral valve prolapse alone typically produces a mid-systolic click with a later murmur rather than this holosystolic pattern.
  40. The correct answer is C (An opening snap from a stenotic, rheumatically scarred mitral valve): A high pitched sound shortly after S2 followed by a diastolic rumble is the opening snap of a stenotic, rheumatic mitral valve. A pericardial knock occurs in constrictive pericarditis, S3 and S4 gallops are lower pitched filling sounds, and an ejection click occurs in early systole rather than diastole.
  41. The correct answer is A (Mechanical valves require lifelong warfarin because of high thrombogenicity): Mechanical prosthetic valves require lifelong warfarin due to their high thrombogenicity, and direct oral anticoagulants are not recommended for this indication. Bioprosthetic valves generally need only aspirin or a limited period of anticoagulation rather than lifelong warfarin. Neither valve type can safely go without any antithrombotic therapy long term.
  42. The correct answer is D (Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction and diastolic dysfunction): Preserved ejection fraction with left ventricular hypertrophy and diastolic dysfunction causing elevated filling pressures defines HFpEF, common in longstanding hypertensive patients. HFrEF requires a reduced ejection fraction, high output failure involves an increased cardiac output state, cor pulmonale specifically involves right heart failure from lung disease, and acute decompensated systolic failure implies reduced rather than preserved ejection fraction.
  43. The correct answer is B (Viral myocarditis following a recent upper respiratory viral illness): A viral prodrome followed by new heart failure symptoms and reduced ejection fraction in a previously healthy young patient is classic for viral myocarditis. Ischemic cardiomyopathy requires coronary disease, amyloid infiltration and alcoholic cardiomyopathy have different typical histories, and peripartum cardiomyopathy is specific to the peripartum period in women.
  44. The correct answer is C (Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy with dynamic outflow obstruction and family history): A murmur that intensifies with standing and Valsalva, combined with a family history of sudden cardiac death in a young athlete, is highly suggestive of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, which is inherited and a leading cause of sudden death in young athletes. Mitral valve prolapse and atrial septal defect do not typically have this maneuver response, aortic stenosis murmurs decrease rather than increase with standing, and dilated cardiomyopathy does not classically produce this dynamic murmur.
  45. The correct answer is E (Restrictive cardiomyopathy with preserved chamber size and impaired diastolic filling): Normal chamber size and preserved ejection fraction with thickened walls, impaired diastolic filling, and biatrial enlargement in the setting of amyloidosis is classic for restrictive cardiomyopathy. Dilated cardiomyopathy shows ventricular enlargement with reduced ejection fraction, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy shows asymmetric septal hypertrophy often with outflow obstruction, Takotsubo is a stress induced transient wall motion abnormality, and ARVC primarily affects the right ventricle with fibrofatty replacement.
  46. The correct answer is C (Dilated cardiomyopathy from chronic toxic alcohol induced myocardial injury): Chronic heavy alcohol use is a well established toxic cause of dilated cardiomyopathy, presenting with ventricular dilation and globally reduced systolic function. Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy involves wall thickening rather than dilation, restrictive cardiomyopathy has normal chamber size with diastolic dysfunction, stress cardiomyopathy is triggered by acute emotional or physical stress, and constrictive physiology relates to the pericardium, not alcohol toxicity.
  47. The correct answer is E (Cor pulmonale, right heart failure caused by chronic lung disease): Right ventricular enlargement and failure caused by pulmonary hypertension secondary to chronic lung disease, without primary left heart disease, defines cor pulmonale. Left heart failure would show left ventricular abnormality as the primary process, constrictive pericarditis involves the pericardium, high output failure involves increased cardiac output states, and hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is a primary myocardial disease unrelated to lung disease.
  48. The correct answer is A (Infective endocarditis, often right sided, related to injection drug use): Fever, a new regurgitant murmur, septic emboli, and injection drug use with Staphylococcus aureus bacteremia together strongly indicate infective endocarditis, often right sided in this population. Rheumatic carditis follows streptococcal pharyngitis rather than bacteremia, viral pericarditis does not cause a new regurgitant murmur or emboli, and Libman-Sacks and marantic endocarditis are non-infective and associated with lupus or malignancy rather than active Staphylococcus infection.
  49. The correct answer is D (Prophylaxis is recommended given his high risk prosthetic valve status): Patients with prosthetic heart valves are among the high risk cardiac conditions for which antibiotic prophylaxis is recommended before dental procedures involving gingival manipulation. This recommendation applies regardless of valve type or diabetes status, and it is not limited to extractions alone since gingival manipulation during cleaning also poses bacteremia risk.
  50. The correct answer is B (Cardiac implantable electronic device infection involving the pocket and leads): Erythema, tenderness over a device pocket, fever, and positive blood cultures describe an infection of a cardiac implantable electronic device, which can involve the pocket, leads, or both. Pericarditis and myocarditis are inflammatory conditions without a device pocket component, rheumatic fever follows streptococcal infection, and prosthetic valve thrombosis is a mechanical rather than infectious complication.
  51. The correct answer is A (Carotid artery stenosis causing turbulent flow and an audible bruit): A cervical bruit in an older smoker on routine exam raises concern for turbulent flow from carotid artery stenosis, an important finding to evaluate further. Subclavian steal produces arm symptoms with a blood pressure discrepancy rather than isolated neck bruit, aortic dissection presents acutely with severe pain, SVC obstruction causes facial and neck swelling rather than a bruit, and Takayasu arteritis is a much less common cause typically in younger patients.
  52. The correct answer is D (Men aged sixty five to seventy five who have ever smoked): Guidelines recommend a one time abdominal ultrasound screening for AAA in men aged 65 to 75 with any smoking history, given their significantly elevated risk. Universal screening in all women or all adults is not recommended, and screening is not restricted only to patients with known coronary disease or family history of stroke.
  53. The correct answer is C (Aortic dissection with sudden tearing pain radiating into the back): Sudden onset, severe tearing or ripping pain radiating to the back that reaches maximum intensity immediately is the classic description of aortic dissection, distinct from the more gradual crescendo pain of ischemia. Pericarditis pain is positional and pleuritic, stable angina is exertional and gradual, pulmonary embolism causes pleuritic pain, and reflux does not present with this tearing quality.
  54. The correct answer is B (Peripheral arterial disease with claudication and a reduced ankle brachial index): Reproducible exertional calf pain relieved by rest, known as claudication, along with a reduced ankle brachial index below 0.9, confirms peripheral arterial disease. DVT presents with swelling and tenderness rather than exertional pain relieved by rest, venous insufficiency worsens with standing rather than walking, spinal stenosis pain is often positional and relieved by leaning forward, and a popliteal cyst causes localized posterior knee symptoms.
  55. The correct answer is A (This value meets the current hemodynamic definition of pulmonary hypertension): A resting mean pulmonary artery pressure of at least 20 mmHg on right heart catheterization meets current hemodynamic criteria for pulmonary hypertension, so 30 mmHg clearly qualifies. This is not simply a normal or exercise only finding, and while pulmonary embolism and left heart disease are among many possible causes, the elevated pressure itself is not diagnostic of one specific etiology.
  56. The correct answer is A (Virchow triad of venous stasis, endothelial injury, and hypercoagulability): Venous thromboembolism risk is classically explained by Virchow triad, which includes venous stasis from immobility, endothelial injury, and a hypercoagulable state, all relevant after surgery and prolonged immobility. Beck triad relates to tamponade, Cushing triad relates to elevated intracranial pressure, and the other listed groupings do not describe VTE risk factors.
  57. The correct answer is D (Superior vena cava syndrome from tumor compression of the SVC): Facial and neck swelling with distended chest wall veins and headache worsened by bending forward, in a patient with lung cancer, is the classic presentation of superior vena cava syndrome from tumor compression or invasion. Constrictive pericarditis, right heart failure, and tamponade present with more generalized systemic venous congestion rather than isolated facial and upper body swelling, and DVT of the leg would not cause facial swelling.
  58. The correct answer is C (Vasovagal reflex syncope triggered by prolonged standing with prodrome): Prodromal nausea and warmth with syncope triggered by prolonged standing, followed by rapid spontaneous recovery, is characteristic of vasovagal reflex syncope. Cardiac arrhythmic syncope typically has little to no warning, orthostatic hypotension relates to positional change rather than prolonged standing alone, seizures have a postictal confusion period, and aortic stenosis syncope occurs specifically with exertion.
  59. The correct answer is B (Orthostatic hypotension defined by a sustained drop in standing blood pressure): A sustained systolic blood pressure drop of at least 20 mmHg within three minutes of standing, without compensatory tachycardia, defines orthostatic hypotension. Vasovagal syncope is triggered by a reflex response rather than simple positional change, postural tachycardia syndrome shows an excessive heart rate rise without significant blood pressure drop, cardiac syncope is typically abrupt and unrelated to position, and situational syncope is tied to specific triggers like coughing or urination.
  60. The correct answer is E (The Revised Cardiac Risk Index for perioperative noncardiac surgery risk): The Revised Cardiac Risk Index is the most widely used validated tool for estimating perioperative cardiac risk before noncardiac surgery. CHA2DS2-VASc and HAS-BLED assess stroke and bleeding risk in atrial fibrillation, the Framingham score estimates long term cardiovascular risk, and the TIMI score is used for acute coronary syndrome risk stratification, none of which are designed for perioperative assessment.
  61. The correct answer is B (Statins, which are first line for LDL lowering and risk reduction): Statins are first line therapy for LDL lowering and cardiovascular risk reduction given their strong outcomes evidence. Fibrates primarily target triglycerides, niacin and bile acid sequestrants are less effective and less well tolerated second line options, and omega-3 fatty acids mainly address triglycerides rather than LDL as primary therapy.
  62. The correct answer is B (Inhibition of hepatic synthesis of vitamin K dependent clotting factors): Warfarin works by inhibiting the hepatic synthesis of vitamin K dependent clotting factors II, VII, IX, and X. Direct thrombin and factor Xa inhibition describe direct oral anticoagulants like dabigatran and apixaban, aspirin irreversibly inhibits cyclooxygenase, and clopidogrel blocks the P2Y12 receptor, none of which describe warfarin's mechanism.
  63. The correct answer is C (An S3 gallop occurring during rapid early diastolic ventricular filling): A low pitched extra sound just after S2 in early diastole during rapid ventricular filling is an S3 gallop, commonly heard in volume overloaded states like decompensated heart failure. An S4 occurs in late diastole before S1, an opening snap is higher pitched and associated with mitral stenosis, a pericardial friction rub has a scratchy quality throughout the cycle, and an ejection click occurs in early systole.
  64. The correct answer is E (Fibromuscular dysplasia producing a beaded appearance of the renal artery): A beaded, string of pearls appearance of the renal artery in a young woman without atherosclerotic risk factors is classic for fibromuscular dysplasia, a common cause of renovascular hypertension in younger patients. Atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis typically affects older patients with vascular risk factors and produces ostial narrowing rather than a beaded pattern, and the other listed conditions do not produce this angiographic appearance.
  65. The correct answer is A (Hypertensive emergency with acute, ongoing target organ damage present): Severely elevated blood pressure accompanied by acute end organ damage, such as encephalopathy with papilledema, defines hypertensive emergency, requiring prompt but controlled blood pressure reduction. Hypertensive urgency involves similarly high pressures without acute end organ damage. Stage 2 hypertension, white coat hypertension, masked hypertension, and resistant hypertension are chronic classifications that do not describe this acute emergent presentation.
  66. The correct answer is C (Patent ductus arteriosus producing a continuous machinery-like murmur): A continuous, machinery-like murmur throughout systole and diastole with bounding pulses in a premature infant is the classic finding of a patent ductus arteriosus. Atrial and ventricular septal defects produce systolic murmurs only, coarctation produces a systolic murmur with pulse discrepancy, and tetralogy of Fallot produces a systolic ejection murmur from pulmonic stenosis rather than a continuous murmur.
  67. The correct answer is A (Pulmonic stenosis with an ejection murmur radiating toward the back): A systolic ejection murmur at the left upper sternal border radiating to the back, with wide splitting of S2 and a soft pulmonic component, reflects obstruction to right ventricular outflow from pulmonic stenosis. Ventricular septal defect produces a holosystolic murmur, patent ductus arteriosus produces a continuous murmur, coarctation produces a murmur with pulse discrepancy, and aortic stenosis produces a murmur radiating to the carotids rather than the back.
  68. The correct answer is C (Mobitz type I block, also called Wenckebach, with progressive PR lengthening.): Progressive PR prolongation culminating in a dropped QRS complex, then resetting, is the hallmark of Mobitz type I, or Wenckebach, block. Mobitz type II shows sudden dropped beats without preceding PR prolongation, third degree block shows complete AV dissociation, first degree block has no dropped beats, and sinus arrest shows an absent P wave without this progressive pattern.
  69. The correct answer is B (Third degree, or complete, heart block with full AV dissociation): Complete independence between P waves and QRS complexes, each firing at its own regular rate, defines third degree or complete heart block. First degree block has a fixed prolonged PR interval with every beat conducted, Mobitz I and II involve intermittently dropped beats with some conducted beats, and sinus bradycardia maintains normal AV conduction with a slow but regular sinus rate.
  70. The correct answer is E (AV nodal reentrant tachycardia terminating abruptly with vagal maneuvers): A sudden onset regular narrow complex tachycardia around 150 to 220 beats per minute that terminates abruptly with vagal maneuvers like carotid massage is classic for AV nodal reentrant tachycardia. Atrial fibrillation is irregularly irregular and does not terminate abruptly with vagal maneuvers, ventricular tachycardia is wide complex, multifocal atrial tachycardia has variable P wave morphology, and sinus tachycardia gradually slows rather than abruptly terminating.
  71. The correct answer is D (Ventricular tachycardia with AV dissociation on the rhythm strip): A wide complex rhythm at a rapid rate with three or more consecutive similar wide beats and AV dissociation is diagnostic of ventricular tachycardia. Supraventricular tachycardia with aberrancy can appear similar but AV dissociation strongly favors a ventricular origin, atrial flutter with rapid conduction and junctional tachycardia are typically narrow complex, and torsades is a distinct polymorphic pattern rather than a monomorphic wide complex rhythm.
  72. The correct answer is E (Ventricular fibrillation, a shockable rhythm requiring immediate defibrillation): Ventricular fibrillation, along with pulseless ventricular tachycardia, is a shockable rhythm requiring immediate defibrillation during cardiac arrest. Pulseless electrical activity and asystole are non-shockable rhythms managed with CPR and epinephrine rather than defibrillation, and sinus bradycardia or idioventricular rhythm with a pulse are not cardiac arrest rhythms at all.
  73. The correct answer is A (Angina, syncope, and heart failure symptoms indicating severe disease): The classic symptom triad of severe symptomatic aortic stenosis is angina, syncope, and heart failure symptoms, and their onset signals a markedly worse prognosis that warrants prompt evaluation for valve intervention. The other listed symptom groupings, such as fever with weight loss or headache with tinnitus, are not part of the recognized aortic stenosis symptom triad.
  74. The correct answer is C (Mitral valve prolapse with a click and late systolic murmur): A mid-systolic click followed by a late systolic murmur that moves earlier with standing and Valsalva, due to reduced ventricular volume, is classic for mitral valve prolapse. Aortic stenosis produces an ejection murmur without a mid-systolic click, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy has a different maneuver response without a click, mitral stenosis produces a diastolic murmur, and tricuspid regurgitation produces a holosystolic murmur at the lower sternal border.
  75. The correct answer is B (Chronic hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction leading to cor pulmonale): Chronic hypoxemia from severe lung disease causes sustained pulmonary vasoconstriction and vascular remodeling, raising pulmonary artery pressure and leading to right ventricular hypertrophy and failure, known as cor pulmonale. Viral injury, rheumatic scarring, microvascular disease, and amyloid deposition are unrelated mechanisms that do not explain this hypoxia driven right heart process.
  76. The correct answer is E (They inhibit HMG-CoA reductase, lowering hepatic cholesterol synthesis): Statins work by inhibiting HMG-CoA reductase, the rate limiting enzyme in hepatic cholesterol synthesis, which upregulates LDL receptors and lowers circulating LDL. Bile acid sequestrants bind bile acids, cholesterol absorption inhibitors like ezetimibe block intestinal absorption, and lipoprotein lipase activation and chylomicron clearance relate to triglyceride metabolism, not the statin mechanism.
  77. The correct answer is B (Obtain basic labs, urinalysis, and assess for target organ damage): Initial evaluation of newly diagnosed hypertension includes basic metabolic panel, urinalysis, lipid panel, and assessment for target organ damage such as left ventricular hypertrophy before starting therapy, along with lifestyle counseling and often first line medication. Immediate triple therapy is not standard initial treatment, renal imaging and adrenal vein sampling are reserved for suspected secondary causes, and catheterization is not indicated for uncomplicated new hypertension.
  78. The correct answer is A (Adjust or restart oral antihypertensives with close outpatient follow-up): For hypertensive urgency without acute end organ damage, blood pressure should be lowered gradually over days with oral medications and close follow-up rather than rapid reduction. ICU admission and IV agents are reserved for hypertensive emergency with end organ damage, rapid correction within an hour risks organ hypoperfusion, and discharge with no changes is inadequate given the markedly elevated reading.
  79. The correct answer is C (High dose NSAIDs combined with colchicine as first line therapy): First line therapy for acute idiopathic or viral pericarditis without high risk features is a combination of high dose NSAIDs and colchicine, which reduces symptoms and recurrence risk. Pericardiocentesis is reserved for tamponade, corticosteroids alone are generally avoided as first line due to higher recurrence rates, antibiotics are not indicated without evidence of bacterial infection, and pericardiectomy is reserved for refractory constrictive disease.
  80. The correct answer is D (Perform urgent pericardiocentesis to relieve the tamponade physiology): Confirmed cardiac tamponade with hemodynamic compromise requires urgent pericardiocentesis to relieve the pressure on the heart. Diuretics can worsen hemodynamics by reducing preload in tamponade, outpatient anti-inflammatory therapy and observation are inappropriate given hemodynamic instability, and beta blockade can blunt the compensatory tachycardia needed to maintain cardiac output.
  81. The correct answer is D (A twelve lead ECG and an initial cardiac troponin level): Rapid evaluation of possible acute coronary syndrome requires an immediate ECG to look for ischemic changes and a troponin level to assess for myocardial injury. CT angiogram and D-dimer target pulmonary embolism, stress echocardiogram and lipid panel are not immediate diagnostic tests, coronary angiography and BNP are not first line, and chest x-ray with CBC do not diagnose ACS.
  82. The correct answer is C (Primary percutaneous coronary intervention within ninety minutes of arrival): For STEMI patients at a PCI capable hospital, primary percutaneous coronary intervention within 90 minutes of arrival is the preferred reperfusion strategy with the best outcomes. Fibrinolytics are used when timely PCI is unavailable, delaying for troponin peak or serial ECGs wastes critical time, and routine bypass grafting is not first line emergency therapy for STEMI.
  83. The correct answer is A (Papillary muscle rupture causing acute severe mitral regurgitation): Sudden hypotension with a new holosystolic murmur and acute pulmonary edema days after an inferior MI, which often affects the posteromedial papillary muscle, suggests papillary muscle rupture with acute mitral regurgitation. Free wall rupture typically presents with tamponade and PEA arrest rather than a new murmur, pericarditis does not cause a new regurgitant murmur or acute pulmonary edema, right ventricular infarction causes hypotension without this murmur, and ventricular septal rupture produces a different murmur location with a step up in oxygen saturation.
  84. The correct answer is E (A standard exercise treadmill ECG stress test as the initial study): For a patient able to exercise with a normal, interpretable resting ECG, a standard exercise treadmill stress test is the preferred initial evaluation for stable angina. Coronary angiography is more invasive and not first line, cardiac MRI is not the standard initial stress test, dobutamine stress echo is used when a patient cannot exercise, and resting echocardiogram alone does not assess for inducible ischemia.
  85. The correct answer is B (A trial of proton pump inhibitor therapy along with reassurance): With reproducible chest wall tenderness, a normal ECG and troponin, and a low pretest probability for cardiac disease, a trial of acid suppression and reassurance addressing a likely musculoskeletal or reflux cause is reasonable before further cardiac workup. Emergent catheterization, coronary CT angiography, admission for serial troponins, and stress testing are excessive for this low risk, reproducible presentation.
  86. The correct answer is E (Rate control with a beta blocker plus stroke risk assessment): For hemodynamically stable new atrial fibrillation, initial management focuses on rate control and assessing stroke risk and anticoagulation need before considering rhythm control, particularly since duration is unknown and cardioversion carries thromboembolic risk. Immediate cardioversion without anticoagulation assessment risks embolization, ablation is not an emergency intervention, observation alone is inadequate given the rapid rate, and amiodarone is not first line for simple rate control.
  87. The correct answer is A (Atropine given first while preparing for possible transcutaneous pacing): Atropine is the first line pharmacologic treatment for symptomatic bradycardia while preparing for transcutaneous pacing if it is ineffective. Amiodarone and adenosine are used for tachyarrhythmias, digoxin can worsen bradycardia, and metoprolol would further slow the heart rate, worsening the situation.
  88. The correct answer is D (Immediate synchronized cardioversion given the patient's hemodynamic instability): An unstable wide complex tachycardia with hypotension and altered mental status requires immediate synchronized cardioversion rather than pharmacologic therapy. Oral amiodarone and vagal maneuvers act too slowly for an unstable patient, observation delays needed treatment, and adenosine is not appropriate as first line therapy for unstable wide complex tachycardia.
  89. The correct answer is C (Epinephrine given next to improve coronary perfusion pressure): For refractory ventricular fibrillation after the first shock and CPR, epinephrine is administered per ACLS protocol to improve coronary perfusion pressure during ongoing resuscitation. Atropine is not indicated for shockable rhythms, calcium gluconate is reserved for specific electrolyte or toxin related arrests, sodium bicarbonate is not given routinely, and digoxin has no role in acute cardiac arrest management.
  90. The correct answer is B (Periodic clinical and echocardiographic follow-up given the small defect size): A small, asymptomatic secundum ASD without right heart enlargement or pulmonary hypertension can often be monitored with periodic follow-up rather than immediate closure, since spontaneous closure or continued small size without hemodynamic consequence is possible. Immediate surgical or percutaneous closure is generally reserved for defects with hemodynamic significance, anticoagulation and lung transplant evaluation are not appropriate for this incidental finding.
  91. The correct answer is A (Obtain three sets of blood cultures from separate venipuncture sites): Before starting empiric antibiotics for suspected infective endocarditis, obtaining multiple sets of blood cultures from separate venipuncture sites is essential to identify the causative organism and guide targeted therapy. Starting vancomycin without cultures can obscure the diagnosis, chest CT is not the key initial step, outpatient oral antibiotics are inappropriate for suspected endocarditis, and delaying cultures until echocardiogram results wastes critical diagnostic opportunity.
  92. The correct answer is B (Transesophageal echocardiogram for better prosthetic valve visualization): Transesophageal echocardiography provides superior visualization of prosthetic valves and is indicated when prosthetic valve endocarditis is suspected, especially when transthoracic imaging is inconclusive. Stopping anticoagulation is not the immediate priority, repeating transthoracic echo in six months delays urgent diagnosis, empiric antibiotics without cultures risks missing the causative organism, and cardiac MRI is not the standard test for suspected prosthetic valve infection.
  93. The correct answer is E (Transthoracic echocardiogram to assess the suspected aortic valve lesion): Exertional syncope with a harsh systolic murmur radiating to the carotids and a diminished carotid upstroke suggests aortic stenosis, and transthoracic echocardiogram is the appropriate next step to confirm severity. Tilt table testing and ambulatory monitoring are used for suspected reflex or arrhythmic syncope without a murmur, carotid duplex evaluates the carotid arteries rather than the valve, and exercise stress testing is generally avoided in suspected severe symptomatic aortic stenosis due to safety concerns.
  94. The correct answer is D (Proceeding to surgery without any additional preoperative cardiac stress testing): A patient with stable coronary disease and good functional capacity above four METs undergoing an elective low risk procedure generally does not need additional preoperative cardiac stress testing. Coronary angiography, stress echocardiography, and cardiac MRI are reserved for patients with poor functional capacity or high risk surgery, and delaying surgery for months of rehabilitation is unnecessary in a stable patient.
  95. The correct answer is E (Transthoracic echocardiogram to characterize this new diastolic murmur): A new diastolic murmur, which is almost always pathologic, warrants evaluation with transthoracic echocardiogram to characterize the valve lesion and its severity. Cardiac catheterization and stress testing are not first line for murmur characterization, chest x-ray alone provides limited valve detail, and Holter monitoring evaluates rhythm rather than valve structure.
  96. The correct answer is B (Contrast enhanced CT angiography of the chest for suspected dissection): Sudden tearing chest pain radiating to the back with hypertension and a pulse deficit strongly suggests aortic dissection, and CT angiography is the fastest and most widely available study to confirm the diagnosis and define its extent. Echocardiogram alone may miss distal dissection extent, V/Q scan targets pulmonary embolism, stress testing is contraindicated in suspected dissection, and plain chest x-ray lacks sufficient sensitivity and specificity.
  97. The correct answer is E (Ankle brachial index measurement performed as the first line test): The ankle brachial index is the appropriate first line, noninvasive test to evaluate for peripheral arterial disease in a patient with claudication and diminished pulses. Venous duplex evaluates for venous disease rather than arterial disease, CT angiography and digital subtraction angiography are more invasive and reserved for further characterization or intervention planning, and MRI of the spine would be used if a neurogenic cause were suspected instead.
  98. The correct answer is D (Compression ultrasound of the affected leg as the initial study): Compression ultrasound is the standard initial imaging test to confirm suspected deep vein thrombosis. D-dimer alone is a screening test best combined with pretest probability rather than used in isolation for diagnosis, CT venography and MRI are reserved for equivocal or complex cases, and contrast venography, though historically the gold standard, is invasive and rarely used as a first line test today.
  99. The correct answer is C (Contrast enhanced CT of the chest to define the obstruction): Contrast enhanced chest CT is the preferred imaging study to confirm superior vena cava syndrome and identify the level and cause of obstruction. Plain chest x-ray lacks sufficient detail, transthoracic echocardiogram does not adequately visualize the SVC, neck ultrasound evaluates the carotid or jugular vessels rather than the mediastinum, and exercise stress testing has no role in this evaluation.
  100. The correct answer is A (Calculate his ten year ASCVD risk score to guide the statin decision): For primary prevention in a patient without known cardiovascular disease, calculating the 10-year ASCVD risk score helps guide the shared decision about starting statin therapy alongside the LDL level. Starting a fibrate does not address LDL reduction for cardiovascular risk, waiting five years to recheck delays appropriate risk reduction, and statin therapy is not reserved only for extremely high LDL levels or symptomatic disease.
  101. The correct answer is C (The CHA2DS2-VASc score used to estimate stroke risk in atrial fibrillation): The CHA2DS2-VASc score is the standard validated tool used to estimate stroke risk in atrial fibrillation and guide anticoagulation decisions. The Revised Cardiac Risk Index assesses perioperative risk, the TIMI score assesses acute coronary syndrome risk, the Wells score assesses pulmonary embolism or DVT probability, and the Framingham score estimates general cardiovascular risk, none of which are designed for AFib stroke risk.
  102. The correct answer is A (Bridge with therapeutic heparin while warfarin is temporarily held): Patients with mechanical mitral valves are at high thromboembolic risk, so perioperative management typically involves holding warfarin briefly and bridging with therapeutic heparin to minimize time without anticoagulation. Stopping warfarin without bridging or continuing it uninterrupted through a major surgery both carry unacceptable risks, switching permanently to a direct oral anticoagulant is not recommended for mechanical valves, and stopping only aspirin does not address the warfarin interruption issue.
  103. The correct answer is B (Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction and diastolic dysfunction): Exertional dyspnea with a normal ejection fraction, elevated natriuretic peptides, left atrial enlargement, and diastolic dysfunction supports a diagnosis of HFpEF. HFrEF requires reduced ejection fraction, constrictive pericarditis requires additional pericardial and hemodynamic findings before being confirmed, pulmonary embolism presents acutely rather than with this chronic pattern, and restrictive lung disease would show different pulmonary function findings rather than left atrial enlargement.
  104. The correct answer is D (Cardiac MRI to characterize myocardial edema and inflammation): Cardiac MRI is the most useful imaging modality to confirm myocarditis, as it can detect myocardial edema, hyperemia, and late gadolinium enhancement indicating inflammation and injury, especially when coronary angiography excludes obstructive disease. Stress echocardiogram, chest x-ray, V/Q scan, and carotid ultrasound do not evaluate myocardial tissue characteristics relevant to myocarditis.
  105. The correct answer is E (Clinical evaluation and screening echocardiogram for his first degree relatives): Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is typically an autosomal dominant inherited condition, so first degree relatives should undergo clinical screening including echocardiogram, and genetic testing can also be considered. Screening should not be limited to older relatives or only those with symptoms, since the condition can be present without symptoms, and genetic testing is not contraindicated in family members.
  106. The correct answer is C (Referral for aortic valve replacement, either surgical or transcatheter): Severe symptomatic aortic stenosis has a poor prognosis without intervention, so referral for surgical or transcatheter aortic valve replacement is the standard of care. Medical therapy alone does not alter the natural history of severe symptomatic disease, balloon valvuloplasty is a temporizing measure rather than definitive therapy in most adults, high dose vasodilators can be dangerous in severe AS, and delaying reassessment for two years is inappropriate given his current symptoms.
  107. The correct answer is E (Percutaneous balloon mitral valvuloplasty given the favorable valve anatomy): In a patient with symptomatic mitral stenosis and favorable, non-calcified valve morphology without significant regurgitation or thrombus, percutaneous balloon valvuloplasty is the preferred, less invasive intervention. Surgical replacement is reserved for unfavorable anatomy or when valvuloplasty is contraindicated, medical therapy alone does not relieve the mechanical obstruction, and the other listed procedures target different valves entirely.
  108. The correct answer is B (Give amoxicillin prophylaxis before the scheduled dental procedure): Patients with prosthetic heart valves are considered high risk and should receive antibiotic prophylaxis, typically amoxicillin, before dental procedures involving manipulation of gingival tissue, including extractions. Giving prophylaxis after the procedure or omitting it entirely does not provide the intended protection, and substituting an extra warfarin dose has no role in infection prevention.
  109. The correct answer is D (Complete removal of the infected device and all leads): Cardiac implantable electronic device infection with bacteremia generally requires complete removal of the device and leads in addition to antibiotic therapy, since infection cannot be reliably cleared with antibiotics alone while hardware remains in place. Increasing the pacing rate, local wound care alone, adding anticoagulation, and simple observation all fail to address the underlying infected hardware.
  110. The correct answer is A (Carotid duplex ultrasound as the initial noninvasive evaluation): Carotid duplex ultrasound is the appropriate first line, noninvasive test to evaluate a carotid bruit and estimate the degree of stenosis. Cerebral angiography is more invasive and reserved for further characterization, MRI of the brain evaluates for prior stroke rather than vessel stenosis directly, transcranial Doppler alone is less standard for initial extracranial carotid evaluation, and CT of the head without contrast does not assess vascular stenosis.
  111. The correct answer is C (Periodic surveillance imaging performed every six to twelve months): A 4.2 cm AAA falls below the typical repair threshold of 5.0 to 5.5 cm, so periodic surveillance imaging is the appropriate management to monitor for growth or symptoms. Immediate open or endovascular repair is reserved for larger or symptomatic aneurysms, no follow-up at all would miss important growth, and thrombolytic therapy has no role in aneurysm management.
  112. The correct answer is A (Renal artery duplex ultrasound or magnetic resonance angiography first): Renal artery duplex ultrasound or magnetic resonance angiography are appropriate noninvasive first line studies to evaluate for renal artery stenosis in suspected renovascular hypertension. Renal biopsy and intravenous pyelogram do not directly assess renal artery anatomy, adrenal vein sampling evaluates for primary aldosteronism rather than renal artery disease, and voiding cystourethrogram evaluates the urinary tract rather than renal vasculature.
  113. The correct answer is D (Pulsus paradoxus, suggesting an underlying cardiac tamponade physiology): An exaggerated drop in systolic blood pressure greater than 10 mmHg with inspiration is pulsus paradoxus, a key finding supporting cardiac tamponade in a patient with a known effusion and hemodynamic compromise. Kussmaul sign describes a jugular venous finding in constriction, Corrigan pulse and water hammer pulse describe aortic regurgitation, and pulsus alternans describes alternating pulse amplitude in severe systolic dysfunction.
  114. The correct answer is E (Continue the beta blocker therapy through the perioperative period): Patients on chronic beta blocker therapy for cardiac indications should generally continue the medication through the perioperative period, since abrupt withdrawal can precipitate rebound tachycardia and ischemia. Stopping it a week before surgery or the night before, doubling the dose, or switching agents are not recommended standard approaches.
  115. The correct answer is B (Triple therapy duration is minimized before transitioning to dual therapy): Current guidance favors minimizing the duration of triple antithrombotic therapy after PCI in atrial fibrillation patients, transitioning to dual therapy with an anticoagulant and a single antiplatelet agent to reduce bleeding risk while still protecting against stent thrombosis and stroke. Lifelong triple therapy carries excessive bleeding risk, and stopping anticoagulation entirely or antiplatelet therapy immediately after stenting both increase risk of stroke or stent thrombosis respectively.
  116. The correct answer is B (Treat this presentation as a STEMI equivalent needing urgent reperfusion): New left bundle branch block with ongoing ischemic symptoms should prompt urgent reperfusion evaluation, since a new LBBB can obscure typical ST elevation criteria. Current guidelines no longer treat new LBBB alone as an automatic STEMI equivalent, since most such patients do not have an acute occlusion, and Sgarbossa criteria applied to the LBBB pattern help identify which patients truly need emergent catheterization. Treating this only as NSTEMI, discharging for outpatient testing, or observing with delayed ECGs or serial troponins alone risks missing a time sensitive reperfusion opportunity in a patient with active ischemic symptoms.
  117. The correct answer is D (Systemic thrombolytic therapy given the hemodynamic instability present): A hemodynamically unstable, or massive, pulmonary embolism warrants systemic thrombolytic therapy to rapidly reduce clot burden and right heart strain when there are no major contraindications. Oral anticoagulation alone acts too slowly for hemodynamic instability, outpatient management and observation are inappropriate for an unstable patient, and aspirin alone provides insufficient anticoagulation for a massive PE.
  118. The correct answer is A (Cardiac MRI or CT combined with hemodynamic catheterization data): Distinguishing constrictive pericarditis from restrictive cardiomyopathy relies on advanced imaging such as cardiac MRI or CT to assess pericardial thickness, combined with hemodynamic catheterization showing characteristic discordant ventricular pressure changes. Chest x-ray, complete blood count, exercise testing, coronary angiography, and ankle brachial index do not provide the specific structural and hemodynamic detail needed for this distinction.
  119. The correct answer is E (Constrictive pericarditis given the pericardial thickening and discordant pressures): Pericardial thickening and calcification on imaging combined with discordant respiratory variation in right and left ventricular pressures on catheterization are the classic distinguishing features of constrictive pericarditis rather than restrictive cardiomyopathy, which typically lacks pericardial thickening and shows concordant pressure changes. Hypertrophic and dilated cardiomyopathy and acute myocarditis do not produce this specific hemodynamic and pericardial imaging pattern.
  120. The correct answer is C (Free wall rupture presenting with sudden tamponade and cardiac arrest): Sudden pulseless electrical activity with distended neck veins and a new pericardial effusion causing tamponade physiology days after a large MI is the classic presentation of free wall rupture, an often catastrophic mechanical complication. Papillary muscle rupture and ventricular septal rupture cause acute regurgitant murmurs rather than sudden tamponade and PEA, Dressler syndrome is a delayed autoimmune pericarditis occurring weeks later, and right ventricular infarction presents with hypotension and clear lungs rather than tamponade.
  121. The correct answer is E (Situational reflex syncope triggered specifically by urination at night): Syncope specifically triggered by micturition, with rapid recovery, a normal cardiac workup, and a prior similar episode, is classic for situational reflex syncope, in this case micturition syncope. Cardiac arrhythmic syncope and aortic stenosis syncope would not be specifically tied to urination, seizures involve postictal confusion, and orthostatic hypotension relates to positional change rather than a specific situational trigger.
  122. The correct answer is C (Hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy with a preload dependent dynamic murmur): The murmur of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy increases with reduced preload maneuvers like standing and Valsalva and decreases with increased preload like squatting, the opposite pattern of aortic stenosis, whose murmur decreases with standing. Mitral regurgitation and tricuspid regurgitation murmurs do not show this dynamic amplification pattern, and mitral stenosis is a diastolic rather than systolic murmur.
  123. The correct answer is D (Emergent surgical repair given this ascending aorta involvement): Dissection involving the ascending aorta, a Stanford type A dissection, is a surgical emergency due to high risk of rupture, tamponade, and coronary or valve involvement, and requires prompt surgical repair. Medical management alone, outpatient follow-up, observation, or endovascular stenting as first line are inadequate and inappropriate for ascending aortic dissection, which requires open surgical intervention.
  124. The correct answer is A (Prominent pulsus paradoxus favoring a diagnosis of tamponade): Pronounced pulsus paradoxus is a more characteristic finding of cardiac tamponade, while Kussmaul sign and a pericardial knock are more typical of constrictive pericarditis, helping distinguish the two conditions clinically. The two conditions do not present identically, and ascites can occur in either condition rather than being unique to tamponade.
  125. The correct answer is B (The acute setting lacking time for atrial and ventricular compensation): In acute mitral regurgitation, such as from papillary muscle rupture, the left atrium and ventricle have not had time to dilate and compensate, so a sudden regurgitant volume causes a steep rise in left atrial pressure and pulmonary edema, often with a softer murmur due to rapidly equalizing pressures. Chronic regurgitation allows gradual chamber remodeling that better tolerates the volume load over years, the opposite of the acute scenario described.
  126. The correct answer is C (High output heart failure driven by the underlying severe anemia): Conditions like severe anemia create a hyperdynamic circulatory state where cardiac output is normal or increased, yet the heart cannot meet the body's excessive metabolic or oxygen delivery demands, leading to high output heart failure. Low output failure involves reduced cardiac output, and restrictive cardiomyopathy, constrictive pericarditis, and isolated right heart failure describe different structural or hemodynamic processes unrelated to this high output anemia driven mechanism.
  127. The correct answer is D (AV nodal blocking agents such as adenosine or verapamil): In atrial fibrillation with WPW syndrome, AV nodal blocking agents can preferentially block the AV node while allowing unopposed rapid conduction down the accessory pathway, potentially degenerating into ventricular fibrillation. Procainamide and ibutilide are generally considered safer options that slow conduction through the accessory pathway, synchronized cardioversion is appropriate for unstable patients, and amiodarone may be used cautiously in select cases, unlike pure AV nodal blockers which should be avoided.
  128. The correct answer is E (Atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis typical of an older smoker): In an older patient with diffuse atherosclerosis and significant smoking history, atherosclerotic renal artery stenosis, typically involving the ostial renal artery, is the more likely cause of renovascular hypertension, contrasted with fibromuscular dysplasia which more commonly affects younger women. Essential hypertension alone would not explain a renal artery specific finding, coarctation presents earlier in life with pulse discrepancy, and pheochromocytoma presents with episodic symptoms rather than gradual progressive hypertension.
  129. The correct answer is A (Cardiogenic shock from severe pump failure after a large infarction): Hypotension, tachycardia, cool extremities, and elevated lactate following a large MI with severely reduced ejection fraction and no infectious source indicate cardiogenic shock from pump failure. Septic shock requires an infectious source, hypovolemic shock from bleeding would show a different clinical context, anaphylactic shock follows an allergic trigger, and neurogenic shock follows spinal cord injury, none of which fit this post-MI presentation.
  130. The correct answer is E (HACEK organisms and Coxiella burnetii requiring specialized testing): Culture negative endocarditis can result from fastidious organisms like the HACEK group or from Coxiella burnetii, the agent of Q fever, which require special culture techniques or serologic testing to identify. Streptococcus viridans, Staphylococcus aureus, Enterococcus faecalis, and Escherichia coli are typically identified with standard blood culture techniques and are not the classic causes of culture negative endocarditis.
  131. The correct answer is D (Warfarin remains recommended, since direct oral anticoagulants are unsafe here): Clinical trials have shown increased thromboembolic and bleeding events with direct oral anticoagulants in patients with mechanical heart valves, so warfarin remains the recommended agent despite its monitoring requirements. Direct oral anticoagulants are not preferred or equally acceptable in this setting, aspirin alone is insufficient given the high thrombotic risk of a mechanical valve, and direct oral anticoagulants are specifically contraindicated here rather than always preferred.
  132. The correct answer is B (Group 2, pulmonary hypertension caused by left heart disease): Pulmonary hypertension resulting from chronically elevated left heart filling pressures, such as from longstanding heart failure, is classified as WHO Group 2. Group 1 refers to primary pulmonary arterial hypertension, Group 3 relates to chronic lung disease, Group 4 relates to chronic thromboembolic disease, and Group 5 covers unclear or multifactorial causes, none of which describe pulmonary hypertension secondary to left heart disease.
  133. The correct answer is C (His functional capacity above four METs makes further stress testing unnecessary): A patient who can climb two flights of stairs without symptoms demonstrates functional capacity above four METs, which generally allows proceeding to intermediate risk surgery without additional stress testing, even with a remote history of MI. Mandatory catheterization, stress echocardiography regardless of functional status, or denying surgery are not supported when functional capacity is adequate and the patient is asymptomatic.
  134. The correct answer is E (Aortic regurgitation with a wide pulse pressure and bounding pulse): A wide pulse pressure, diastolic decrescendo murmur, and bounding peripheral pulse together reflect the hemodynamics of chronic aortic regurgitation, where blood regurgitates back into the left ventricle during diastole. Mitral and tricuspid stenosis produce diastolic murmurs without this wide pulse pressure pattern, pulmonic stenosis produces a systolic murmur, and aortic stenosis produces a narrow pulse pressure with a delayed pulse rather than a bounding one.
  135. The correct answer is A (Papillary muscle rupture with acute severe mitral regurgitation): A new holosystolic murmur, hypotension, and pulmonary edema after MI, with a flail mitral leaflet on echo and no oxygen saturation step-up between right atrium and ventricle, which would be expected with a ventricular septal defect, confirms papillary muscle rupture causing acute mitral regurgitation rather than a septal rupture. Free wall rupture presents with tamponade and PEA, right ventricular infarction causes hypotension without a new murmur, and Dressler syndrome is a delayed pericarditis rather than an acute mechanical complication.
  136. The correct answer is C (Intravenous magnesium sulfate given regardless of measured serum level): Torsades de pointes, a polymorphic VT associated with QT prolongation, is specifically treated with intravenous magnesium sulfate regardless of serum magnesium level, in addition to correcting other contributing factors. Standard amiodarone can actually prolong the QT interval further and worsen torsades, beta blockers alone do not address the acute rhythm, elective cardioversion without addressing the underlying QT issue risks recurrence, and digoxin is not indicated and can be proarrhythmic.
  137. The correct answer is A (Discontinue the offending drug and correct electrolyte abnormalities): Drug-induced QT prolongation with resulting polymorphic ventricular tachycardia is managed by stopping the causative agent and correcting any contributing electrolyte abnormalities such as low potassium or magnesium. Continuing the antibiotic or adding another QT-prolonging drug would worsen the risk, class IC antiarrhythmics can further prolong repolarization and are inappropriate, increasing the dose is dangerous, and this arrhythmia is not benign and requires action.
  138. The correct answer is E (Transition to dual or single therapy, prioritizing continued anticoagulation): Given a significant bleed and sufficient time since stenting, transitioning from triple therapy to dual or single antithrombotic therapy, generally continuing anticoagulation with a single antiplatelet or eventually anticoagulation alone, balances ongoing stroke and stent thrombosis prevention against further bleeding risk. Continuing unchanged triple therapy or adding a third antiplatelet agent increases bleeding risk further, while stopping all therapy or using aspirin alone without anticoagulation leaves him inadequately protected against stroke given his CHA2DS2-VASc score.
  139. The correct answer is B (A medial malleolar ulcer with hyperpigmentation and worsening edema): Venous ulcers classically occur near the medial malleolus with surrounding hyperpigmentation from hemosiderin deposition and associated edema that worsens with dependency through the day. Arterial ulcers, in contrast, occur on the toes or pressure points, are painful, have a pale wound base, and are associated with diminished pulses and claudication, features described in the other options rather than the venous ulcer pattern.
  140. The correct answer is D (Catheter-associated thrombosis of the superior vena cava itself): In a patient with a central venous catheter, SVC syndrome is more likely caused by catheter-associated thrombosis narrowing or occluding the vessel, distinct from malignant extrinsic compression seen in cancer patients. Constrictive pericarditis and right heart failure cause more generalized venous congestion rather than isolated SVC obstruction, and aortic dissection extending into the SVC is an unusual and unlikely mechanism in this catheter-related context.
  141. The correct answer is E (Massive septal hypertrophy or a family history of sudden cardiac death): Established major risk factors for sudden cardiac death in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy include markedly increased septal wall thickness and a family history of sudden cardiac death, which support ICD placement for primary prevention. Mild septal thickness without symptoms, a normal exercise blood pressure response, absence of family history, and a murmur that decreases with squatting are all reassuring findings rather than risk factors supporting ICD placement.
  142. The correct answer is D (Troponin can reflect myocardial strain or injury without obstructive coronary disease): Troponin can be elevated in numerous non-ACS conditions including pulmonary embolism with right heart strain, sepsis with demand ischemia, and myocarditis with direct myocardial injury, reflecting myocardial injury broadly rather than only plaque rupture. It is not specific to coronary plaque rupture alone, does not always mandate emergent catheterization without clinical correlation, remains clinically useful in all three settings, and can clearly be elevated by processes other than ACS.
  143. The correct answer is A (Dobutamine stress echocardiogram to assess flow-dependent valve response): In low-flow, low-gradient aortic stenosis with reduced ejection fraction, dobutamine stress echocardiogram helps distinguish true severe stenosis, where the gradient and valve area change appropriately with augmented flow, from pseudo-severe stenosis, where the valve opens more with increased flow. Chest x-ray, exercise treadmill testing, Holter monitoring, and carotid ultrasound do not provide the flow-dependent hemodynamic assessment needed to resolve this specific diagnostic uncertainty.
  144. The correct answer is C (Tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy reversed by achieving rate or rhythm control): Chronically uncontrolled rapid ventricular rates can themselves cause a dilated, reduced ejection fraction cardiomyopathy that improves once the heart rate is controlled, a phenomenon known as tachycardia-mediated cardiomyopathy. Coincidental viral myocarditis resolution, spontaneous ischemic cardiomyopathy improvement, valve replacement, and amyloid regression do not explain the specific temporal relationship between rate control and improved function described here.
  145. The correct answer is B (B-type natriuretic peptide, released in response to ventricular wall stretch): B-type natriuretic peptide is released in response to ventricular wall stretch and is significantly elevated in heart failure, helping distinguish a cardiac cause of dyspnea from a primary pulmonary process where it is typically normal or only mildly elevated.
