C-reactive protein and procalcitonin suggest inflammation or infection, D-dimer is more specific for thromboembolic disease, and lactate reflects tissue hypoperfusion broadly rather than specifically indicating a cardiac cause.
  146. The correct answer is B (Prosthetic valve thrombosis given the mobile mass and reduced motion): Reduced leaflet motion with an associated mobile mass on TEE and fluoroscopy is characteristic of prosthetic valve thrombosis, in contrast to pannus formation, which typically shows reduced leaflet motion from fixed fibrous tissue without a distinct mobile mass. Normal valve function would not show reduced motion or an elevated gradient, an isolated paravalvular leak would not explain reduced leaflet excursion, and hemolytic anemia is a separate complication not explained by this imaging finding.
  147. The correct answer is E (Mobitz type II carries higher risk of progressing to complete heart block): Mobitz type II block, caused by disease below the AV node, carries a significantly higher risk of sudden progression to complete heart block and generally warrants pacemaker placement, whereas Mobitz type I is usually benign, especially when asymptomatic and found incidentally. The other statements incorrectly reverse or equate the risk profiles of these two distinct conduction abnormalities.
  148. The correct answer is B (Add ezetimibe or a PCSK9 inhibitor to further lower his LDL): For a patient with true statin intolerance on maximally tolerated statin therapy and persistently elevated LDL, adding a non-statin agent such as ezetimibe or a PCSK9 inhibitor is the appropriate next step to further reduce cardiovascular risk. Discontinuing all therapy leaves significant risk unaddressed, pushing the statin dose despite intolerable myalgias is not appropriate, fibrates are not primarily indicated for LDL lowering, and diet alone is unlikely to achieve adequate control at this LDL level.
  149. The correct answer is C (Bloody, exudative fluid containing malignant cells on cytology): A malignant pericardial effusion classically produces bloody, exudative fluid that shows malignant cells on cytologic analysis, distinguishing it from the typically clear transudative fluid of uremic effusions or effusions that may show viral markers after a viral illness. Elevated BUN and creatinine in the fluid would suggest a uremic cause, and spontaneous resolution with dialysis or a positive viral PCR point toward uremic or viral causes rather than malignancy.
  150. The correct answer is A (Distended neck veins with echocardiographic effusion and chamber collapse): Jugular venous distension combined with a pericardial effusion showing chamber collapse on point of care echocardiography specifically points to cardiac tamponade as a reversible cause of PEA. Absent breath sounds with tracheal deviation suggests tension pneumothorax, track marks and pinpoint pupils suggest opioid toxicity, hyperkalemia is identified by labs and characteristic ECG changes, and hypothermia is identified by core temperature, none of which specifically indicate tamponade.
  151. The correct answer is E (Severe aortic stenosis given the reduced area and elevated gradient): An aortic valve area below 1.0 cm squared, a mean gradient above 40 mmHg, and a peak velocity above 4 m/s with normal flow all meet standard echocardiographic criteria for severe aortic stenosis. Mild, moderate, and trace stenosis would show a larger valve area and lower gradients and velocities, and these findings clearly exceed normal valve parameters.
  152. The correct answer is B (Echocardiographic evaluation for tricuspid disease or right ventricular cardiomyopathy): Isolated right heart failure without pulmonary hypertension, lung disease, or left ventricular dysfunction should prompt evaluation for a primary right-sided cause such as tricuspid valve disease or a right ventricular cardiomyopathy like ARVC, rather than assuming cor pulmonale, which requires pulmonary hypertension from lung disease. Empiric diuretics without imaging, coronary angiography alone, and renal ultrasound alone would fail to identify the underlying structural cardiac cause.
  153. The correct answer is C (DAPT duration is generally shorter after elective stable disease PCI than ACS): Current practice generally uses shorter dual antiplatelet therapy durations after elective PCI for stable disease compared with longer durations after an ACS-related stent, reflecting the higher ischemic risk in ACS, though exact duration is individualized based on bleeding and thrombotic risk. A fixed identical duration for both scenarios, no antiplatelet therapy at all, aspirin alone immediately, or no therapy in ACS patients are all inconsistent with standard post-PCI antiplatelet management.
  154. The correct answer is D (They reduced preload and afterload, worsening dynamic outflow obstruction): In hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy, medications that reduce preload, such as diuretics and nitrates, or reduce afterload worsen the dynamic left ventricular outflow tract obstruction by allowing the hypertrophied septum to come closer to the mitral valve during systole, which can cause hypotension. These agents do not increase preload, have no effect on the gradient, or improve diastolic filling in this condition, and they do not acutely change wall thickness.
  155. The correct answer is A (Sick sinus syndrome, also known as tachy-brady syndrome): Alternating episodes of sinus node dysfunction causing pauses and bradycardia along with periods of inappropriate tachycardia describe sick sinus syndrome, also known as tachy-brady syndrome. Isolated first degree AV block and complete heart block do not produce alternating tachycardia, vasovagal syncope is a reflex mediated event rather than an intrinsic conduction disorder, and atrial flutter with fixed conduction would produce a consistent rather than alternating rate pattern.
  156. The correct answer is E (Balancing embolic and hemodynamic risk against the risk of worsening hemorrhage): Decisions about the timing of valve surgery after an embolic stroke in endocarditis require weighing the ongoing risk of further embolization or hemodynamic decompensation against the risk of worsening hemorrhage if surgery, which requires anticoagulation, is performed too soon after a hemorrhagic transformation. A rigid six hour window, ignoring neurologic status, a mandatory one year delay in all cases, and assuming antibiotics alone are always curative all oversimplify this nuanced, individualized decision.
  157. The correct answer is C (Refer for aortic valve surgery based on these objective LV parameters): Guidelines recommend surgery for chronic severe aortic regurgitation even in asymptomatic patients once objective evidence of left ventricular decompensation appears, such as declining ejection fraction to 50 percent or less or significant left ventricular enlargement, since waiting for symptoms risks irreversible dysfunction. Continued observation, diuretics alone, afterload reduction alone, or repeating imaging in five years all delay necessary intervention once these threshold criteria are met.
  158. The correct answer is D (Arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy with fibrofatty replacement): Fibrofatty replacement of the right ventricular myocardium on MRI, along with ventricular arrhythmias of left bundle branch morphology and a family history of sudden death in a young athlete, is the classic presentation of arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy. Hypertrophic and restrictive cardiomyopathy show different structural patterns without this fibrofatty right ventricular replacement, ischemic cardiomyopathy relates to coronary disease, and Takotsubo is an acute stress-related, typically reversible condition.
  159. The correct answer is C (Vitamin K and prothrombin complex reverse warfarin, and specific agents reverse certain DOACs): Warfarin associated bleeding is reversed with vitamin K and prothrombin complex concentrate, while direct oral anticoagulants have their own specific reversal agents, such as idarucizumab for dabigatran or andexanet alfa for certain factor Xa inhibitors, representing an important practical difference in emergency management. Both classes do have available reversal strategies, they are not reversed identically with plasma alone, and it is incorrect to say either class lacks any reversal option.
  160. The correct answer is A (Adult coarctation of the aorta presenting later in life): Resistant hypertension with leg claudication-like symptoms, diminished femoral pulses, and lower blood pressure in the legs than the arms in an adult should raise suspicion for previously undiagnosed adult coarctation of the aorta, which can present later in life. Essential hypertension, primary aldosteronism, pheochromocytoma, and renal parenchymal disease alone would not typically produce this specific pattern of differential upper and lower extremity blood pressure with diminished femoral pulses.
  161. The correct answer is A (Early onset flexural pruritic dermatitis occurring with a personal or family history of atopy): Atopic dermatitis classically presents in early childhood with intensely pruritic flexural lesions and is strongly associated with a personal or family history of asthma, allergic rhinitis, or eczema. Contact dermatitis relates to a specific trigger exposure rather than a flexural distribution present since infancy. Herpes zoster causes dermatomal vesicles and psoriasis favors extensor surfaces with silvery scale, neither matching this presentation.
  162. The correct answer is B (A type IV delayed hypersensitivity reaction triggered by nickel within the jewelry): Allergic contact dermatitis to nickel is a classic T-cell mediated type IV hypersensitivity reaction that produces sharply demarcated eczematous patches limited to contact sites. IgE-mediated reactions like urticaria develop within minutes rather than over days. Dermatophyte infection and autoimmune blistering disease do not explain a rash confined precisely to jewelry contact areas.
  163. The correct answer is C (Stasis dermatitis resulting from long-standing chronic venous hypertension in the legs): Chronic venous insufficiency leads to stasis dermatitis with bilateral pretibial hyperpigmentation, scaling, and edema from capillary leakage and hemosiderin deposition. Cellulitis is typically unilateral, acute, and tender, which does not fit this chronic bilateral picture. Necrobiosis lipoidica causes yellow-brown atrophic plaques, a distinctly different appearance from the scaling described here.
  164. The correct answer is D (A morbilliform drug eruption reflecting delayed hypersensitivity to the amoxicillin): A symmetric blanching maculopapular rash appearing about a week after starting a beta-lactam, without mucosal involvement or systemic toxicity, is the classic morbilliform drug eruption. Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis require mucosal involvement and skin sloughing, which are absent here. Urticarial vasculitis produces fixed purpuric lesions rather than a diffuse blanching maculopapular rash.
  165. The correct answer is E (Individual lesions lasting beyond 24 hours and leaving residual purpura behind): Urticarial vasculitis is distinguished from ordinary urticaria by lesions that persist beyond 24 hours, leave residual bruising or pigmentation, and often burn rather than itch. Ordinary urticaria resolves quickly, blanches fully, and responds well to antihistamines, none of which apply to vasculitic lesions. Cold-triggered lesions describe cold urticaria, a separate entity.
  166. The correct answer is B (Reactivation of herpes simplex virus in the days preceding the eruption): Erythema multiforme is most commonly triggered by herpes simplex virus reactivation, with target lesions typically appearing on the palms and soles about one to two weeks after an outbreak. Sulfonamide antibiotics and Mycoplasma are more classically linked to Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis rather than typical erythema multiforme. Lupus and vaccines are not typical triggers for this presentation.
  167. The correct answer is C (Sarcoidosis presenting acutely as erythema nodosum with hilar adenopathy): The combination of tender pretibial nodules, fever, and bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy is the classic presentation of Lofgren syndrome, an acute form of sarcoidosis. Erythema nodosum can also occur with infections or inflammatory bowel disease, but hilar adenopathy specifically points toward sarcoidosis. Gonococcal infection and Lyme disease produce different characteristic skin findings unrelated to hilar lymphadenopathy.
  168. The correct answer is A (Follicular hyperkeratinization causing sebum retention with Cutibacterium acnes overgrowth): Acne vulgaris arises from follicular plugging by keratinocytes and excess sebum, creating an environment that promotes Cutibacterium acnes proliferation and subsequent inflammation. It is not an autoimmune or infectious dermatophyte process, ruling out those mechanisms. Rosacea involves vascular reactivity, a different pathway from the follicular process driving acne.
  169. The correct answer is D (Rosacea with facial erythema exacerbated by common vasodilating dietary triggers): Rosacea presents with persistent central facial erythema, telangiectasias, and flushing that is characteristically worsened by alcohol, spicy foods, heat, and sun exposure. Seborrheic dermatitis typically causes greasy scale in the nasolabial folds rather than flushing triggers. Lupus malar rash spares the nasolabial folds and is not typically provoked by food or alcohol in this manner.
  170. The correct answer is E (Koebner phenomenon, referring to new lesions developing at trauma sites): The Koebner phenomenon refers to new psoriatic or lichenoid lesions developing at sites of skin trauma, such as a scratch or scar. Auspitz sign describes pinpoint bleeding after scale removal, and Nikolsky sign refers to epidermal sliding with lateral pressure, both distinct findings. Wickham striae are the lacy white lines seen in lichen planus, unrelated to trauma-induced lesions.
  171. The correct answer is B (Pityriasis rosea beginning with a solitary herald patch on the trunk): Pityriasis rosea classically starts with a single herald patch followed one to two weeks later by smaller oval lesions distributed along skin cleavage lines in a Christmas tree pattern on the trunk. Guttate psoriasis presents with numerous small drop-like plaques simultaneously rather than a preceding herald patch. Tinea corporis would show a single expanding annular lesion rather than this sequential eruption pattern.
  172. The correct answer is A (Wickham striae, the lacy white lines characteristically seen overlying lichen planus): Wickham striae are the characteristic fine white lacy lines seen on the surface of the violaceous, polygonal, flat-topped papules of lichen planus. Auspitz sign and Nikolsky sign are unrelated findings seen in psoriasis and blistering disorders respectively. Koebner phenomenon describes lesion formation at trauma sites, not a surface pattern on individual papules.
  173. The correct answer is C (A potassium hydroxide preparation of skin scrapings examined for branching hyphae): A KOH preparation is the quick bedside test used to visualize branching hyphae and confirm dermatophyte infection in an annular scaly plaque with central clearing. Wood lamp examination is more useful for certain fungal and bacterial conditions but is not diagnostic alone for most tinea infections. A Tzanck smear detects viral cytopathic changes and is not relevant to a fungal presentation.
  174. The correct answer is D (Start oral valacyclovir promptly, since treatment began within 72 hours of onset): Antiviral therapy started within 72 hours of herpes zoster onset reduces viral shedding, speeds healing, and lowers the risk of postherpetic neuralgia. Delaying treatment until crusting misses the window of maximal benefit. Corticosteroids alone without antiviral coverage do not adequately address the underlying viral reactivation.
  175. The correct answer is E (Cephalexin, which reliably targets streptococcal and methicillin-sensitive staphylococcal species): Nonpurulent cellulitis without abscess is most often caused by beta-hemolytic streptococci and methicillin-sensitive Staphylococcus aureus, making a cephalosporin like cephalexin appropriate first-line empiric therapy. Vancomycin targeting MRSA is reserved for purulent infections or risk factors for resistant organisms. Metronidazole and fluconazole target anaerobes and fungi, which are not the typical pathogens in this scenario.
  176. The correct answer is A (Topical permethrin 5% cream applied from the neck down over the entire body): Permethrin 5% cream applied to the entire body from neck to toes and left on for eight to fourteen hours is the recommended first-line treatment for scabies. Household contacts should be treated simultaneously regardless of symptoms. Topical corticosteroids and mupirocin do not eradicate the mite and oral antifungals are not effective against ectoparasites.
  177. The correct answer is B (Skin biopsy with direct immunofluorescence showing intercellular IgG deposits): Pemphigus vulgaris is confirmed by biopsy showing intercellular IgG deposition in a characteristic fishnet pattern along with a positive Nikolsky sign clinically. A KOH preparation and patch testing are unrelated to autoimmune blistering disease evaluation. Antinuclear antibody testing evaluates for lupus rather than a desmosomal autoimmune blistering process.
  178. The correct answer is D (Granular IgA deposition localized specifically at the tips of dermal papillae): Dermatitis herpetiformis, associated with celiac disease, shows granular IgA deposits localized to the tips of dermal papillae on direct immunofluorescence. Linear IgG deposition along the basement membrane is seen in bullous pemphigoid, a different disease. Intercellular IgG deposition characterizes pemphigus vulgaris, not the gluten-associated vesicular eruption described here.
  179. The correct answer is C (Urine porphyrin measurement, which typically shows markedly elevated uroporphyrins): Porphyria cutanea tarda, often associated with hepatitis C, is confirmed by demonstrating elevated urinary uroporphyrins, and the urine may show a characteristic pink or orange fluorescence under Wood lamp. Serum tryptase and immunoglobulin electrophoresis evaluate mast cell disorders and plasma cell dyscrasias, unrelated to this presentation. Antinuclear antibody testing is used for lupus rather than a porphyrin metabolism disorder.
  180. The correct answer is E (Alopecia areata presenting as a sharply demarcated, smooth, nonscarring patch): Alopecia areata classically produces a well-circumscribed, smooth, round patch of complete nonscarring hair loss with normal surrounding hair, consistent with the description given. Androgenetic alopecia causes diffuse patterned thinning rather than a discrete round patch. Tinea capitis and trichotillomania both typically show broken hairs or scale rather than a smooth bald patch.
  181. The correct answer is B (Cryotherapy or topical 5-fluorouracil to treat these premalignant sun-damaged lesions): Actinic keratoses are premalignant lesions with potential to progress to squamous cell carcinoma, so they warrant treatment with cryotherapy, topical 5-fluorouracil, or similar field therapy rather than observation alone. Oral antifungals and systemic corticosteroids do not address the abnormal keratinocyte proliferation involved. Wide excision with sentinel node biopsy is reserved for invasive malignancy, not premalignant lesions.
  182. The correct answer is A (A full-thickness excisional biopsy performed with narrow margins around the entire lesion): A full-thickness excisional biopsy with narrow margins preserves the ability to accurately measure Breslow depth, which guides melanoma staging and management. Shave biopsy risks transecting the base of a deeper lesion and compromising depth assessment. Fine-needle aspiration and curettage do not provide adequate tissue architecture for depth measurement.
  183. The correct answer is D (Slow, indolent growth occurring over many months with a rare risk of metastasis): Basal cell carcinoma typically grows slowly over months to years and rarely metastasizes, distinguishing it from squamous cell carcinoma, which can grow faster and carries higher metastatic potential. Development at chronic scars or ulcers and thick keratotic scale with a crater are more characteristic of squamous cell carcinoma. Mucosal lip location is also more typical of squamous cell carcinoma than basal cell carcinoma.
  184. The correct answer is C (Tinea versicolor caused by superficial overgrowth of Malassezia yeast organisms): The combination of yellow-green Wood lamp fluorescence and hyphae with spores in a spaghetti and meatball pattern on KOH preparation is diagnostic of tinea versicolor from Malassezia overgrowth. Vitiligo shows complete depigmentation without fungal elements on KOH testing. Pityriasis alba and postinflammatory hypopigmentation also lack the characteristic fungal findings described here.
  185. The correct answer is E (Perform rapid rewarming in a circulating water bath kept at 37 to 39 degrees Celsius): Frostbite is best managed with rapid rewarming in a warm water bath in the specified temperature range, which minimizes further tissue damage. Massaging the extremity, applying dry heat, or rubbing with snow all cause additional mechanical or thermal injury to already compromised tissue. Delaying rewarming unnecessarily prolongs ischemic injury to the affected extremity.
  186. The correct answer is A (Stage 1, characterized by nonblanchable erythema occurring over intact skin): A sacral wound with intact skin showing nonblanchable deep red discoloration and no open ulceration or exposed tissue matches the definition of a Stage 1 pressure injury. Stage 3 and Stage 4 require actual full-thickness skin loss with visible fat, muscle, or bone, none of which is present here. Deep tissue injury typically shows a maroon or purple discoloration or blood-filled blister rather than simple nonblanchable erythema.
  187. The correct answer is A (Pain that is markedly disproportionate to exam findings with rapidly advancing skin changes): Necrotizing fasciitis is suggested by severe pain out of proportion to visible findings combined with rapidly progressive skin discoloration, bullae, crepitus, and systemic toxicity, all of which demand emergent surgical evaluation. Mild well-demarcated erythema and slow indolent progression are more consistent with simple cellulitis. Isolated pruritus without pain or fever does not suggest a life-threatening soft tissue infection.
  188. The correct answer is D (Immediately discontinue the offending drug and transfer the patient to a burn unit): Stevens-Johnson syndrome and toxic epidermal necrolysis require immediate discontinuation of the culprit drug and transfer to a burn unit or intensive care setting for supportive wound care. Continuing the drug at any dose or switching to a related anticonvulsant risks worsening the reaction through cross-reactivity. Outpatient management with topical steroids or antihistamines is inadequate for a condition with this degree of body surface involvement.
  189. The correct answer is C (Topical hydroquinone combined consistently with strict daily sun protection measures): Melasma is managed with topical hydroquinone and rigorous sun protection as first-line therapy once breastfeeding or pregnancy concerns have resolved, since these interventions reduce melanin production and prevent worsening. Oral isotretinoin and hydroxychloroquine are not standard treatments for melasma. Systemic corticosteroids and dermabrasion are not appropriate first-line options for this pigmentary condition.
  190. The correct answer is E (Psoriatic nail disease, presenting classically with pitting and an oil-drop sign): Nail pitting combined with the oil-drop or salmon-patch discoloration in a patient with known skin plaques is characteristic of psoriatic nail involvement. Onychomycosis typically causes thickening and subungual debris, as described on the other foot, rather than pitting and oil-drop changes. Clubbing produces increased nail curvature and soft tissue enlargement, a distinctly different finding from pitting.
  191. The correct answer is B (Plasma aldosterone concentration to plasma renin activity ratio): resistant hypertension with unexplained hypokalemia is the classic clinical trigger for screening for primary hyperaldosteronism, and the aldosterone-to-renin ratio is the recommended first screening test. A high ratio with suppressed renin suggests autonomous aldosterone production. Cortisol testing, dexamethasone suppression, metanephrines, and ACTH stimulation evaluate other adrenal or pituitary conditions, not aldosterone excess.
  192. The correct answer is D (Primary adrenal insufficiency from autoimmune destruction of the adrenal cortex): the combination of chronic fatigue, weight loss, salt craving, hyperpigmentation, hyponatremia, and hyperkalemia is classic for primary adrenal insufficiency, most commonly caused by autoimmune adrenalitis. Hyperpigmentation occurs because low cortisol removes negative feedback, raising ACTH and its melanocyte-stimulating precursor. Cushing syndrome causes the opposite electrolyte pattern, and hypothyroidism or SIADH do not explain hyperkalemia or hyperpigmentation.
  193. The correct answer is A (Full hormonal workup for cortisol, catecholamine, and aldosterone excess): every adrenal incidentaloma requires baseline hormonal evaluation for subclinical cortisol excess, pheochromocytoma, and, if hypertensive or hypokalemic, primary aldosteronism, even without overt symptoms. Surgery, PET, adrenal vein sampling, or genetic testing are only pursued based on the results of this initial workup, not before it.
  194. The correct answer is E (Exophthalmos with periorbital edema from orbital involvement): Graves orbitopathy with proptosis and periorbital tissue involvement is unique to Graves disease among causes of hyperthyroidism, reflecting autoimmune infiltration of orbital tissue. Elevated free thyroxine, tachycardia, tremor, and goiter occur with any cause of thyrotoxicosis and are not specific to Graves disease.
  195. The correct answer is C (High TSH together with a low free thyroxine level): in primary hypothyroidism the thyroid gland fails, so thyroxine production falls and the pituitary compensates by raising TSH. This inverse relationship distinguishes primary thyroid failure from central hypothyroidism, where TSH would be low or inappropriately normal despite a low thyroxine level. The other combinations do not match primary gland failure.
  196. The correct answer is B (Subacute granulomatous thyroiditis following a viral illness): a painful, tender goiter with fever after a viral prodrome, accompanied by a transient thyrotoxic phase, is the hallmark presentation of subacute (de Quervain) thyroiditis. Hashimoto and Riedel thyroiditis are typically painless, Graves disease does not follow this transient course, and toxic multinodular goiter is not classically tender or postviral.
  197. The correct answer is D (Microcalcifications combined with irregular infiltrative nodule margins on ultrasound): this combination is a high-risk sonographic pattern strongly associated with papillary thyroid carcinoma and warrants biopsy regardless of nodule size. Purely cystic nodules, smooth margins, eggshell calcification, and homogeneous isoechoic texture are all reassuring features associated with a low risk of malignancy.
  198. The correct answer is A (Euthyroid sick syndrome related to nonthyroidal critical illness): critically ill patients commonly show low T3, variably low T4, and a normal or mildly low TSH due to altered peripheral hormone metabolism and cytokine effects, without true thyroid gland dysfunction. This pattern normalizes with recovery and should not prompt thyroid hormone replacement, unlike primary hypothyroidism, central hyperthyroidism, or Hashimoto thyroiditis.
  199. The correct answer is C (Acute physiologic stress such as infection or surgery): thyroid storm is most often precipitated by an acute stressor such as infection, surgery, trauma, or childbirth in a patient with underlying uncontrolled hyperthyroidism. Beta blocker overdose, medication withdrawal, iodine deficiency, and lithium therapy are not the classic precipitants described for this life-threatening decompensation.
  200. The correct answer is E (Myxedema coma resulting from severe untreated hypothyroidism): hypothermia, bradycardia, hyporeflexia, and altered mental status in a patient with known untreated hypothyroidism, often precipitated by cold exposure, is classic for myxedema coma. Thyroid storm and adrenal crisis typically present with hyperthermia or tachycardia rather than hypothermia and bradycardia, and DKA or cerebral salt wasting do not fit this specific picture.
  201. The correct answer is A (The TSH range shifts lower from hCG stimulation of the thyroid): hCG has weak TSH-receptor agonist activity, and its high levels in early pregnancy mildly stimulate the thyroid, suppressing TSH and shifting the normal reference range downward, especially in the first trimester. TSH does not become undetectable in most women, and increased thyroid binding globulin affects total but not TSH ranges directly.
  202. The correct answer is B (A catecholamine secreting tumor of the adrenal medulla): episodic headache, palpitations, diaphoresis, and paroxysmal hypertension are the classic triad of pheochromocytoma, caused by intermittent catecholamine release from the adrenal medulla. Insulinoma causes hypoglycemia rather than hypertension, aldosteronoma causes sustained rather than paroxysmal hypertension, and prolactinoma or medullary thyroid carcinoma do not produce this symptom pattern.
  203. The correct answer is D (Familial hypercholesterolemia from an LDL receptor mutation): markedly elevated LDL from a young age, tendon xanthomas, and a strong family history of early myocardial infarction are classic for familial hypercholesterolemia, an inherited LDL receptor defect. Chylomicronemia primarily raises triglycerides, not LDL, and diabetic, thyroid, or nephrotic causes of dyslipidemia do not typically produce tendon xanthomas.
  204. The correct answer is C (Massive chylomicron accumulation circulating in the plasma): severe hypertriglyceridemia above roughly 1000 to 2000 mg/dL reflects massive chylomicron accumulation, which is the primary driver of acute pancreatitis risk through capillary obstruction and lipotoxicity. LDL particles, HDL, lipoprotein(a), and apolipoprotein B100 are not the mechanism behind chylomicronemia-induced pancreatitis.
  205. The correct answer is E (Rotterdam criteria requiring any two of three key features): the Rotterdam criteria diagnose PCOS when at least two of oligo-ovulation, hyperandrogenism, and polycystic ovaries on imaging are present, after excluding other causes. Requiring all three features is incorrect since Rotterdam only requires two, and Framingham, Duke, and ATP III criteria are unrelated diagnostic systems for other conditions.
  206. The correct answer is B (Premature ovarian insufficiency with early estrogen loss): amenorrhea before age 40 with elevated FSH and low estradiol on two occasions confirms premature ovarian insufficiency, reflecting loss of ovarian follicular function. PCOS typically shows normal or low FSH, hypothalamic amenorrhea shows low FSH, and prolactinoma or Asherman syndrome do not produce this specific hormone pattern.
  207. The correct answer is A (Primary testicular failure with loss of negative feedback): elevated LH and FSH with low testosterone indicates the testes themselves are failing to respond, so the pituitary increases gonadotropin output through loss of negative feedback, defining primary hypogonadism. Secondary hypogonadism would show low or inappropriately normal gonadotropins, ruling out options B, D, and E, and androgen insensitivity presents differently.
  208. The correct answer is D (Antiandrogen activity along with weak estrogenic receptor binding): spironolactone blocks androgen receptors and has intrinsic weak estrogenic activity, shifting the androgen-to-estrogen balance in breast tissue and causing gynecomastia. It does not primarily act through prolactin stimulation, LH suppression, hepatic estrogen clearance changes, or aromatase induction, which are mechanisms of other drugs or conditions.
  209. The correct answer is E (Positive GAD65 and islet cell autoantibodies on testing): autoantibody positivity confirms autoimmune beta cell destruction characteristic of type 1 diabetes, especially in a lean adolescent with acute symptoms. Elevated C-peptide or insulin and acanthosis nigricans suggest insulin resistance typical of type 2 diabetes, and a family history of obesity also points toward type 2 rather than type 1 disease.
  210. The correct answer is C (Hemoglobin A1c of 6.8 percent on repeat testing): an A1c of 6.5 percent or higher meets diagnostic criteria for diabetes, and 6.8 percent qualifies independently alongside the fasting glucose already confirming the diagnosis. An A1c of 5.6 percent is prediabetic range, a random glucose of 150 without symptoms does not meet criteria, and the other values listed fall below diagnostic thresholds.
  211. The correct answer is D (Significant metabolic acidosis occurring together with ketosis): DKA is defined by the combination of ketosis and metabolic acidosis with a low pH, while HHS features extreme hyperglycemia with minimal ketosis and near-normal pH. Degree of hyperglycemia, polyuria, age, and sodium level can overlap between the two conditions and do not reliably distinguish them.
  212. The correct answer is B (Spot urine albumin to creatinine ratio measurement): annual screening for diabetic nephropathy relies on a spot urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio, which detects microalbuminuria earlier and more conveniently than other tests. Serum creatinine alone misses early kidney damage, a full 24-hour collection is impractical for routine screening, and renal ultrasound or cystatin C are not standard annual screening tools.
  213. The correct answer is A (Primary hyperparathyroidism resulting from a solitary parathyroid adenoma): among ambulatory outpatients, primary hyperparathyroidism, usually from a solitary parathyroid adenoma, is the most common cause of mild asymptomatic hypercalcemia found incidentally. Malignancy is the most common cause in hospitalized patients, while vitamin D toxicity, sarcoidosis, and milk alkali syndrome are far less common overall causes.
  214. The correct answer is E (Inadvertent removal or vascular injury of the parathyroid glands): postoperative hypocalcemia after thyroidectomy most commonly results from accidental removal or devascularization of the closely adjacent parathyroid glands, impairing PTH secretion. Calcitonin excess, vitamin D malabsorption, renal wasting, and magnesium overload are not the typical mechanism for this well-recognized surgical complication.
  215. The correct answer is C (Paget disease of bone with focal remodeling): an isolated elevated alkaline phosphatase with normal calcium, phosphate, and liver enzymes in an older adult with bone pain is classic for Paget disease, reflecting focal excessive bone remodeling. Osteomalacia and hyperparathyroidism typically alter calcium or phosphate, and myeloma or metastatic disease usually cause additional abnormal findings.
  216. The correct answer is A (A T-score of negative 2.5 or lower): the WHO defines osteoporosis as a T-score of -2.5 or below at the hip or spine, while a T-score between -1.0 and -2.5 defines osteopenia. A T-score above -1.0 is normal, and Z-scores are used for premenopausal women or younger patients rather than for standard postmenopausal diagnosis.
  217. The correct answer is D (Osteomalacia resulting from chronic vitamin D deficiency): diffuse bone pain, proximal muscle weakness, low vitamin D, low-normal calcium, and elevated alkaline phosphatase together describe osteomalacia from impaired bone mineralization due to vitamin D deficiency. Paget disease and hyperparathyroidism have different calcium and phosphate patterns, and myeloma or renal osteodystrophy would show additional distinguishing features.
  218. The correct answer is B (Phosphate retention and reduced calcitriol production stimulating the parathyroid glands): in CKD, declining renal function causes phosphate retention and reduced activation of vitamin D to calcitriol, both of which stimulate chronic parathyroid hormone secretion, driving CKD-MBD. This is the opposite of excess vitamin D activation, and it is not caused by an autonomous adenoma or direct urea toxicity to bone.
  219. The correct answer is E (Hypothalamic amenorrhea driven by relative energy deficiency): low body weight, intense exercise, low LH, FSH, and estradiol with normal prolactin and thyroid function describe functional hypothalamic amenorrhea from energy deficiency suppressing GnRH pulsatility. PCOS typically shows normal or elevated LH, and prolactinoma or Asherman syndrome would show different hormone or anatomic findings.
  220. The correct answer is C (A prolactin secreting lactotroph adenoma of the pituitary): markedly elevated prolactin causing amenorrhea and galactorrhea is the classic presentation of a prolactin-secreting pituitary adenoma, the most common functioning pituitary tumor. Growth hormone, ACTH, and TSH secreting adenomas cause distinct clinical syndromes, and nonfunctioning adenomas do not directly elevate prolactin to this degree, though they can cause mild elevation through stalk compression.
  221. The correct answer is B (Growth hormone secretion and the gonadotropin axes): in progressive pituitary failure from mass compression, growth hormone and gonadotropins are typically lost earliest, followed later by TSH and ACTH, with ADH loss being uncommon in anterior pituitary tumors. This typical order helps guide which hormone axes to test first when evaluating a pituitary mass.
  222. The correct answer is D (Central diabetes insipidus from impaired ADH release): traumatic brain injury can damage the hypothalamus or pituitary stalk, impairing ADH release and causing polyuria with dilute urine and rising sodium despite water access. SIADH and cerebral salt wasting cause hyponatremia rather than hypernatremia, and primary polydipsia would not cause rising sodium with free water access.
  223. The correct answer is A (Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion causing hyponatremia): hyponatremia with low serum osmolality, inappropriately concentrated urine, high urine sodium, and clinical euvolemia is the classic diagnostic profile of SIADH. Diabetes insipidus causes hypernatremia with dilute urine, and primary aldosteronism, adrenal insufficiency, and psychogenic polydipsia do not match this specific combination of findings.
  224. The correct answer is C (Whipple triad linking hypoglycemic symptoms, low glucose, and relief with feeding): the Whipple triad, consisting of symptoms consistent with hypoglycemia, a documented low glucose during symptoms, and relief after glucose administration, supports a diagnosis of an insulin-secreting tumor such as insulinoma. The other named triads describe entirely unrelated clinical syndromes in cardiology, hepatobiliary disease, or thrombosis.
  225. The correct answer is E (Serotonin and related vasoactive mediators released by the tumor): carcinoid syndrome results from excess serotonin and other vasoactive substances released by a neuroendocrine tumor, typically once liver metastases allow these mediators to bypass hepatic degradation and reach systemic circulation. Cortisol, VIP, calcitonin, and gastrin are associated with other distinct endocrine tumor syndromes, not classic carcinoid syndrome.
  226. The correct answer is D (Polyglandular autoimmune syndrome type 2, or Schmidt syndrome): the combination of autoimmune adrenal insufficiency, autoimmune thyroid disease, and type 1 diabetes developing in adulthood defines polyglandular autoimmune syndrome type 2, also called Schmidt syndrome. MEN syndromes involve tumors rather than autoimmune destruction, type 1 polyglandular syndrome presents in childhood with different features, and Waterhouse-Friderichsen is acute adrenal hemorrhage.
  227. The correct answer is D (A corrected calcium of approximately 9.4 mg/dL): using the formula of adding 0.8 mg/dL for every 1 g/dL the albumin is below 4, an albumin of 2.0 is 2 g/dL below normal, adding 1.6 mg/dL to the measured calcium of 7.8, giving a corrected value of about 9.4 mg/dL. This correction reveals the patient's true calcium is actually normal rather than low.
  228. The correct answer is A (Measure free thyroxine and free triiodothyronine levels next): once a low TSH is found, checking free T4 and free T3 determines whether the patient has overt primary hyperthyroidism, subclinical hyperthyroidism, or a central process affecting pituitary TSH secretion. Ultrasound, empiric treatment, simply waiting a year, or an uptake scan are inappropriate before establishing the actual thyroid hormone levels first.
  229. The correct answer is C (Start levothyroxine replacement therapy given the elevated TSH): in pregnancy, an elevated TSH, especially with positive thyroid peroxidase antibodies, warrants levothyroxine treatment because untreated maternal hypothyroidism can affect fetal neurodevelopment and pregnancy outcomes. Withholding treatment because free T4 is still normal is not recommended in pregnancy, and antithyroid drugs, an uptake scan, or thyroidectomy are inappropriate for hypothyroidism.
  230. The correct answer is E (A peak cortisol above approximately 18 to 20 mcg/dL): a robust cortisol rise to roughly 18 to 20 mcg/dL or higher after cosyntropin stimulation demonstrates adequate adrenal reserve and effectively excludes primary adrenal insufficiency. A blunted rise, a very low baseline alone, any detectable cortisol, or a decline after stimulation do not confirm normal adrenal function in this test.
  231. The correct answer is B (Obtain plasma free metanephrines first, then pursue imaging if positive): biochemical confirmation with plasma or urine metanephrines should always precede imaging in suspected pheochromocytoma, since imaging findings are meaningless without biochemical evidence of catecholamine excess. Going straight to CT, adrenal vein sampling, MIBG scan, or genetic testing before confirming the diagnosis biochemically is out of sequence.
  232. The correct answer is D (Perform fine needle aspiration biopsy of the nodule): a nodule showing high suspicion sonographic features such as microcalcifications and irregular margins warrants FNA biopsy even when relatively small, since these features carry meaningful malignancy risk. Simple observation, levothyroxine suppression, or proceeding directly to radioactive iodine ablation without a tissue diagnosis first are not appropriate next steps.
  233. The correct answer is A (Propylthiouracil given during the first trimester of pregnancy): propylthiouracil is preferred in the first trimester because methimazole carries a higher risk of specific birth defects during this critical period of organogenesis, with a switch back to methimazole often made after the first trimester. Radioactive iodine is contraindicated throughout pregnancy, and levothyroxine or propranolol alone do not adequately treat hyperthyroidism.
  234. The correct answer is C (Start an antithyroid drug first, then give iodine roughly an hour later): antithyroid medication must be given before iodine solution in thyroid storm, since giving iodine first can transiently worsen thyrotoxicosis by providing substrate for new hormone synthesis before the antithyroid drug blocks synthesis. Beta blockers are also needed but do not replace antithyroid therapy, and radioactive iodine or emergency surgery are not appropriate acute interventions.
  235. The correct answer is A (Intravenous levothyroxine given together with stress dose hydrocortisone): myxedema coma is treated with IV thyroid hormone plus stress dose corticosteroids, because rapid thyroid hormone replacement can unmask or worsen coexisting undiagnosed adrenal insufficiency if steroids are not also given. Oral therapy is inadequate in this critically ill state, and hydrocortisone alone, a beta blocker with antithyroid drug, or radioactive iodine do not address the hypothyroid emergency.
  236. The correct answer is E (Start high intensity statin therapy to substantially lower LDL): guidelines recommend high intensity statin therapy for secondary prevention in patients with established ASCVD who remain above LDL goals despite lifestyle changes, given the significant reduction in cardiovascular events this provides. Low intensity statins, fibrates, niacin, and omega-3 fatty acids alone are not preferred first line therapy for this indication.
  237. The correct answer is B (A fibrate such as fenofibrate as first line therapy): fibrates are the preferred first line agents specifically for severe hypertriglyceridemia because they substantially lower triglycerides and reduce pancreatitis risk, which statins address less effectively. Statins primarily target LDL rather than severe triglyceride elevation, and ezetimibe, bile acid sequestrants, and PCSK9 inhibitors are not first line for this indication.
  238. The correct answer is D (Combined oral contraceptive pills as first line therapy): combined oral contraceptives regulate menstrual cycles and reduce androgen levels, addressing both irregular menses and hirsutism simultaneously, making them first line therapy in women with PCOS not seeking pregnancy. Metformin mainly targets insulin resistance, spironolactone alone does not regulate cycles, and clomiphene or letrozole are fertility treatments not indicated here.
  239. The correct answer is C (Hematocrit and prostate specific antigen levels monitored during treatment): testosterone therapy can cause secondary polycythemia and may affect prostate tissue, so hematocrit and PSA should be monitored periodically during treatment. Thyroid function, glucose, potassium, and cortisol are not the primary parameters requiring monitoring specifically for testosterone replacement therapy safety.
  240. The correct answer is A (Begin potassium replacement before starting an insulin infusion): insulin drives potassium into cells and can precipitate dangerous hypokalemia, so if potassium is below about 3.3 mEq/L, it must be replaced before starting insulin therapy in DKA. Starting insulin immediately regardless of potassium, withholding both fluids and insulin, giving an insulin bolus, or waiting for potassium above 6 are all unsafe approaches.
  241. The correct answer is E (A GLP-1 receptor agonist with proven cardiovascular benefit): in patients with type 2 diabetes and established ASCVD, guidelines favor agents with demonstrated cardiovascular benefit, such as certain GLP-1 receptor agonists or SGLT2 inhibitors, as add-on therapy to metformin. Sulfonylureas, thiazolidinediones, alpha-glucosidase inhibitors, and DPP-4 inhibitors lack this same level of proven cardiovascular protection.
  242. The correct answer is B (A fifty gram one hour oral glucose challenge test): the standard approach to gestational diabetes screening at 24 to 28 weeks in most practices is a 50-gram one hour glucose challenge test, followed by a diagnostic 100-gram three hour test if elevated. Fasting glucose alone, random glucose, A1c alone, and urine dipstick testing are not the standard screening approach in pregnancy.
  243. The correct answer is D (Gabapentinoids such as pregabalin used as first line therapy): gabapentinoids like pregabalin and duloxetine are considered first line pharmacologic options for painful diabetic peripheral neuropathy given their established efficacy and safety profile. Opioids carry dependence risk and are not first line, NSAIDs have limited efficacy for neuropathic pain, and topical steroids or vitamin B12 alone are not standard first line treatments.
  244. The correct answer is C (Intravenous isotonic saline followed by an intravenous bisphosphonate): severe symptomatic hypercalcemia is treated with aggressive IV isotonic saline hydration to promote calcium excretion, followed by an IV bisphosphonate to inhibit ongoing bone resorption. Oral calcium binders, thiazide diuretics, loop diuretics without fluids first, and oral phosphate are inappropriate or counterproductive in this acute severe setting.
  245. The correct answer is A (An intravenous calcium gluconate infusion given urgently): symptomatic severe hypocalcemia with tetany and a prolonged QT interval requires urgent IV calcium gluconate to rapidly restore calcium levels and prevent life-threatening arrhythmia. Oral calcium, magnesium alone without calcium, loop diuretics, and oral vitamin D act too slowly for this acute emergency.
  246. The correct answer is E (Severe renal impairment that limits bisphosphonate use here): bisphosphonates are generally avoided or used with caution when GFR falls below about 30 to 35 mL per minute due to renal clearance concerns and risk of toxicity, making renal function the most important consideration here. Age, prior fracture, family history, and low body mass index are relevant risk factors but do not directly limit bisphosphonate use like renal impairment does.
  247. The correct answer is C (High dose ergocalciferol given weekly for eight to twelve weeks): significant vitamin D deficiency is commonly treated with high dose ergocalciferol, such as 50,000 IU weekly, for eight to twelve weeks to replete stores, followed by a lower maintenance dose. Standard 400 IU daily dosing is insufficient for repletion, a single dose without follow-up is inadequate, and calcium alone or IV vitamin D are not the standard initial approach.
  248. The correct answer is B (Phosphate binders taken together with meals to reduce absorption): phosphate binders taken with meals reduce intestinal absorption of dietary phosphate, which is essential in advanced CKD when dietary restriction alone fails to control hyperphosphatemia. Diuretics do not effectively lower phosphate in this setting, calcitonin addresses calcium rather than phosphate, and bisphosphonates work on bone remodeling, not intestinal phosphate absorption.
  249. The correct answer is D (Hormonal screening panel with visual field testing if near the chiasm): even small asymptomatic pituitary incidentalomas warrant baseline hormonal screening to detect subclinical hormone excess or deficiency, plus visual field testing if the lesion is near the optic chiasm. Immediate surgery or radiation is unwarranted for a small asymptomatic lesion, doing nothing skips important screening, and routine MEN1 genetic testing is not indicated for every incidental finding.
  250. The correct answer is A (Fluid restriction as the initial management strategy): mild to moderate asymptomatic hyponatremia from SIADH is typically managed first with fluid restriction to gradually raise serum sodium. Hypertonic saline is reserved for severe or symptomatic hyponatremia, aggressive normal saline hydration can actually worsen SIADH, desmopressin would worsen hyponatremia, and a high salt diet without fluid restriction is insufficient alone.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is E (Central diabetes insipidus from impaired ADH secretion): failure to concentrate urine during water deprivation followed by a significant rise in urine osmolality after exogenous desmopressin indicates the kidney can respond to ADH but the body was not producing enough, confirming central rather than nephrogenic DI. Nephrogenic DI would show minimal response to desmopressin, and SIADH or cerebral salt wasting would not present with polyuria requiring this test.
  2. The correct answer is C (Inappropriately elevated insulin and C-peptide during hypoglycemia): during a supervised fast, finding inappropriately high insulin and C-peptide at the time of documented hypoglycemia confirms endogenous hyperinsulinism from a source like an insulinoma, since normally insulin should be suppressed during hypoglycemia. Low insulin and C-peptide would suggest a non-insulin cause, and a detectable sulfonylurea with undetectable insulin would suggest factitious use rather than insulinoma.
  3. The correct answer is B (A twenty-four hour urine 5-hydroxyindoleacetic acid collection): 5-HIAA is the major breakdown product of serotonin, and an elevated 24-hour urine level confirms excess serotonin production consistent with carcinoid syndrome. Gastrin, metanephrines, calcinin, and urine free cortisol evaluate entirely different endocrine tumors such as gastrinoma, pheochromocytoma, medullary thyroid carcinoma, and Cushing syndrome respectively.
  4. The correct answer is E (Treatment is generally favored given her age and associated risks): in older patients, subclinical hyperthyroidism is associated with increased risk of atrial fibrillation and osteoporotic fracture, so treatment is generally recommended even without overt symptoms, particularly with a persistently suppressed TSH. Withholding treatment entirely, or jumping straight to radioactive iodine, high dose antithyroid drugs, or thyroidectomy without appropriate risk stratification are not the recommended balanced approach.
  5. The correct answer is B (Consider adrenalectomy given her significant associated comorbidities): mild autonomous cortisol secretion, even without classic Cushingoid features, contributes to worsening hypertension, diabetes, and cardiovascular risk, so surgical removal is reasonable when significant related comorbidities are present. Ignoring the finding because features are subtle, treating unrelated conditions like the thyroid or diabetes alone, or simply repeating imaging without hormonal reassessment fail to address the underlying driver of her comorbidities.
  6. The correct answer is D (Start alpha blockade first, followed later by beta blockade): alpha blockade must precede beta blockade in pheochromocytoma preparation, because starting a beta blocker first leaves alpha-mediated vasoconstriction unopposed, which can precipitate a severe hypertensive crisis. ACE inhibitors and calcium channel blockers alone do not provide adequate perioperative catecholamine blockade, and omitting blood pressure medication before surgery is dangerous given the risk of intraoperative catecholamine surges.
  7. The correct answer is A (Type 1 disease, treated primarily with an antithyroid drug): increased vascularity and elevated uptake indicate type 1 amiodarone-induced thyrotoxicosis, driven by excess hormone synthesis from the iodine load, which is treated with antithyroid drugs to block synthesis. Type 2 disease is a destructive thyroiditis typically treated with corticosteroids rather than antithyroid drugs, and radioactive iodine is generally not useful in either type given the iodine loading from amiodarone.
  8. The correct answer is C (Use clinical context, pituitary imaging, and retesting after illness resolves): distinguishing euthyroid sick syndrome from central hypothyroidism in critical illness requires integrating clinical context, considering pituitary imaging if central disease is suspected, and retesting thyroid function once the acute illness resolves. TSH alone is unreliable in this setting, starting levothyroxine empirically is not recommended without further evaluation, and an uptake scan or thyroglobulin level do not help differentiate these two conditions.
  9. The correct answer is E (Anaplastic thyroid carcinoma with rapid local invasion): rapid growth, hard fixed consistency, hoarseness, and stridor developing over weeks in an older patient with a longstanding goiter are classic for anaplastic thyroid carcinoma, one of the most aggressive human cancers. Papillary, follicular, medullary, and Hurthle cell carcinomas typically progress much more slowly and do not present with this rapid, locally destructive course.
  10. The correct answer is D (An early morning 17-hydroxyprogesterone level drawn for evaluation): nonclassic congenital adrenal hyperplasia from 21-hydroxylase deficiency can mimic PCOS with hirsutism and irregular cycles, and an elevated early morning 17-hydroxyprogesterone level identifies this alternative diagnosis before confirming PCOS. Fasting insulin, testosterone, LH to FSH ratio, and anti-Mullerian hormone support a PCOS workup but do not specifically exclude congenital adrenal hyperplasia.
  11. The correct answer is B (Elevated ACTH and its precursor peptide stimulate melanocytes in primary disease): in primary adrenal insufficiency, loss of cortisol negative feedback markedly raises ACTH and its co-secreted precursor peptide, which stimulates melanocyte-stimulating hormone receptors and causes hyperpigmentation. Secondary adrenal insufficiency has low ACTH from pituitary failure, so this stimulation does not occur, explaining the pale skin seen in that patient instead.
  12. The correct answer is C (Klinefelter syndrome is primary hypogonadism while Kallmann is hypogonadotropic hypogonadism): Klinefelter syndrome causes primary testicular failure with elevated gonadotropins due to loss of negative feedback, while Kallmann syndrome results from impaired GnRH neuron migration causing low gonadotropins and hypogonadotropic hypogonadism. The two conditions have opposite gonadotropin patterns and different genetic mechanisms, so the other options describing identical or reversed patterns are incorrect.
  13. The correct answer is A (Noninsulinoma pancreatogenous hypoglycemia or late dumping syndrome): postprandial hypoglycemia occurring one to three hours after meals following bariatric surgery, rather than during fasting, is characteristic of late dumping syndrome or noninsulinoma pancreatogenous hypoglycemia, related to altered gastric emptying and exaggerated insulin response. Classic insulinoma typically causes fasting rather than postprandial hypoglycemia, and the other listed conditions do not fit this specific postprandial timing and surgical history.
  14. The correct answer is D (A positive plasma or urine sulfonylurea screen result): a positive sulfonylurea screen in a patient with elevated insulin and C-peptide indicates the hyperinsulinemic state is caused by exogenous sulfonylurea ingestion rather than an endogenous insulinoma. An isolated elevated proinsulin level, undetectable C-peptide, a negative drug screen, or low insulin antibody titers do not point toward surreptitious sulfonylurea use as the cause.
  15. The correct answer is E (Careful volume status assessment, since cerebral salt wasting causes hypovolemia): the key distinguishing feature between SIADH and cerebral salt wasting is volume status, since SIADH patients are euvolemic while cerebral salt wasting causes true hypovolemia from renal sodium loss, changing management from fluid restriction to volume repletion. Serum sodium, urine osmolality, TSH, and potassium levels can overlap between the two conditions and do not reliably differentiate them.
  16. The correct answer is B (A significant rise after desmopressin confirms central DI rather than polydipsia): failure to concentrate urine during water deprivation followed by a clear response to desmopressin shows the kidneys can respond appropriately to ADH but endogenous secretion was inadequate, confirming central diabetes insipidus rather than primary polydipsia, where urine would have concentrated during deprivation alone. Nephrogenic DI would show a poor response even after desmopressin is given, unlike what is described here.
  17. The correct answer is D (Adrenal imaging, since the low ACTH suggests a primary adrenal source): an undetectable ACTH in the setting of confirmed hypercortisolism indicates ACTH-independent disease, meaning the cortisol excess is coming directly from the adrenal gland, making adrenal imaging the appropriate next step. High dose dexamethasone suppression, CRH stimulation, and petrosal sinus sampling are used to localize ACTH-dependent disease, and chest CT for ectopic ACTH is irrelevant when ACTH is undetectable.
  18. The correct answer is C (A saline infusion test or oral sodium loading confirmatory test): after a positive screening aldosterone-to-renin ratio, a confirmatory test such as saline infusion or oral sodium loading is required to demonstrate failure of aldosterone suppression before pursuing subtype localization. Simply repeating the initial ratio does not confirm autonomy, adrenal vein sampling is used later for lateralization, and urine cortisol or cosyntropin stimulation evaluate unrelated conditions.
  19. The correct answer is D (Evaluate for secondary causes such as hypogonadism or steroid use): osteoporosis or fragility fracture in a young man is atypical and should prompt a thorough evaluation for secondary causes, such as hypogonadism, glucocorticoid use, or other metabolic bone disorders, before assuming primary age-related disease. Starting a bisphosphonate immediately, doing nothing further, simply repeating imaging, or using calcium alone all skip this essential secondary cause workup.
  20. The correct answer is E (High output heart failure and possible sarcomatous transformation of the bone): Paget disease can cause increased vascularity within affected bone leading to high output cardiac failure, and new worsening focal bone pain raises concern for rare malignant transformation into osteosarcoma, both of which require prompt evaluation. The other options describing unrelated cardiac disease, hypocalcemia, unrelated osteoporosis, or simple anemia do not account for this specific combination of new findings.
  21. The correct answer is B (Avoid oversuppression of PTH in order to prevent adynamic bone disease): in dialysis patients with low PTH and low bone turnover, aggressive suppression of PTH through excessive vitamin D or calcium therapy should be avoided because it can worsen adynamic bone disease, which impairs bone remodeling and increases fracture risk. Parathyroidectomy and bisphosphonates are not appropriate for a low PTH low-turnover state, and ignoring PTH trends entirely is unsafe management.
  22. The correct answer is D (Residual beta cell function causes a honeymoon phase needing continued low-dose insulin): shortly after diagnosis, remaining functional beta cells can partially recover and transiently produce more endogenous insulin, temporarily lowering exogenous insulin requirements in a phenomenon called the honeymoon phase, but low-dose insulin should still be continued rather than stopped. This does not indicate a wrong diagnosis, type 2 diabetes, worsening insulin resistance, or a coexisting insulinoma.
  23. The correct answer is C (Accelerated starvation ketosis and enhanced lipolysis occur during pregnancy): pregnancy is a state of accelerated starvation with enhanced lipolysis and insulin resistance from placental hormones, allowing ketoacidosis to develop at lower glucose levels than typically seen outside of pregnancy. Pregnancy does not uniformly increase insulin sensitivity or eliminate ketone production, and fetal glucose consumption actually contributes to this lower glucose threshold rather than having no effect.
  24. The correct answer is E (Diabetic autonomic neuropathy causing delayed gastric emptying, or gastroparesis): long-standing diabetes can damage the autonomic nerves controlling gastric motility, leading to gastroparesis with early satiety, bloating, and unpredictable glucose control from erratic food absorption relative to insulin dosing. Dietary nonadherence, unrelated peptic ulcer disease, insulin allergy, and isolated exocrine insufficiency do not typically produce this specific combination of symptoms in a diabetic patient.
  25. The correct answer is A (Sheehan syndrome resulting from postpartum pituitary infarction): severe postpartum hemorrhage can cause ischemic infarction of the enlarged pituitary gland, leading to Sheehan syndrome with failure to lactate from low prolactin and subsequent hypopituitarism symptoms like amenorrhea and fatigue. Primary ovarian failure, postpartum thyroiditis, Asherman syndrome, and unrelated lymphocytic hypophysitis do not connect directly to the hemorrhage-induced pituitary injury described here.
  26. The correct answer is B (This childhood pattern with candidiasis suggests polyglandular autoimmune syndrome type 1): chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis combined with hypoparathyroidism and adrenal insufficiency presenting in childhood is characteristic of polyglandular autoimmune syndrome type 1, distinct from the adult-onset type 2 pattern of adrenal insufficiency with thyroid disease and diabetes. Type 2 does not typically include hypoparathyroidism, type 1 does involve the adrenal glands, and the two syndromes have different genetic causes.
  27. The correct answer is D (A perioperative octreotide infusion to blunt hormone release during surgery): octreotide suppresses the release of serotonin and other vasoactive mediators triggered by tumor manipulation during surgery, making a perioperative infusion the key measure to prevent carcinoid crisis. Beta blockers alone, avoiding sedation, high dose corticosteroids alone, and skipping specific precautions do not adequately address the risk of a life-threatening intraoperative mediator surge.
  28. The correct answer is C (A clinical scoring system such as Burch-Wartofsky assessing systemic decompensation): thyroid storm is a clinical diagnosis based on the degree of systemic organ decompensation, and scoring systems like the Burch-Wartofsky score integrate temperature, cardiovascular, gastrointestinal, and central nervous system findings to differentiate storm from uncomplicated severe hyperthyroidism. TSH, free thyroxine, uptake percentage, and ultrasound vascularity do not reliably distinguish thyroid storm from severe hyperthyroidism alone.
  29. The correct answer is E (An intravenous insulin infusion, with or without therapeutic apheresis): severe hypertriglyceridemia-induced pancreatitis with inability to tolerate oral intake is treated acutely with an IV insulin infusion, which enhances triglyceride clearance, with apheresis considered for extremely severe or refractory cases. Oral fibrate, fish oil, and statin therapy require oral intake and act too slowly for this acute setting, and delaying treatment risks ongoing pancreatic injury.
  30. The correct answer is B (Elevated 25-hydroxyvitamin D suggests toxicity while elevated 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D suggests granulomatous disease): vitamin D intoxication is characterized by markedly elevated 25-hydroxyvitamin D from excess intake, while granulomatous diseases like sarcoidosis cause extrarenal conversion to the active form, elevating 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D specifically, distinguishing the two low-PTH hypercalcemia causes. PTH alone is low in both conditions and cannot differentiate them, and the metabolite patterns are not identical or uniformly low in either condition.
  31. The correct answer is D (Transient relaxation of the lower esophageal sphincter allowing gastric acid to reflux upward): typical GERD symptoms result mainly from inappropriate transient relaxations of the lower esophageal sphincter allowing gastric acid to reflux into the esophagus. Dysmotility can contribute but is not the primary mechanism. Autoimmune destruction and bacterial overgrowth are not recognized causes of GERD. A fixed stricture would cause dysphagia rather than typical reflux symptoms.
  32. The correct answer is A (Intestinal metaplasia of the distal esophageal mucosa with columnar epithelium containing goblet cells): Barrett esophagus is defined histologically by replacement of normal squamous epithelium with columnar intestinal metaplasia containing goblet cells. Squamous dysplasia with keratin pearls describes squamous cell carcinoma features, not Barrett. Chronic gastritis and granulomatous inflammation describe unrelated conditions. Eosinophilic infiltration describes eosinophilic esophagitis, a distinct diagnosis.
  33. The correct answer is C (Surveillance endoscopy repeated every three to five years to monitor for dysplasia): nondysplastic Barrett esophagus is managed with periodic surveillance endoscopy, typically every three to five years, along with continued acid suppression. Esophagectomy and ablation are reserved for dysplasia or malignancy, not nondysplastic disease. A two week repeat endoscopy is unnecessarily frequent. Stopping surveillance entirely ignores the ongoing cancer risk associated with Barrett esophagus.
  34. The correct answer is B (Achalasia with failure of lower esophageal sphincter relaxation and absent esophageal peristalsis): achalasia is defined by impaired relaxation of the lower esophageal sphincter combined with absent esophageal peristalsis on manometry. Diffuse esophageal spasm shows uncoordinated contractions rather than absent peristalsis with a nonrelaxing sphincter. Nutcracker esophagus involves high amplitude peristaltic contractions. Scleroderma esophagus shows low sphincter pressure rather than failure to relax, and Zenker diverticulum is an anatomic outpouching, not a manometric diagnosis.
  35. The correct answer is C (Achalasia with a classic bird beak narrowing at the gastroesophageal junction on barium study): the bird beak appearance on barium swallow with progressive dysphagia to both solids and liquids is the classic radiographic finding of achalasia. Peptic stricture and Schatzki ring typically cause dysphagia mainly to solids. Esophageal webs are thin membranes usually in the proximal esophagus. Eosinophilic esophagitis usually shows rings or strictures but not the bird beak sign.
  36. The correct answer is B (Candida albicans esophagitis producing characteristic white plaques visible throughout the esophageal mucosa): Candida esophagitis is the most common infectious esophagitis in immunocompromised patients and classically presents with white plaques visible on endoscopy. Cytomegalovirus and herpes simplex virus typically cause discrete ulcers rather than plaques. Mycobacterium tuberculosis rarely causes esophagitis. Helicobacter pylori affects the stomach and duodenum, not the esophagus.
  37. The correct answer is A (A Mallory-Weiss tear, a longitudinal mucosal laceration at the gastroesophageal junction from retching): a longitudinal mucosal tear at the gastroesophageal junction following forceful retching or vomiting is the classic description of a Mallory-Weiss tear. Boerhaave syndrome involves full-thickness esophageal rupture, a more severe and different entity. Esophageal varices are dilated submucosal veins unrelated to vomiting-induced tears. Peptic ulcer perforation and Dieulafoy lesion have different mechanisms and endoscopic appearances.
  38. The correct answer is B (Urea breath testing, a highly accurate noninvasive method to detect active infection): the urea breath test is a highly accurate noninvasive method to detect active Helicobacter pylori infection and is also useful to confirm eradication. Serum IgG antibody testing cannot distinguish active from past infection. Complete blood count and fecal occult blood testing do not detect Helicobacter pylori. Abdominal ultrasound has no role in diagnosing this infection.
  39. The correct answer is A (Quadruple therapy combining bismuth, tetracycline, metronidazole, and a proton pump inhibitor together): bismuth-based quadruple therapy is a recommended first-line regimen for Helicobacter pylori eradication, especially given rising clarithromycin resistance. Proton pump inhibitor monotherapy does not eradicate the infection. H2 blocker plus sucralfate and antacids alone do not treat the underlying infection. Simply stopping aspirin does not address active Helicobacter pylori infection.
  40. The correct answer is C (Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, which impair prostaglandin-mediated protection of the gastric lining): chronic NSAID use impairs prostaglandin-mediated mucosal protection and is a leading cause of erosive gastritis and peptic ulcers. Selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors and calcium channel blockers are not major causes of ulcer disease. Beta blockers and thiazide diuretics likewise lack this strong association with gastric mucosal injury.
  41. The correct answer is A (Functional non-ulcer dyspepsia, a diagnosis of exclusion after a negative structural workup): when epigastric symptoms persist despite a normal endoscopy and negative Helicobacter pylori testing, functional dyspepsia is the most likely diagnosis. Perforated ulcer would present acutely with peritoneal signs, not chronic recurrent discomfort. Gastric adenocarcinoma and Zollinger-Ellison syndrome would typically show structural or biochemical abnormalities on workup. Achalasia primarily causes dysphagia rather than epigastric discomfort.
  42. The correct answer is C (Long-standing diabetes mellitus causing vagal nerve damage and delayed gastric emptying): chronic diabetes can damage the vagus nerve and lead to diabetic gastroparesis, presenting with early satiety, bloating, and delayed gastric emptying. Hyperthyroidism typically causes increased gut motility rather than delayed emptying. Chronic constipation is a symptom rather than a cause of gastroparesis. Iron deficiency anemia and B12 deficiency are not established causes of gastroparesis.
  43. The correct answer is C (Elevated tissue transglutaminase IgA antibody, the preferred initial serologic screening test): tissue transglutaminase IgA antibody is the preferred initial serologic screening test for celiac disease due to its high sensitivity and specificity. Anti-mitochondrial antibody is associated with primary biliary cholangitis, not celiac disease. Perinuclear antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody is linked to ulcerative colitis. Anti-smooth muscle antibody suggests autoimmune hepatitis, and low complement levels are not part of celiac disease screening.
  44. The correct answer is E (Intestinal dysmotility or anatomic stasis such as diverticula, strictures, or blind loops): conditions that slow intestinal transit or create stagnant loops, such as strictures, diverticula, or motility disorders, predispose to bacterial overgrowth. Isolated lactose intolerance and uncomplicated peptic ulcer disease do not cause bacterial overgrowth. Acute viral gastroenteritis is a transient infection unrelated to chronic overgrowth. Well-controlled hypothyroidism is not a typical risk factor.
  45. The correct answer is E (Short bowel syndrome from extensive resection leaving insufficient absorptive intestinal surface area): extensive small bowel resection leading to chronic diarrhea, malabsorption, and fat-soluble vitamin deficiency defines short bowel syndrome. Irritable bowel syndrome does not follow bowel resection and does not cause vitamin deficiencies. Gastroparesis involves delayed gastric emptying, not malabsorption from resection. Chronic pancreatitis causes malabsorption through enzyme deficiency rather than loss of absorptive bowel length.
  46. The correct answer is A (Acute mesenteric ischemia from an embolus in a patient with atrial fibrillation): sudden severe abdominal pain out of proportion to physical exam findings in a patient with atrial fibrillation strongly suggests embolic acute mesenteric ischemia. Irritable bowel syndrome and gastroparesis present with chronic, not acute, symptoms and are not linked to embolic risk. Diverticulitis typically causes localized left lower quadrant pain with more gradual onset and less severe pain-to-exam mismatch.
  47. The correct answer is C (Skip lesions with transmural inflammation extending through the full thickness of the bowel wall): Crohn disease characteristically shows discontinuous skip lesions with transmural inflammation, distinguishing it from the continuous mucosal-only inflammation of ulcerative colitis. Continuous inflammation and universal rectal sparing describe ulcerative colitis patterns inaccurately paired here. Toxic megacolon can occur in either disease but is not a defining colonoscopic feature. Backwash ileitis without granulomas is typical of ulcerative colitis, not Crohn disease.
  48. The correct answer is A (Campylobacter gastroenteritis acquired from ingestion of undercooked or improperly handled poultry): Campylobacter is a common cause of acute bacterial gastroenteritis following ingestion of undercooked poultry, presenting with watery or bloody diarrhea, fever, and cramps. Clostridioides difficile colitis typically follows antibiotic exposure, not poultry ingestion. Celiac disease and ulcerative colitis are chronic conditions, not acute food-related illnesses. Ischemic colitis is more common in older patients with vascular risk factors.
  49. The correct answer is B (Age forty-five, the current recommended starting age for average-risk colorectal screening): major guidelines now recommend average-risk colorectal cancer screening beginning at age 45 due to rising incidence in younger adults. Age 30 and 40 are too early for average-risk screening. Age 60 and 65 are too late and would miss the recommended starting point. This lowered threshold reflects updated evidence-based guideline changes.
  50. The correct answer is B (Familial adenomatous polyposis with hundreds of colonic adenomas from APC gene mutations): hundreds to thousands of colonic adenomatous polyps with early-onset colon cancer in the family is classic for familial adenomatous polyposis caused by APC gene mutations. Juvenile polyposis and Peutz-Jeghers syndrome involve hamartomatous polyps, not innumerable adenomas. Cronkhite-Canada syndrome is a rare nonhereditary polyposis with distinct features. Cowden syndrome involves hamartomas and increased risk of several cancers but not innumerable adenomatous polyps.
  51. The correct answer is E (Most diverticula located in the sigmoid colon, with the majority remaining asymptomatic lifelong): diverticula are most commonly found in the sigmoid colon and the majority of affected patients remain asymptomatic throughout life. They are not confined to the ascending colon. Diverticulosis is not primarily an infectious process, and surgery is not required at initial diagnosis. Massive hematochezia can occur but is not the typical presentation of uncomplicated diverticular disease.
  52. The correct answer is A (Continuous mucosal inflammation beginning at the rectum and extending proximally without gaps): ulcerative colitis characteristically begins in the rectum and extends proximally in a continuous, uninterrupted pattern limited to the mucosa. Patchy skip lesions and isolated terminal ileal involvement describe Crohn disease. Perianal fistulas are a hallmark of Crohn disease, not ulcerative colitis. Granulomatous esophageal inflammation is not a feature of either condition in this way.
  53. The correct answer is E (Irritable bowel syndrome, a functional disorder with pain related to defecation and a normal colonoscopy): recurrent abdominal pain associated with defecation and altered bowel habits in the setting of a normal colonoscopy is characteristic of irritable bowel syndrome, a functional disorder.
