Crohn disease and colorectal cancer would typically show structural abnormalities on colonoscopy. Ischemic colitis presents acutely, often in older patients with vascular risk factors. Microscopic colitis has a normal-appearing colonoscopy but abnormal biopsies, unlike straightforward irritable bowel syndrome.
  54. The correct answer is D (Internal hemorrhoids causing painless bright red bleeding with associated perianal itching): painless bright red blood coating the stool with anal itching is the classic presentation of internal hemorrhoids. Anal fissures typically cause painful defecation rather than painless bleeding. Rectal prolapse presents with visible tissue protrusion. Perianal abscess causes significant pain and swelling, and colorectal polyps more often cause occult or intermittent bleeding without the classic itching pattern.
  55. The correct answer is D (Recent broad-spectrum antibiotic therapy disrupting normal protective colonic bacterial flora): disruption of normal colonic flora by broad-spectrum antibiotics is the strongest and most well-established risk factor for Clostridioides difficile colitis. Influenza vaccination and mild viral respiratory infections have no established link. A low-fiber diet is not a primary risk factor. Proton pump inhibitor use alone carries a smaller relative risk compared with recent antibiotic exposure.
  56. The correct answer is D (Acute appendicitis with classic periumbilical pain migrating to the right lower quadrant): pain migrating from the periumbilical region to the right lower quadrant with anorexia and low-grade fever is the classic presentation of acute appendicitis. Diverticulitis typically causes left lower quadrant pain. Mesenteric adenitis can mimic appendicitis but is less classic in presentation and often follows a viral illness. Crohn disease flare and ovarian torsion have different associated features and are less consistent with this classic migratory pain pattern.
  57. The correct answer is C (Elevated serum lipase greater than three times the upper limit of normal): a lipase level more than three times normal, combined with characteristic pain, supports the diagnosis of acute pancreatitis. Low amylase and elevated ammonia are not diagnostic criteria. A low white blood cell count would be atypical since pancreatitis often causes leukocytosis. Fecal calprotectin reflects intestinal inflammation, not pancreatic injury.
  58. The correct answer is B (Chronic abdominal pain, steatorrhea, and diabetes mellitus from progressive pancreatic insufficiency): the classic triad of chronic pancreatitis reflects progressive exocrine and endocrine pancreatic insufficiency alongside chronic pain. Jaundice, fever, and right upper quadrant pain describe cholangitis. Hematemesis and melena suggest GI bleeding, not chronic pancreatitis. Bloody diarrhea and tenesmus suggest colitis, while dysphagia and odynophagia suggest esophageal disease.
  59. The correct answer is A (An insulinoma causing recurrent hypoglycemia with elevated fasting insulin and C-peptide levels): recurrent hypoglycemia with elevated fasting insulin and C-peptide in the setting of a pancreatic mass is classic for insulinoma. Gastrinoma causes recurrent peptic ulcers and diarrhea, not hypoglycemia. Glucagonoma classically causes hyperglycemia and a characteristic skin rash. VIPoma causes watery diarrhea and electrolyte disturbances, and somatostatinoma classically causes diabetes, gallstones, and steatorrhea.
  60. The correct answer is D (Female sex, obesity, and rapid weight loss, all of which alter bile cholesterol saturation): the classic risk factors for cholesterol gallstones include female sex, obesity, and rapid weight loss, all of which alter bile cholesterol saturation. Male sex and chronic constipation are not established major risk factors. Regular vigorous exercise and low body mass index with a vegetarian diet are generally protective rather than causative. Chronic proton pump inhibitor use is not a recognized major gallstone risk factor.
  61. The correct answer is B (Acute cholecystitis presenting with fever, right upper quadrant pain, and a positive Murphy sign): fever, right upper quadrant pain, and a positive Murphy sign together are the classic clinical triad of acute cholecystitis. Acute pancreatitis typically causes epigastric pain radiating to the back rather than a positive Murphy sign. Peptic ulcer disease presents with epigastric burning pain. Acute hepatitis usually presents with diffuse right upper quadrant discomfort and jaundice rather than a focal Murphy sign, and choledocholithiasis without infection typically lacks fever.
  62. The correct answer is B (A dilated common bile duct with a stone directly visualized within the duct): direct visualization of a stone within a dilated common bile duct confirms choledocholithiasis rather than simple gallbladder stones. Gallbladder wall thickening and pericholecystic fluid suggest cholecystitis rather than duct obstruction. Normal liver enzymes with gallstones suggests uncomplicated cholelithiasis. A sonographic Murphy sign alone points toward cholecystitis, not bile duct stone disease.
  63. The correct answer is E (Ascending cholangitis caused by biliary obstruction and infection producing fever, jaundice, and pain): Charcot triad of fever, jaundice, and right upper quadrant pain is classically associated with ascending cholangitis caused by biliary obstruction and infection. Chronic pancreatitis and primary biliary cholangitis present with more insidious, chronic symptoms. Acute cholecystitis presents with fever and pain but not typically jaundice as a core feature. Hepatocellular carcinoma does not classically present with this acute febrile triad.
  64. The correct answer is D (Ulcerative colitis, which has a strong, well-documented association with this biliary condition): primary sclerosing cholangitis has a strong, well-documented association with ulcerative colitis, with many patients having both conditions. Celiac disease, irritable bowel syndrome, diverticulosis, and gastroparesis do not share this strong established link with primary sclerosing cholangitis.
  65. The correct answer is B (An isolated positive hepatitis B surface antibody without core antibody, consistent with vaccination): vaccination produces isolated surface antibody positivity without core antibody, since the vaccine contains only surface antigen. A positive surface antigen indicates active infection. Positive core antibody IgM indicates acute natural infection. Positive e antigen indicates high viral replication in natural infection, and hepatitis C antibody positivity is unrelated to hepatitis B immunity status.
  66. The correct answer is C (Esophageal varices and caput medusae forming from portosystemic collateral vessel development): portal hypertension leads to formation of portosystemic collaterals, manifesting as esophageal varices and caput medusae on the abdominal wall. Bradycardia with hypertension and elevated diastolic pressure are not typical portal hypertension findings. Peripheral vasoconstriction is inconsistent with the vasodilated hemodynamic state of advanced cirrhosis, and warm skin with bounding pulses actually reflects the hyperdynamic circulation seen in cirrhosis, not vasoconstriction.
  67. The correct answer is C (Upper endoscopy to evaluate for complications or alternative diagnoses causing refractory symptoms): when GERD symptoms fail to respond to an adequate trial of proton pump inhibitor therapy, upper endoscopy is indicated to evaluate for complications or alternative diagnoses. Simply increasing the dose or switching to an H2 blocker without further evaluation risks missing a more serious underlying process. Abdominal CT and barium enema are not appropriate first-line tools for evaluating refractory reflux symptoms.
  68. The correct answer is B (Endoscopic radiofrequency ablation or endoscopic resection to eradicate the dysplastic tissue): confirmed high-grade dysplasia in Barrett esophagus warrants endoscopic eradication therapy such as radiofrequency ablation or endoscopic mucosal resection to reduce progression to cancer. Continued routine surveillance or observation alone is insufficient given the significant malignancy risk. Lifelong medical therapy without an ablative procedure does not address the dysplastic tissue itself.
  69. The correct answer is D (Upper endoscopy to exclude pseudoachalasia caused by a malignant tumor at the junction): before treating presumed achalasia, endoscopy is essential to rule out pseudoachalasia caused by a tumor at the gastroesophageal junction mimicking the manometric findings. Fecal fat testing, hydrogen breath testing, serum gastrin, and colonoscopy do not evaluate for this critical mimicking condition.
  70. The correct answer is D (A nonselective beta blocker or endoscopic band ligation for primary variceal bleeding prevention): primary prophylaxis against variceal bleeding relies on nonselective beta blockers or endoscopic band ligation for patients with known varices. Proton pump inhibitors and sucralfate do not reduce variceal bleeding risk. Indefinite prophylactic antibiotics are not standard for primary prevention, and scheduled sclerotherapy regardless of variceal size is not the preferred prophylactic strategy.
  71. The correct answer is A (Cytomegalovirus, producing deep punched-out esophageal ulcers best biopsied from the ulcer base): deep, punched-out ulcers in the esophagus of an immunocompromised patient are characteristic of cytomegalovirus esophagitis, and biopsies from the ulcer base improve diagnostic yield. Candida typically causes plaques rather than deep ulcers. Herpes simplex virus typically causes small vesicular or volcano-shaped ulcers with biopsies best taken from the ulcer edge. Helicobacter pylori and Epstein-Barr virus are not typical causes of this endoscopic finding.
  72. The correct answer is D (Fluid resuscitation followed promptly by urgent upper endoscopy for diagnosis and hemostasis): hemodynamically unstable upper GI bleeding requires immediate fluid resuscitation followed by urgent endoscopy for diagnosis and hemostasis. Surgery is reserved for cases refractory to endoscopic therapy. Discharge and oral iron are inappropriate given active instability. Colonoscopy is not the correct initial procedure for an upper GI bleeding source.
  73. The correct answer is C (A urea breath test or stool antigen test performed four weeks after finishing therapy): confirmation of Helicobacter pylori eradication should use a urea breath test or stool antigen test performed at least four weeks after completing antibiotic therapy and while off proton pump inhibitors if possible. Serum antibody testing remains positive long after eradication and is not useful for confirmation. Symptom improvement alone is unreliable, and testing during therapy would produce false negative results.
  74. The correct answer is C (Co-prescribing a proton pump inhibitor alongside continued nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug therapy): adding a proton pump inhibitor to ongoing NSAID therapy significantly reduces the risk of recurrent ulcer and bleeding. Switching to aspirin does not eliminate ulcer risk since aspirin also causes mucosal injury. An H2 blocker alone is less effective than a proton pump inhibitor for this indication. Increasing the NSAID dose or providing no additional therapy would increase, not decrease, ulcer risk.
  75. The correct answer is E (Small bowel biopsy via upper endoscopy performed while the patient still eats gluten): duodenal biopsy while the patient continues gluten intake is required to confirm the diagnosis of celiac disease after a positive serology. Starting a gluten-free diet before biopsy can cause false negative histology. Colonoscopy examines the colon, not the small bowel site of celiac injury. An empiric lactose-free trial or repeating serology later does not confirm the diagnosis.
  76. The correct answer is D (Hydrogen breath testing after ingestion of a substrate such as lactulose or glucose): hydrogen breath testing after ingestion of a substrate such as lactulose or glucose is a commonly used noninvasive test to support a diagnosis of small intestinal bacterial overgrowth. Tissue transglutaminase antibody evaluates celiac disease. Fecal calprotectin assesses intestinal inflammation. Anti-mitochondrial antibody relates to primary biliary cholangitis, and urea breath test is specific for Helicobacter pylori.
  77. The correct answer is C (Total parenteral nutrition with gradual enteral feeding advanced as tolerated over time): patients with short bowel syndrome typically require parenteral nutrition support initially, with cautious enteral feeding advanced as intestinal adaptation occurs. Immediate high-fiber solid food is poorly tolerated early on. Complete indefinite bowel rest delays adaptation. High-fat supplementation alone and strict fluid restriction do not address the overall nutritional needs of these patients.
  78. The correct answer is B (CT angiography of the mesenteric vessels to promptly evaluate for arterial occlusion): CT angiography is the preferred imaging modality to promptly evaluate the mesenteric vasculature in suspected acute mesenteric ischemia in a stable patient. Ultrasound and plain radiographs lack adequate sensitivity for vascular occlusion. Barium enema is contraindicated in suspected ischemia due to perforation risk, and a hepatobiliary scan evaluates biliary rather than vascular pathology.
  79. The correct answer is D (Corticosteroids for induction with a planned transition to a steroid-sparing maintenance agent): corticosteroids are standard induction therapy for moderate Crohn disease flares, with a plan to transition to immunomodulators or biologics for maintenance. Long-term opioids and loperamide do not treat the underlying inflammation. Antibiotics alone are not effective maintenance therapy, and bulk-forming laxatives are not appropriate for active inflammatory disease.
  80. The correct answer is A (Oral rehydration with supportive care, reserving antibiotics for severe or persistent illness): most cases of traveler's diarrhea are self-limited and managed supportively with oral rehydration, reserving antibiotics for severe, febrile, or persistent illness. Empiric colonoscopy and routine intravenous antibiotics for all patients are excessive for typical presentations. Loperamide as unconditional first-line therapy can be harmful if there is invasive infection, and fecal microbiota transplant has no role in acute traveler's diarrhea.
  81. The correct answer is C (Colonoscopy repeated once every ten years following a completely normal screening examination): after a normal colonoscopy in an average-risk patient, the standard surveillance interval is ten years, assuming no new symptoms or risk factors develop. Annual colonoscopy is unnecessarily frequent and not supported by guidelines. Stopping screening after five annual exams or a single lifetime colonoscopy does not follow accepted long-term screening recommendations.
  82. The correct answer is D (Prophylactic colectomy once polyp burden exceeds what endoscopic surveillance can manage safely): patients with familial adenomatous polyposis face a near-universal lifetime risk of colorectal cancer, so prophylactic colectomy is generally recommended once polyp burden becomes unmanageable by endoscopic surveillance. Fecal immunochemical testing and surveillance colonoscopy alone are inadequate given the extremely high cancer risk. Diet and aspirin chemoprevention alone do not sufficiently reduce this risk.
  83. The correct answer is E (Oral antibiotics with a clear liquid diet advanced gradually as symptoms improve): mild uncomplicated diverticulitis in a patient tolerating oral intake can typically be managed as an outpatient with oral antibiotics and dietary advancement. Immediate surgery and total parenteral nutrition are reserved for complicated or severe cases. Colonoscopy during an acute flare is avoided due to perforation risk, and starting a high-fiber diet during the acute flare is not appropriate until inflammation resolves.
  84. The correct answer is A (Topical or oral 5-aminosalicylate therapy, either alone or combined, for mild distal disease): mild distal ulcerative colitis is typically managed initially with 5-aminosalicylate therapy, either topical, oral, or combined. Systemic corticosteroids and biologics are generally reserved for more severe or refractory disease. Total colectomy is a treatment for severe, refractory, or complicated disease, not initial mild disease, and long-term antibiotics are not standard therapy for ulcerative colitis.
  85. The correct answer is B (A trial of a fiber supplement or osmotic laxative after dietary modification fails): after dietary modification fails in constipation-predominant irritable bowel syndrome, a fiber supplement or osmotic laxative is a reasonable next step. Colonic resection and long-term opioids are inappropriate for this functional disorder. Immediate colonoscopy is unnecessary without alarm features, and empiric antibiotics have no established role in this presentation.
  86. The correct answer is A (Topical nitroglycerin or calcium channel blocker ointment to relax the internal anal sphincter): topical agents that relax the internal anal sphincter, such as nitroglycerin or calcium channel blocker ointment, are appropriate next-line therapy after conservative measures fail for a chronic anal fissure. Immediate surgery is typically reserved for refractory cases. Oral opioids can worsen constipation and fissure symptoms. Systemic corticosteroids and colonoscopy do not address the local sphincter spasm causing the fissure.
  87. The correct answer is D (Oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin as current first-line therapy for confirmed toxin-positive disease): current guidelines recommend oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin as first-line therapy for Clostridioides difficile infection, replacing metronidazole as first-line in most cases. Intravenous metronidazole is no longer preferred first-line therapy. Loperamide can worsen toxin retention and is discouraged. Broad-spectrum antibiotics targeting gram-negative organisms do not treat this infection, and observation without treatment is inappropriate for confirmed toxin-positive disease.
  88. The correct answer is C (Ultrasound, with MRI when needed, to avoid ionizing radiation exposure to the fetus): ultrasound, and MRI when needed, are preferred initial imaging choices in pregnancy to evaluate suspected appendicitis while avoiding ionizing radiation exposure to the fetus. Plain radiographs have poor sensitivity for appendicitis. Non-contrast and contrast-enhanced CT involve radiation exposure that is best avoided when alternative imaging is available. Barium enema is not appropriate for evaluating suspected appendicitis.
  89. The correct answer is E (Cholecystectomy performed during the same hospital admission once the pancreatitis has improved): for gallstone pancreatitis without cholangitis, cholecystectomy during the index admission after clinical improvement reduces the risk of recurrent pancreatitis. Delaying indefinitely increases recurrence risk. Urgent surgical exploration within hours is not necessary without cholangitis or ongoing obstruction. Ursodeoxycholic acid does not substitute for definitive gallbladder removal in this setting, and omitting cholecystectomy entirely leaves the patient at high risk for recurrence.
  90. The correct answer is D (Pancreatic enzyme replacement therapy taken with meals to correct exocrine insufficiency): pancreatic enzyme replacement corrects the exocrine insufficiency causing steatorrhea and malabsorption in chronic pancreatitis. High-dose proton pump inhibitor alone does not replace missing digestive enzymes. Fat restriction without enzyme supplementation does not resolve the underlying malabsorption. Insulin is used for glycemic control when diabetes develops, not primarily for steatorrhea, and total parenteral nutrition is not first-line for this indication.
  91. The correct answer is B (Elective laparoscopic cholecystectomy as definitive treatment for symptomatic recurrent biliary colic): definitive treatment for symptomatic cholelithiasis with recurrent biliary colic is elective laparoscopic cholecystectomy. Dietary fat restriction alone does not prevent recurrent symptoms. Oral bile acid dissolution therapy is rarely used and has limited effectiveness compared with surgery. Observation with infrequent imaging and endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography without cholecystectomy do not address the underlying gallbladder disease.
  92. The correct answer is E (Urgent biliary decompression using endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography combined with antibiotics): acute cholangitis with sepsis and obstruction requires urgent biliary drainage, typically via endoscopic retrograde cholangiopancreatography, combined with antibiotics. Elective outpatient surgery and oral antibiotics alone are inadequate for this life-threatening presentation. Watchful waiting risks clinical deterioration, and percutaneous liver biopsy does not address the biliary obstruction or infection.
  93. The correct answer is E (Empiric antibiotic therapy for spontaneous bacterial peritonitis started promptly after diagnosis): an ascitic fluid neutrophil count above 250 cells per microliter is diagnostic of spontaneous bacterial peritonitis and warrants prompt empiric antibiotic therapy. Observation without treatment risks progression to sepsis. Repeating paracentesis in a month delays necessary treatment. Immediate transplant evaluation alone and diuretics without antibiotics do not treat the active infection.
  94. The correct answer is A (Long-standing Barrett esophagus progressing stepwise from metaplasia through dysplasia to carcinoma): esophageal adenocarcinoma at the gastroesophageal junction typically arises through a stepwise progression from chronic GERD to Barrett metaplasia to dysplasia to carcinoma. Acute infectious esophagitis and a single vomiting episode do not lead to malignant transformation. Untreated Helicobacter pylori gastritis is more associated with gastric, not esophageal, adenocarcinoma, and diffuse esophageal spasm is a motility disorder without malignant potential.
  95. The correct answer is A (Fibrous replacement of esophageal smooth muscle impairing normal motility and sphincter tone): scleroderma causes progressive fibrosis of esophageal smooth muscle, leading to absent peristalsis and a weak, incompetent lower esophageal sphincter, which explains both severe reflux and dysphagia. Isolated neuronal destruction does not fully capture the fibrotic muscular process of scleroderma. Chronic Candida infection, mechanical webs, and eosinophilic infiltration are distinct processes unrelated to this systemic autoimmune fibrosis.
  96. The correct answer is B (Vasoactive drug therapy such as octreotide, antibiotic prophylaxis, and urgent band ligation): acute variceal hemorrhage management combines resuscitation with vasoactive medication, prophylactic antibiotics to reduce infection and rebleeding risk, and urgent endoscopic band ligation. Surgical shunting is reserved for refractory bleeding. Oral iron and outpatient follow up are inappropriate for active hemorrhage. Proton pump inhibitor alone without endoscopy and watchful waiting fail to address the active bleeding source.
  97. The correct answer is C (Chronic acid injury producing fibrosis and progressive narrowing of the esophageal lumen): repeated acid exposure from poorly controlled reflux causes inflammation and subsequent fibrotic scarring that narrows the esophageal lumen, producing a peptic stricture. Acute eosinophilic infiltration and a single Mallory-Weiss tear do not produce chronic fibrotic strictures. Herpetic ulceration is an infectious process, and achalasia involves sphincter dysfunction rather than fibrotic luminal narrowing.
  98. The correct answer is E (Continuing dual antiplatelet therapy with a proton pump inhibitor after endoscopic hemostasis): after achieving endoscopic hemostasis, continuing necessary antiplatelet therapy with concurrent proton pump inhibitor protection balances cardiac stent thrombosis risk against rebleeding risk. Permanently stopping both agents risks stent thrombosis. Switching to full-dose NSAID therapy would worsen bleeding risk. Stopping only aspirin without gastroprotection and adding a second antiplatelet agent are both inappropriate in this bleeding scenario.
  99. The correct answer is D (Gastric adenocarcinoma and type one gastric carcinoid tumors from chronic hypergastrinemia): chronic atrophic gastritis with pernicious anemia increases long-term risk for gastric adenocarcinoma and type 1 gastric carcinoid tumors due to hypergastrinemia and mucosal changes. Esophageal varices, achalasia, Zenker diverticulum, and Mallory-Weiss tear are unrelated conditions with different mechanisms and are not recognized complications of atrophic gastritis.
  100. The correct answer is E (Ongoing inadvertent gluten exposure or incomplete adherence despite reported dietary compliance): persistent symptoms and elevated antibodies despite reported adherence are most often explained by inadvertent or hidden gluten exposure. Achalasia and Crohn disease are unrelated diagnoses that would not explain this specific serologic pattern. Concurrent Helicobacter pylori infection alone does not account for persistently elevated celiac-specific antibodies, and normal expected antibody persistence regardless of diet is incorrect since strict adherence should lower antibody titers over time.
  101. The correct answer is A (Impaired vitamin B12 absorption from terminal ileal Crohn disease affecting the absorption site): the terminal ileum is the specific site of vitamin B12 absorption, so Crohn disease affecting this region commonly leads to B12 deficiency and resulting megaloblastic anemia with neurologic symptoms. Excess gastric acid and loss of intrinsic factor relate to different mechanisms of B12 deficiency not tied to ileal Crohn disease. Folate malabsorption occurs more proximally, and chronic blood loss would cause iron deficiency rather than megaloblastic anemia.
  102. The correct answer is B (Chronic mesenteric ischemia from mesenteric artery atherosclerosis causing postprandial intestinal angina): postprandial pain, food avoidance, and weight loss over weeks in a patient with atherosclerosis describes classic chronic mesenteric ischemia, often called intestinal angina. Acute mesenteric ischemia presents suddenly and severely rather than gradually. Irritable bowel syndrome and gastroparesis do not typically cause this pattern of food-related pain with significant weight loss tied to vascular disease. Peptic ulcer disease does not typically cause this specific postprandial pain pattern with food fear.
  103. The correct answer is E (Toxic megacolon, a life-threatening emergency defined by fever and marked colonic dilation): fever, abdominal distension, and colonic dilation greater than 6 centimeters in a patient with ulcerative colitis defines toxic megacolon, a life-threatening emergency. This is not an uncomplicated flare manageable with oral therapy alone. Irritable bowel syndrome overlap and functional constipation do not cause fever and colonic dilation. Simple diverticulitis is a distinct condition unrelated to this presentation in a colitis patient.
  104. The correct answer is E (Microsatellite instability with early age of onset and a strong family cancer history): Lynch syndrome is characterized by microsatellite instability, early age of cancer onset, right-sided predominance, and a strong family history, distinguishing it from sporadic colorectal cancer. Older age with left-sided tumors is more typical of sporadic cancer. Hundreds of polyps suggest familial adenomatous polyposis instead. Isolated hamartomatous polyps suggest a different hereditary polyposis syndrome, and chronic NSAID use is not a defining feature of Lynch syndrome.
  105. The correct answer is C (Chronic diverticular inflammation eroding through the bowel wall into the adjacent bladder): a colovesical fistula with pneumaturia and recurrent urinary infections indicates that chronic diverticular inflammation has eroded through the bowel wall to communicate with the bladder. This is not simple uncomplicated diverticulitis, which would not create a fistula. Isolated bacterial cystitis would not explain pneumaturia. Hemorrhoidal disease and colonic polyps do not cause fistula formation to the bladder.
  106. The correct answer is A (Development of combined exocrine and endocrine pancreatic insufficiency from progressive glandular fibrosis): chronic alcohol-induced pancreatitis with calcifications and ductal changes over years typically leads to progressive loss of both exocrine function, causing steatorrhea, and endocrine function, causing diabetes. Function does not fully reverse with alcohol cessation once significant fibrosis has occurred. Isolated hyperinsulinemia without exocrine dysfunction and spontaneous ductal regeneration are not expected outcomes, and progression is not guaranteed to present only as further acute attacks.
  107. The correct answer is C (Zollinger-Ellison syndrome confirmed by a markedly elevated fasting serum gastrin level): recurrent refractory peptic ulcers with diarrhea and a pancreatic neuroendocrine tumor point to Zollinger-Ellison syndrome, confirmed by markedly elevated fasting serum gastrin. Insulinoma presents with hypoglycemia, not ulcers. VIPoma causes profound watery diarrhea with electrolyte disturbances but not refractory ulcers as the defining feature. Glucagonoma and somatostatinoma have distinct presentations involving skin rash or gallstones and diabetes rather than refractory peptic ulcer disease.
  108. The correct answer is E (Hepatorenal syndrome with progressive renal failure unresponsive to adequate volume expansion): progressive renal failure with a bland urine sediment unresponsive to volume expansion in a cirrhotic patient, with no other cause identified, is characteristic of hepatorenal syndrome. Acute tubular necrosis typically shows an abnormal urine sediment with casts. Prerenal azotemia would improve with fluid administration. Obstructive and postrenal causes would show hydronephrosis or an identifiable structural cause on imaging, which is absent here.
  109. The correct answer is E (Autoimmune hepatitis defined by elevated IgG, anti-smooth muscle antibody, and interface hepatitis): elevated IgG, positive anti-smooth muscle antibody, and interface hepatitis on biopsy together define autoimmune hepatitis. Primary biliary cholangitis is associated with anti-mitochondrial antibody rather than anti-smooth muscle antibody. Wilson disease and hemochromatosis are metabolic liver diseases with distinct laboratory markers unrelated to these autoimmune findings. Nonalcoholic fatty liver disease does not typically show this autoimmune serologic and histologic pattern.
  110. The correct answer is A (Primary biliary cholangitis presenting with pruritus, fatigue, and a positive anti-mitochondrial antibody): pruritus, fatigue, elevated alkaline phosphatase, and a positive anti-mitochondrial antibody in a middle-aged woman is the classic presentation of primary biliary cholangitis. Autoimmune hepatitis is associated with different antibodies such as anti-smooth muscle antibody. Wilson disease and Budd-Chiari syndrome have distinct clinical and laboratory features unrelated to anti-mitochondrial antibody. Alcoholic hepatitis presents with a history of heavy alcohol use and different laboratory findings such as an elevated AST to ALT ratio.
  111. The correct answer is B (Wilson disease from impaired copper excretion causing tremor, dysarthria, and corneal rings): tremor, dysarthria, liver dysfunction, and a Kayser-Fleischer ring at the corneal limbus together are classic for Wilson disease, caused by impaired biliary copper excretion and copper accumulation. Hemochromatosis causes iron overload with different systemic findings such as bronze skin and diabetes. Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency primarily affects the liver and lungs without these neurologic and ocular findings. Primary sclerosing cholangitis and autoimmune hepatitis do not produce this specific neurologic and corneal presentation.
  112. The correct answer is E (Hereditary hemochromatosis confirmed genetically by HFE gene mutation testing for iron overload): bronze skin, new diabetes, and elevated iron indices strongly suggest hereditary hemochromatosis, confirmed genetically by HFE gene mutation testing. Wilson disease presents with low, not high, iron studies and different features. Alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency and primary biliary cholangitis have distinct diagnostic markers unrelated to iron overload. Gilbert syndrome causes isolated mild unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia without these systemic iron-related findings.
  113. The correct answer is D (Budd-Chiari syndrome from hepatic vein thrombosis in a patient with hypercoagulability): sudden abdominal pain, ascites, hepatomegaly, and hepatic vein thrombosis in a patient with a hypercoagulable disorder define Budd-Chiari syndrome. Portal vein thrombosis alone would not show hepatic vein occlusion specifically. Cirrhosis from viral hepatitis develops gradually rather than presenting acutely with vein thrombosis. Acute cholecystitis and hepatorenal syndrome have different presentations unrelated to hepatic venous outflow obstruction.
  114. The correct answer is C (Nonalcoholic steatohepatitis with fibrosis progressing toward significant long-term cirrhosis risk): hepatic steatosis with inflammation and fibrosis on biopsy in an obese diabetic patient without significant alcohol use defines nonalcoholic steatohepatitis, which can progress to cirrhosis. Alcoholic hepatitis requires significant alcohol exposure, which is absent here. Autoimmune hepatitis and Wilson disease have distinct serologic and metabolic markers not described in this case. Acute viral hepatitis A presents acutely rather than with chronic steatohepatitis and fibrosis.
  115. The correct answer is D (Alcoholic hepatitis presenting with jaundice, fever, tender hepatomegaly, and an elevated AST ratio): jaundice, fever, tender hepatomegaly, and an AST to ALT ratio greater than 2 in a heavy alcohol user is the classic presentation of alcoholic hepatitis. Hepatitis A typically presents with a different exposure history and different enzyme pattern. Wilson disease and primary sclerosing cholangitis have distinct clinical pictures unrelated to acute alcohol-related liver injury. Gilbert syndrome causes mild isolated bilirubin elevation without fever or tender hepatomegaly.
  116. The correct answer is B (Worsening encephalopathy together with severe coagulopathy and a rising serum lactate level): progressive encephalopathy, severe coagulopathy, and rising lactate indicate rapidly deteriorating liver function and are key indications for urgent transplant evaluation in fulminant liver failure. Mild transaminase elevation with normal mental status suggests a much less severe course. Isolated bilirubin elevation with normal INR, stable improving enzymes, and normal ammonia with clear mentation all suggest a more reassuring clinical trajectory not requiring emergent transplant evaluation.
  117. The correct answer is A (Gilbert syndrome with mild intermittent jaundice triggered by fasting and isolated bilirubin elevation): mild intermittent jaundice triggered by fasting or illness, with isolated unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia and otherwise normal liver enzymes and imaging, is the classic benign presentation of Gilbert syndrome. Hemolytic anemia would typically show anemia and elevated reticulocyte count, not present here. Acute viral hepatitis would show elevated transaminases. Choledocholithiasis would show conjugated hyperbilirubinemia with duct dilation, and autoimmune hepatitis would show abnormal liver enzymes and autoantibodies.
  118. The correct answer is D (Ultrasound with or without alpha-fetoprotein performed every six months for tumor surveillance): hepatocellular carcinoma surveillance in cirrhotic patients is performed with liver ultrasound, often combined with alpha-fetoprotein, every six months. Annual colonoscopy screens for colorectal cancer, not liver cancer. Serum ammonia monitoring assesses encephalopathy risk, not tumor surveillance. CT chest and upper endoscopy serve other purposes and are not the standard surveillance tools for hepatocellular carcinoma.
  119. The correct answer is E (A presumed variceal bleed still requiring endoscopic evaluation since a nonvariceal source may exist): even in cirrhotic patients with known varices, endoscopy is essential because a substantial proportion of upper GI bleeding in this population arises from nonvariceal sources such as peptic ulcers. Assuming the source without endoscopy risks missing the actual bleeding lesion and undertreating it. Skipping endoscopy entirely is unsafe, and colonoscopy would not identify an upper GI bleeding source such as a gastric ulcer.
  120. The correct answer is B (The colonic diverticula considered the presumed bleeding source given the clinical context and absent other lesion): in a patient with painless hematochezia, anticoagulation, and diverticulosis identified without another bleeding lesion found on colonoscopy, diverticular bleeding is the presumed source based on the clinical picture. Assuming an unevaluated esophageal source is not supported since colonoscopy was already performed and no upper GI symptoms are described. Anticoagulation is clinically relevant to bleeding risk, not unrelated, and ongoing monitoring is appropriate rather than no further follow up at all.
  121. The correct answer is B (Aplastic anemia): pancytopenia with a hypocellular, fatty marrow and no infiltrate or fibrosis defines aplastic anemia, caused by loss of hematopoietic stem cells. MDS and AML would show dysplasia or blasts in the marrow rather than empty fatty spaces. CLL would show lymphocytic infiltration, and myelofibrosis would show marrow fibrosis, not fatty replacement.
  122. The correct answer is C (Decreased erythropoietin production.): failing kidneys produce less erythropoietin, leading to a normocytic to mildly microcytic anemia with a blunted reticulocyte response and normal iron stores. Iron deficiency would show low ferritin and low iron, not normal studies. B12 malabsorption, hemolysis, and marrow fibrosis do not fit the renal failure context or normal iron panel.
  123. The correct answer is A (Low serum ferritin): ferritin reflects total body iron stores and is low specifically in true iron deficiency, while it can be normal or elevated in anemia of chronic disease due to inflammation. CRP reflects inflammation, not iron status. RDW, low reticulocyte count, and microcytosis occur in both iron deficiency and anemia of chronic disease, so they lack specificity.
  124. The correct answer is C (Impaired heme synthesis): ringed sideroblasts form when mitochondria cannot incorporate iron into protoporphyrin to make heme, often from isoniazid interfering with vitamin B6-dependent enzymes. Impaired globin synthesis describes thalassemia, not sideroblastic anemia. Iron absorption and B12 levels are not the primary defect, and there is no autoimmune component here.
  125. The correct answer is E (Elevated methylmalonic acid level): methylmalonic acid rises specifically in B12 deficiency because B12 is a required cofactor for its metabolism, while folate deficiency does not raise it. Homocysteine rises in both deficiencies, so it cannot distinguish them. Macro-ovalocytes, low reticulocyte count, and elevated LDH occur in any megaloblastic process regardless of cause.
  126. The correct answer is B (Inhibition of heme synthesis enzymes): lead inhibits ALA dehydratase and ferrochelatase in the heme synthesis pathway, causing microcytic anemia with basophilic stippling plus systemic toxicity like neuropathy and abdominal pain. It does not impair B12 absorption, cause autoimmune hemolysis, cause marrow fibrosis, or cause copper deficiency.
  127. The correct answer is A (Reduced ability to neutralize oxidative stress): G6PD deficiency impairs the pentose phosphate pathway's production of NADPH, leaving red cells vulnerable to oxidative damage from drugs like dapsone, causing Heinz bodies and bite cells. Autoantibody destruction, mechanical fragmentation, complement-mediated hemolysis, and membrane spectrin defects describe other hemolytic anemias, not G6PD deficiency.
  128. The correct answer is D (Warm-reactive IgG antibody): warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia, often seen with lupus, is mediated by IgG antibodies that bind red cells at body temperature and cause extravascular destruction producing spherocytes. Cold agglutinin disease involves IgM at cooler temperatures, and anti-platelet, anti-HLA, and ANCA antibodies target different cells or are unrelated to hemolysis.
  129. The correct answer is C (Schistocytes on peripheral smear): fragmented red cells reflect the mechanical shearing of cells passing through microthrombi, which is the hallmark of microangiopathic hemolysis in conditions like TTP. A positive direct antiglobulin test points to immune hemolysis instead, spherocytes suggest autoimmune or hereditary causes, and elevated haptoglobin or normal LDH would be inconsistent with any active hemolysis.
  130. The correct answer is E (Beta thalassemia trait): mild microcytic anemia with a normal iron panel, elevated red cell count, and elevated hemoglobin A2 on electrophoresis is classic for beta thalassemia trait. Iron deficiency would show low iron studies, alpha thalassemia trait does not raise hemoglobin A2, and sideroblastic anemia or anemia of chronic disease do not present with this electrophoresis pattern.
  131. The correct answer is A (Elevated reticulocyte count with baseline hemolysis markers): sickle cell patients have chronic hemolysis, so an active vaso-occlusive crisis typically shows ongoing reticulocytosis and elevated bilirubin and LDH at baseline, distinguishing it from an aplastic event. Isolated leukopenia, normal LDH and bilirubin, decreased bilirubin, or absent reticulocytosis would be atypical or suggest a different complication like aplastic crisis.
  132. The correct answer is B (Acute myeloid leukemia): Auer rods are eosinophilic needle-like inclusions found specifically in myeloblasts and are diagnostic of AML. CML presents with a spectrum of maturing granulocytes rather than blasts with Auer rods, CLL and hairy cell leukemia involve mature lymphoid cells, and MDS shows dysplasia without a predominance of Auer rod-containing blasts.
  133. The correct answer is E (t(9;22) Philadelphia chromosome): marked leukocytosis with a full spectrum of granulocyte maturation and splenomegaly is classic for chronic myeloid leukemia, driven by the BCR-ABL fusion from the Philadelphia chromosome. The other translocations and trisomy 12 are associated with acute promyelocytic leukemia, myelodysplastic syndrome, Burkitt lymphoma, and CLL respectively, not CML.
  134. The correct answer is C (Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis): the combination of asthma, sinus disease, marked eosinophilia, and mononeuritis multiplex with purpura is characteristic of this vasculitis, formerly called Churg-Strauss syndrome. Leukemias would show additional blood count abnormalities or blasts, Loeffler endocarditis presents with cardiac findings, and hypereosinophilic syndrome from parasites lacks the vasculitic organ involvement described.
  135. The correct answer is D (Autoantibody-mediated platelet destruction): isolated thrombocytopenia after a viral illness in a child with an otherwise normal exam is classic for immune thrombocytopenic purpura, caused by antibodies against platelet antigens. Marrow infiltration, microangiopathic consumption, heparin antibodies, and vitamin K deficiency do not fit this age group or clinical scenario.
  136. The correct answer is B (Antibody against platelet factor 4-heparin complex): heparin-induced thrombocytopenia results from IgG antibodies against PF4-heparin complexes that activate platelets and cause paradoxical thrombosis despite a falling platelet count. Antibodies to glycoprotein IIb/IIIa cause a different immune thrombocytopenia, and the other options describe unrelated mechanisms of thrombocytopenia.
  137. The correct answer is D (JAK2 V617F mutation): essential thrombocythemia is most commonly driven by the JAK2 V617F mutation, found in roughly half of cases, causing autonomous megakaryocyte proliferation. BCR-ABL defines CML, FLT3-ITD and t(15;17) are associated with subtypes of AML, and PML-RARA specifically defines acute promyelocytic leukemia, none of which characterize essential thrombocythemia.
  138. The correct answer is A (Factor VIII deficiency): hemophilia A causes an isolated prolonged aPTT with hemarthroses and a normal PT and platelet count, since it affects the intrinsic pathway without involving the extrinsic pathway or platelets. Factor VII deficiency prolongs the PT, isolated von Willebrand deficiency more often causes mucocutaneous bleeding, and factor II or XIII deficiencies present differently.
  139. The correct answer is E (Anticardiolipin antibody): recurrent thrombosis and pregnancy loss with a prolonged aPTT that does not correct on mixing study suggests a lupus anticoagulant, and testing for anticardiolipin and anti-beta2 glycoprotein antibodies confirms antiphospholipid syndrome. The other antibodies are associated with lupus, scleroderma, rheumatoid arthritis, or myositis, not this coagulation picture.
  140. The correct answer is C (Acute intermittent porphyria): abdominal pain, neuropsychiatric symptoms, and dark urine triggered by a new medication with elevated urinary porphobilinogen is classic for acute intermittent porphyria. Lead poisoning and sideroblastic anemia cause hematologic rather than acute neurovisceral symptoms, hereditary spherocytosis causes hemolysis, and porphyria cutanea tarda presents with skin blistering, not acute attacks.
  141. The correct answer is A (Presence of a JAK2 mutation): a JAK2 mutation strongly supports primary polycythemia vera, since secondary erythrocytosis from hypoxia or other causes does not carry this mutation. An elevated erythropoietin level and chronic hypoxemia point toward secondary erythrocytosis instead, and normal platelet and white counts or male sex are nonspecific findings that do not distinguish the two conditions.
  142. The correct answer is C (Primary myelofibrosis): massive splenomegaly, teardrop cells, leukoerythroblastic smear findings, and marrow fibrosis together define primary myelofibrosis. CML lacks fibrosis and teardrop cells as prominent features, essential thrombocythemia and polycythemia vera have distinct blood counts without this degree of fibrosis, and MDS marrow shows dysplasia rather than extensive fibrosis.
  143. The correct answer is B (Myelodysplastic syndrome): cytopenias with a hypercellular but dysplastic marrow and fewer than 20 percent blasts defines MDS, a clonal disorder of ineffective hematopoiesis. AML requires 20 percent or more blasts, aplastic anemia shows a hypocellular marrow, CML shows granulocytic proliferation, and multiple myeloma shows plasma cell infiltration, none of which match this description.
  144. The correct answer is E (Acute hemolytic transfusion reaction): rapid onset fever, flank pain, hemoglobinuria, and a positive direct antiglobulin test during transfusion indicate ABO incompatibility causing intravascular hemolysis. Febrile nonhemolytic reactions do not cause hemolysis or a positive Coombs test, TRALI causes respiratory distress, circulatory overload causes volume symptoms, and delayed hemolytic reactions occur days later.
  145. The correct answer is B (Aplastic crisis treated with red blood cell transfusion.): parvovirus B19 suppresses erythroid precursors, causing a sudden drop in hemoglobin with reticulocytopenia in sickle cell patients, requiring transfusion support until marrow recovery. Splenic sequestration and hyperhemolysis would show reticulocytosis rather than reticulocytopenia, and vaso-occlusive crisis or chest syndrome do not explain the profound anemia with an absent reticulocyte response.
  146. The correct answer is C (Colonoscopy and upper endoscopy): new iron deficiency in an older man without an obvious bleeding source requires evaluation for occult gastrointestinal blood loss, most importantly to exclude malignancy. Bone marrow biopsy, hemoglobin electrophoresis, serum protein electrophoresis, and Coombs testing do not address the most likely and dangerous underlying cause of iron loss in this population.
  147. The correct answer is A (Replace vitamin B12 first to avoid worsening neurologic injury): giving folate before correcting B12 deficiency can improve the anemia while allowing neurologic damage from B12 deficiency to progress or worsen, so B12 must be addressed first or concurrently with priority. Replacing folate first or giving folate alone risks masking hematologic correction while neurologic injury continues, and iron is not the priority here.
  148. The correct answer is D (Bone marrow aspirate with cytogenetics): after excluding nutritional and thyroid causes, dysplastic cells on smear warrant a bone marrow biopsy with cytogenetic analysis to confirm myelodysplastic syndrome and assess risk. Erythropoietin level, hemoglobin electrophoresis, Coombs test, and protein electrophoresis do not establish a diagnosis of MDS or its cytogenetic risk category.
  149. The correct answer is B (Corticosteroids with or without IVIG): first-line therapy for ITP with significant bleeding and very low platelet counts is corticosteroids, often combined with IVIG for a faster response. Platelet transfusion alone is largely ineffective since transfused platelets are also destroyed, and splenectomy, rituximab, and thrombopoietin agonists are reserved for refractory or relapsed disease, not first-line use.
  150. The correct answer is E (Stop all heparin and start a direct thrombin inhibitor): confirmed HIT requires immediate discontinuation of all heparin products and anticoagulation with a non-heparin agent such as argatroban to prevent thrombosis. Low molecular weight heparin cross-reacts with HIT antibodies, platelet transfusion can worsen thrombosis risk, and warfarin alone without a bridging agent risks skin necrosis from transient hypercoagulability.
  151. The correct answer is D (Roughly equal ratios of red cells, plasma, and platelets): balanced ratio transfusion, often described as 1:1:1, reduces coagulopathy and improves survival in massive transfusion for trauma. Giving red cells alone or in a ten to one ratio to plasma worsens dilutional coagulopathy, and using only cryoprecipitate or only platelets fails to address the full range of needs in hemorrhagic shock.
  152. The correct answer is B (Hemoglobin electrophoresis): electrophoresis identifies the abnormal hemoglobin fractions, such as elevated hemoglobin A2, that confirm thalassemia trait and distinguish it from iron deficiency, which has normal electrophoresis. Transferrin saturation and reticulocyte count do not differentiate the two conditions, peripheral smear findings overlap between them, and B12 level is unrelated to microcytic anemia evaluation.
  153. The correct answer is C (Testing should be deferred until after anticoagulation is discontinued): acute thrombosis and anticoagulants like heparin and warfarin can transiently lower protein C, protein S, and antithrombin levels, causing false positive results if tested too early. Testing during the acute event or while on warfarin risks inaccurate results, and skipping testing entirely would miss a true underlying thrombophilia in appropriate patients.
  154. The correct answer is A (Skeletal survey or whole-body imaging for lytic lesions): identifying bone lesions is part of the CRAB criteria used to confirm end-organ damage in multiple myeloma and distinguish it from smoldering disease or MGUS. Direct antiglobulin test, hemoglobin electrophoresis, JAK2 testing, and flow cytometry for blasts are not part of the standard myeloma diagnostic workup for bone involvement.
  155. The correct answer is E (Excisional lymph node biopsy): a full excisional biopsy preserves lymph node architecture, which is essential for accurately subtyping lymphoma and guiding treatment. Bone marrow aspirate alone, CBC, isolated LDH, or PET-CT provide staging or supportive information but cannot establish the histologic diagnosis and subtype needed to confirm lymphoma.
  156. The correct answer is D (CD15 and CD30 positivity): Reed-Sternberg cells in classic Hodgkin lymphoma characteristically express CD15 and CD30 on immunohistochemistry. CD20 alone is more typical of B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphomas, TdT marks lymphoblastic lymphoma, cyclin D1 marks mantle cell lymphoma, and myeloperoxidase marks myeloid malignancies, none of which fit classic Hodgkin lymphoma.
  157. The correct answer is C (Mycobacterial and fungal cultures of the marrow): granulomatous, hypocellular marrow in advanced HIV strongly suggests disseminated mycobacterial or fungal infection such as Mycobacterium avium complex or histoplasmosis, which requires targeted marrow cultures. CMV, toxoplasma, EBV, and hepatitis C testing do not typically explain granulomatous marrow infiltration in this setting.
  158. The correct answer is B (Chronic GVHD treated with systemic corticosteroids): dry eyes, skin thickening, and diarrhea occurring months after transplant reflect chronic graft versus host disease, and systemic corticosteroids remain the standard initial treatment. Acute GVHD occurs earlier and is treated differently, veno-occlusive disease presents with hepatic symptoms, engraftment syndrome occurs at the time of neutrophil recovery, and relapse would show blasts rather than these features.
  159. The correct answer is A (Therapeutic phlebotomy to a target hematocrit below 45 percent): phlebotomy reduces red cell mass and thrombotic risk and is the foundation of initial polycythemia vera management. Cytoreductive chemotherapy is reserved for high-risk patients, splenectomy and transplantation are not first-line, and warfarin alone does not address the underlying erythrocytosis driving thrombotic risk.
  160. The correct answer is E (Low-dose aspirin): low-dose aspirin reduces thrombotic risk in essential thrombocythemia patients without contraindications and is standard even in lower-risk patients. Warfarin and hydroxyurea are reserved for higher-risk disease or specific indications, prophylactic platelet transfusion is not indicated and can worsen thrombosis risk, and corticosteroids have no role in this condition.
  161. The correct answer is C (Factor VIII concentrate infusion): prompt factor replacement is the priority in any suspected bleed in severe hemophilia A, since delaying treatment for imaging increases the risk of joint damage. Fresh frozen plasma provides insufficient factor concentration, desmopressin is used for mild hemophilia A or von Willebrand disease, and vitamin K or platelet transfusion do not address a factor VIII deficiency.
  162. The correct answer is E (Warfarin): warfarin remains the preferred long-term anticoagulant in antiphospholipid syndrome because direct oral anticoagulants have shown higher rates of recurrent thrombosis, particularly arterial events, in this population. Apixaban, rivaroxaban, and dabigatran are direct oral agents with inferior outcomes in this setting, and aspirin alone is insufficient for secondary prevention after venous thromboembolism.
  163. The correct answer is D (Corrected count is 4.2 percent, indicating an appropriate marrow response): the corrected reticulocyte count equals the reticulocyte percentage multiplied by the patient hematocrit divided by the normal hematocrit, or 9 times 21 divided by 45, which equals 4.2 percent, a value above the 2 to 3 percent threshold indicating adequate marrow compensation for hemolysis or blood loss. The other calculations either use the wrong math or mischaracterize an adequate response as inadequate.
  164. The correct answer is A (Hemolytic uremic syndrome confirmed by a negative Coombs test with schistocytes): the triad of hemolysis, thrombocytopenia, and renal failure after diarrheal illness with a negative Coombs test confirms a Shiga toxin-mediated microangiopathy rather than immune-mediated hemolysis. Warm and cold autoimmune hemolytic anemias would show a positive Coombs test, and PNH or sickle cell disease do not fit this clinical trigger and lab pattern.
  165. The correct answer is B (Low leukocyte alkaline phosphatase score in CML versus a high score in a reaction): LAP score is classically low in CML due to the abnormal leukemic clone and high in reactive leukocytosis from infection, making it a useful discriminator. Elevated white count and left shift occur in both, basophilia is more typical of CML rather than a leukemoid reaction, and splenomegaly or blasts can occur in either or neither depending on severity.
  166. The correct answer is E (Absence of end-organ damage meeting CRAB criteria): MGUS is defined by a monoclonal protein below myeloma thresholds without hypercalcemia, renal insufficiency, anemia, or bone lesions, which this patient lacks. A monoclonal spike and age over 65 occur in both conditions, marrow plasma cells at 8 percent are below the 10 percent threshold used for myeloma but do not by themselves confirm MGUS, and family history is not diagnostic.
  167. The correct answer is D (Transient hypercoagulable state from rapid protein C depletion): protein C has a short half-life and falls faster than the pro-coagulant factors when warfarin is started, creating a brief hypercoagulable window that causes microthrombosis and skin necrosis in susceptible patients, especially without bridging heparin. This is not a direct toxic, thrombocytopenic, antibody-mediated, or allergic process.
  168. The correct answer is C (Markedly prolonged prothrombin time and low fibrinogen): DIC consumes clotting factors and fibrinogen broadly, producing prolonged PT and low fibrinogen, whereas TTP typically spares the coagulation cascade and fibrinogen remains normal. Reduced ADAMTS13 activity is specific to TTP, and normal PT or isolated renal failure without bleeding do not favor DIC over TTP.
  169. The correct answer is B (Transfusional iron overload monitored with serial ferritin and organ iron studies): repeated transfusions without adequate chelation lead to iron deposition in the skin, pancreas, and heart, producing this classic triad, and monitoring ferritin along with cardiac and hepatic iron imaging helps guide chelation therapy. GVHD, hemolysis, folate deficiency, and alloimmunization do not produce this specific combination of hyperpigmentation, diabetes, and cardiomyopathy.
  170. The correct answer is A (Immune-mediated platelet destruction similar to ITP): HIV can trigger an ITP-like process with antibody-mediated platelet destruction, and increased megakaryocytes reflect a compensatory marrow response rather than aplasia. Direct marrow aplasia would show reduced megakaryocytes, microangiopathic consumption would show schistocytes, drug-induced suppression requires an offending agent, and splenic sequestration requires portal hypertension, none of which are described.
  171. The correct answer is E (Primary myelofibrosis): massive splenomegaly, teardrop cells, and a dry tap from marrow fibrosis in the absence of the Philadelphia chromosome are classic for primary myelofibrosis. CML is excluded by the negative Philadelphia chromosome testing, AML would show blasts rather than a dry tap from fibrosis, and essential thrombocythemia or CLL do not typically cause a dry marrow aspirate.
  172. The correct answer is A (Transfusion-related acute lung injury): acute noncardiogenic pulmonary edema with hypoxemia and hypotension within six hours of transfusion, without volume overload or elevated BNP, defines TRALI, caused by donor antibodies activating recipient neutrophils in the lungs. TACO is distinguished by volume overload and elevated BNP, and febrile, delayed hemolytic, and anaphylactic reactions present with different clinical features.
  173. The correct answer is D (Negative direct antiglobulin test with enzyme assay confirmation): a negative Coombs test argues against antibody-mediated hemolysis, and a confirmatory G6PD enzyme assay establishes the diagnosis, though testing may need to be delayed until after the acute hemolytic episode resolves. Elevated bilirubin, reticulocyte count, LDH, and low haptoglobin are nonspecific markers of hemolysis present in both autoimmune and enzymatic causes.
  174. The correct answer is C (Catastrophic antiphospholipid syndrome treated with anticoagulation plus immunosuppression): rapid multi-organ microthrombosis in a patient with known antiphospholipid syndrome defines the catastrophic variant, which requires aggressive combined anticoagulation and immunosuppressive therapy, often with plasma exchange. DIC, a simple lupus flare, HIT, and isolated TTP do not account for the combination of known antiphospholipid syndrome with widespread biopsy-confirmed microthrombi.
  175. The correct answer is B (Hemoglobin electrophoresis): an elevated red cell count with a normal RDW and normal iron studies is suggestive of thalassemia trait, and electrophoresis confirms the diagnosis by identifying abnormal hemoglobin fractions. Ferritin and total iron binding capacity are already normal and do not add diagnostic value, reticulocyte count is nonspecific, and basophilic stippling alone is not diagnostic of thalassemia.
  176. The correct answer is D (Autoimmune hemolytic anemia complicating CLL): CLL is well known to trigger warm autoimmune hemolytic anemia through dysregulated B-cell clones producing autoantibodies, presenting acutely with hemolysis markers and a positive Coombs test. Richter transformation would show aggressive lymphadenopathy and systemic symptoms rather than isolated hemolysis, marrow infiltration would cause pancytopenia rather than reticulocytosis, and the other options do not explain the positive Coombs test.
  177. The correct answer is E (Transformation to acute myeloid leukemia with worse prognosis): a rise in marrow blasts to 20 percent or more in a patient with prior MDS defines transformation to AML, which carries a significantly worse prognosis than MDS alone. Persistent MDS, aplastic anemia, reactive changes, and myelofibrosis do not involve this blast threshold or the same prognostic implications.
  178. The correct answer is A (Elevated rather than suppressed erythropoietin level): polycythemia vera is characterized by a low or suppressed erythropoietin level due to autonomous red cell production, while secondary erythrocytosis from chronic hypoxia, as in COPD, causes an appropriately elevated erythropoietin level. Elevated hemoglobin, tobacco use, normal platelet count, and male sex do not by themselves distinguish primary from secondary erythrocytosis.
  179. The correct answer is C (Markedly decreased factor VIII activity): factor VIII is produced by vascular endothelium rather than the liver, so it remains normal or elevated in liver disease coagulopathy but becomes markedly reduced when consumed in DIC, making it a useful discriminator. Prolonged PT, thrombocytopenia, elevated INR, and low albumin can all occur in liver disease alone and do not distinguish it from DIC.
  180. The correct answer is B (Eosinophil-mediated toxic damage to the endocardium): sustained hypereosinophilia releases toxic granule proteins that damage the endocardium, leading to thrombosis and fibrosis characteristic of Loeffler endocarditis with restrictive cardiomyopathy. Viral myocarditis, autoimmune pericarditis, amyloid deposition, and iron overload describe distinct mechanisms unrelated to prolonged eosinophilia.
  181. The correct answer is B (Staphylococcus aureus including community acquired methicillin resistant strains): Staphylococcus aureus, including community acquired MRSA, is the leading cause of purulent skin and soft tissue infections with abscess formation. Streptococcus pyogenes more classically causes nonpurulent spreading cellulitis without abscess. Pseudomonas is linked to hot tub folliculitis or puncture wounds through sneakers. Bacteroides and Clostridium are anaerobes tied to bite wounds or necrotizing infections, not typical cellulitis.
  182. The correct answer is A (Markedly elevated protein together with a low glucose concentration): Bacterial meningitis classically shows high protein, low glucose relative to serum, and neutrophilic pleocytosis with elevated opening pressure. Viral meningitis typically shows lymphocyte predominance and normal glucose. Clear fluid and normal pressure favor a viral or aseptic process rather than bacterial infection.
  183. The correct answer is E (Streptococcus milleri group organisms often mixed with anaerobic bacteria): Streptococcus milleri group organisms, often mixed with anaerobes, are the most common isolates in brain abscesses from contiguous spread of ear or sinus infections. Cryptococcus and Toxoplasma cause abscess-like lesions mainly in immunocompromised hosts. Naegleria causes rapidly fatal amebic encephalitis from freshwater exposure. Coxiella causes Q fever, not brain abscess.
  184. The correct answer is C (Temporal lobes with extension into the inferior frontal lobes): HSV encephalitis has a strong predilection for the temporal and inferior frontal lobes, producing hemorrhagic necrosis on imaging. Cerebellar or occipital predominant patterns are unusual for HSV. Symmetric basal ganglia lesions suggest other processes such as toxic or metabolic encephalopathy, and spinal cord involvement points toward myelitis rather than HSV encephalitis.
  185. The correct answer is D (Spinal epidural abscess causing compression of the thecal sac): Fever, back pain, and progressive neurologic deficit after a spinal procedure is the classic triad suggesting spinal epidural abscess, a neurosurgical emergency requiring urgent MRI. The other listed conditions do not explain new focal neurologic deficits following epidural instrumentation. Delay in diagnosis of epidural abscess can lead to permanent paralysis.
  186. The correct answer is E (Contrast enhanced computed tomography of the sinuses and orbits): Contrast enhanced CT provides detailed bony and soft tissue detail needed to detect orbital cellulitis, abscess, or intracranial spread from sinusitis. Plain radiographs lack sensitivity for these complications. The remaining imaging modalities are not directed at the sinus or orbital region and would not identify these complications.
  187. The correct answer is D (Rapid antigen detection test with backup throat culture if negative): A rapid strep antigen test is highly specific, and a negative result in adults is often followed by culture confirmation given lower sensitivity. Antistreptolysin O titers reflect past exposure, not acute infection, and rise later in illness. Blood cultures and chest films are not useful for pharyngitis, and the Monospot test evaluates for infectious mononucleosis instead.
  188. The correct answer is B (Haemophilus influenzae type b prior to widespread conjugate vaccination): Epiglottitis with drooling, stridor, and tripod positioning was classically caused by Haemophilus influenzae type b before the Hib vaccine dramatically reduced its incidence. Parainfluenza causes croup with a barky cough rather than epiglottitis. RSV, pertussis, and Mycoplasma cause other respiratory syndromes without this fulminant airway presentation.
  189. The correct answer is A (Pseudomonas aeruginosa invading the skull base and temporal bone): Malignant otitis externa is a skull base osteomyelitis almost always caused by Pseudomonas aeruginosa, seen predominantly in older diabetic patients. Candida can cause fungal otitis externa but rarely the invasive form. Streptococcus pneumoniae and Klebsiella are more typical of middle ear or respiratory infections rather than this specific invasive external ear disease.
  190. The correct answer is B (Acanthamoeba species linked to poor contact lens hygiene practices): Acanthamoeba keratitis classically occurs in contact lens wearers, particularly with poor lens hygiene or exposure to tap water, producing a painful ring shaped corneal infiltrate. Adenovirus causes conjunctivitis rather than a ring infiltrate ulcer. Chlamydia causes inclusion conjunctivitis or trachoma, and molluscum and zoster produce different characteristic eye lesions.
  191. The correct answer is E (Viridans group streptococci originating from normal oral flora): Viridans streptococci are normal oral flora that cause subacute endocarditis after dental manipulation in patients with underlying valvular abnormalities. Staphylococcus aureus more typically causes acute, fulminant endocarditis unrelated to dental work. Candida and Bartonella are less common causes generally seen in specific risk groups such as intravenous drug use or immunosuppression.
  192. The correct answer is E (Coagulase negative staphylococci forming biofilm on the catheter surface): Coagulase negative staphylococci, largely Staphylococcus epidermidis, are the most common isolates from catheter related bloodstream infections due to their ability to form biofilm on catheter surfaces. Enterococcus and E coli occur less frequently in this setting. Bacteroides and Salmonella are rare causes of catheter infections and are more associated with gut sources.
  193. The correct answer is D (Hepatitis E virus, especially dangerous during the third trimester): Hepatitis E is transmitted through contaminated water and is notorious for causing severe, sometimes fulminant, hepatitis in pregnant women, especially in the third trimester. Hepatitis B, C, and D are transmitted through blood or body fluids, not fecal oral contamination. Hepatitis G is not a well established cause of clinically significant hepatitis.
  194. The correct answer is E (Gram negative enteric bacteria such as Escherichia coli and Klebsiella): Biliary tract disease predisposes to ascending infection with gut flora, making gram negative enteric organisms the leading cause of pyogenic liver abscess in this setting. Mycobacterium avium and Aspergillus typically cause disease in severely immunocompromised patients. Cryptosporidium causes diarrheal illness, and HSV hepatitis is rare and occurs mainly in immunosuppressed or pregnant patients.
  195. The correct answer is E (Ascending cholangitis resulting from obstruction of the common bile duct): Charcot triad reflects biliary obstruction with superimposed bacterial infection, defining classic ascending cholangitis, often from choledocholithiasis. Viral hepatitis causes jaundice but not the fever and pain pattern of the triad. Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis presents with abdominal pain and fever in cirrhotic ascites without biliary obstruction, and appendicitis and mononucleosis do not fit this triad.
  196. The correct answer is E (Odynophagia together with white adherent plaques on the esophageal mucosa): Candida esophagitis classically causes painful swallowing with visible white, adherent plaques seen on endoscopy, often in the setting of advanced HIV or other immunosuppression. HSV and CMV esophagitis instead produce discrete ulcers rather than plaques. A smooth stricture or Barrett changes reflect chronic reflux disease, not infectious esophagitis.
  197. The correct answer is A (Enterotoxigenic Escherichia coli producing toxin mediated watery diarrhea): Enterotoxigenic E coli is the most frequently identified cause of traveler's diarrhea worldwide, producing watery diarrhea through toxin mediated secretion. Clostridioides difficile is typically healthcare associated rather than travel related. Vibrio vulnificus is linked to raw shellfish and wound exposure to seawater. Entamoeba and Giardia are important but less frequent causes overall.
  198. The correct answer is B (Receptive anal intercourse performed without the use of a condom): Receptive anal intercourse carries the highest per act risk of HIV transmission among sexual exposures due to mucosal trauma and prolonged contact time. Household contact, shared utensils, mosquito bites, and pool exposure do not transmit HIV because the virus is not spread through casual contact or insect vectors.
  199. The correct answer is C (Disseminated Mycobacterium avium complex infection reflecting profound immunosuppression): Disseminated MAC infection classically emerges at very low CD4 counts, generally under 50 cells per microliter, reflecting profound immune suppression. Oral candidiasis and zoster reactivation occur at somewhat higher CD4 counts. Bacterial pneumonia and tuberculosis can occur across a wider range of CD4 counts and are not specific to this deep threshold.
  200. The correct answer is B (Paradoxical worsening of an existing infection as immune function recovers): IRIS occurs when a recovering immune system mounts an exaggerated inflammatory response against an existing or previously subclinical infection after antiretroviral therapy is started. It is not primarily drug toxicity, an allergic reaction, or rejection of therapy, and it typically involves a known or unmasked pathogen rather than an entirely new infection.
  201. The correct answer is D (Primary syphilis presenting with a classic indurated chancre lesion): Primary syphilis produces a classically painless, indurated ulcer called a chancre with a clean base. Genital herpes causes multiple painful vesicular and ulcerative lesions. Chancroid produces painful ulcers with ragged borders. Lymphogranuloma venereum and granuloma inguinale have distinct presentations with prominent lymphadenopathy or beefy red granulation tissue rather than a single clean ulcer.
  202. The correct answer is D (A declining nontreponemal titer such as RPR measured over time): Nontreponemal tests like RPR correlate with disease activity and are used to monitor treatment response, with an adequate response shown by a fourfold decline in titer. Treponemal tests generally remain positive for life regardless of treatment and are not used for monitoring. Persistently positive VDRL without titer decline suggests treatment failure or reinfection.
  203. The correct answer is B (HPV types 6 and 11, considered low oncogenic risk types): HPV types 6 and 11 are considered low risk types that cause the majority of genital warts but carry minimal oncogenic potential. Types 16 and 18 are the high risk types most responsible for cervical cancer. The other listed type pairings are also higher risk oncogenic strains rather than the classic wart causing types.
  204. The correct answer is D (Nitrofurantoin given its reliable concentration within the urinary tract): Nitrofurantoin is a preferred first line agent for uncomplicated cystitis due to reliable urinary concentration and a narrow spectrum that limits resistance pressure. Vancomycin is reserved for resistant gram positive infections and is not first line for cystitis. Clindamycin and doxycycline lack reliable urinary tract coverage, and ceftriaxone injection is unnecessary for uncomplicated outpatient cystitis.
  205. The correct answer is D (Neisseria gonorrhoeae following an episode of disseminated gonococcal infection): Disseminated gonococcal infection is a leading cause of septic arthritis in sexually active young adults, sometimes presenting with a migratory polyarthralgia before localizing to a single joint. Staphylococcus epidermidis is a low virulence skin organism rarely causing native joint infection. Salmonella septic arthritis is linked to sickle cell disease, and tuberculosis and Lyme arthritis have more indolent, chronic courses.
  206. The correct answer is C (Staphylococcus aureus, including methicillin resistant strains from skin flora): Staphylococcus aureus, including MRSA, is the leading cause of vertebral osteomyelitis overall, particularly in intravenous drug users due to hematogenous seeding from skin flora. Salmonella is classically linked to sickle cell disease rather than injection drug use. Streptococcus pneumoniae, Bartonella, and Coxiella are far less common causes of vertebral bone infection.
  207. The correct answer is C (Temperature abnormality, tachycardia, tachypnea, and abnormal white blood cell count): SIRS criteria include abnormal temperature, heart rate, respiratory rate, and white blood cell count, with two or more needed for the diagnosis. A positive blood culture is not required for SIRS, since it is a clinical syndrome rather than a microbiologic diagnosis. Hypotension with organ dysfunction better describes septic shock, and procalcitonin is a helpful biomarker but not part of the original criteria.
  208. The correct answer is C (Superantigen mediated massive release of systemic inflammatory cytokines and mediators): Toxic shock syndrome toxin acts as a superantigen, triggering nonspecific T cell activation and an overwhelming cytokine storm that produces the clinical syndrome, often without bacteremia. It does not depend on gram negative endotoxin, autoimmune cross reactivity with the heart, or a complement deficiency, distinguishing it mechanistically from other severe infections.
  209. The correct answer is D (Group A Streptococcus causing an episode of untreated pharyngitis): Acute rheumatic fever is a nonsuppurative sequela of untreated group A streptococcal pharyngitis, driven by molecular mimicry between streptococcal antigens and human tissue. It does not follow staphylococcal, E coli, Mycoplasma, or Haemophilus infections, which are not associated with this specific autoimmune cardiac and joint complication.
  210. The correct answer is B (The throat and the skin, following pharyngitis or impetigo): Poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis classically follows either streptococcal pharyngitis or impetigo, appearing one to three weeks after the preceding infection due to immune complex deposition. Urinary, bone and joint, cardiac, and gastrointestinal sites are not the recognized antecedent infection locations for this specific renal complication.
  211. The correct answer is D (Airborne precautions with placement in a negative pressure room): Tuberculosis is spread by small aerosolized droplet nuclei that remain suspended in air, requiring airborne precautions in a negative pressure room with N95 respirator use. Droplet precautions are appropriate for larger respiratory particles such as influenza. Contact precautions target organisms spread by direct or fomite contact, and standard precautions alone are insufficient for confirmed active tuberculosis.
  212. The correct answer is A (Measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine, a live attenuated formulation): MMR is a live attenuated vaccine and can cause disseminated infection in severely immunocompromised hosts, so it is generally avoided in that population. Inactivated influenza, pneumococcal conjugate, tetanus containing, and hepatitis B vaccines are all inactivated or subunit formulations that are safe to give to immunocompromised patients.
  213.
