The correct answer is E (Combination antiretroviral therapy continued for a defined treatment course): HIV postexposure prophylaxis uses a multidrug antiretroviral regimen started as soon as possible after exposure and continued for a defined duration to reduce transmission risk. Ceftriaxone and azithromycin address bacterial sexually transmitted infections rather than HIV. Corticosteroids and immunoglobulin do not have a role in HIV postexposure prophylaxis.
  214. The correct answer is B (Thick and thin blood smear microscopy performed by a trained technician): Thick and thin blood smears remain the diagnostic gold standard for malaria, allowing detection of parasites, species identification, and quantification of parasitemia. Stool studies, chest radiographs, urinalysis, and throat cultures do not detect blood borne malaria parasites and have no diagnostic role in this illness.
  215. The correct answer is C (A tick bite transmitting a rickettsial organism to the host): Rocky Mountain spotted fever is caused by Rickettsia rickettsii, transmitted through the bite of an infected tick, and classically presents with fever, headache, and a centripetal rash. Mosquitoes, fleas, sandflies, and lice transmit other distinct pathogens such as flaviviruses, plague, leishmaniasis, or epidemic typhus rather than this organism.
  216. The correct answer is D (Fever of unknown origin as classically defined by duration criteria): The classic definition of fever of unknown origin requires documented fever for at least three weeks with no diagnosis despite a reasonable inpatient or outpatient evaluation. Neutropenic fever applies specifically to patients with low absolute neutrophil counts, and nosocomial or postoperative fever refer to hospital acquired timing rather than this duration based definition. Drug fever is only one possible cause among many.
  217. The correct answer is A (Immediate surgical exploration to directly evaluate the affected tissue): Pain out of proportion, bullae, and rapid spread raise concern for necrotizing fasciitis, which requires urgent surgical exploration for definitive diagnosis and debridement since imaging and labs cannot reliably exclude it. Outpatient therapy, topical treatment, observation, or conservative measures alone would dangerously delay lifesaving surgical intervention in a necrotizing soft tissue infection.
  218. The correct answer is E (Obtain blood cultures and start empiric antibiotics, then obtain head CT): When signs suggest elevated intracranial pressure or mass effect, blood cultures and empiric antibiotics should be given first, followed by head CT before lumbar puncture, to avoid herniation risk while not delaying treatment. Performing lumbar puncture first risks herniation, and delaying antibiotics until all imaging is finished can worsen outcomes in bacterial meningitis.
  219. The correct answer is C (Empiric antibiotics combined with close serial imaging follow up): Small brain abscesses in a clinically stable patient can often be managed medically with empiric antibiotics and serial imaging to confirm response, reserving surgery for larger or worsening lesions. Immediate craniotomy is more appropriate for larger abscesses or those causing mass effect. Observation without treatment and radiation therapy have no role in this bacterial process, and antifungals are not indicated absent fungal suspicion.
  220. The correct answer is B (Cerebrospinal fluid HSV polymerase chain reaction testing sent promptly): CSF HSV PCR is the diagnostic test of choice for HSV encephalitis, offering high sensitivity and specificity without the invasiveness of biopsy. Serum antibody testing reflects past exposure rather than active central nervous system infection. Skin culture is not relevant absent visible lesions, and EEG can support the diagnosis but does not confirm it on its own.
  221. The correct answer is C (Urgent neurosurgical consultation to evaluate for possible decompression): Progressive neurologic deficit from spinal epidural abscess is a surgical emergency requiring urgent decompression alongside intravenous antibiotics to prevent permanent paralysis. Outpatient management, physical therapy, or discharge would allow ongoing cord compression to worsen, and steroid injection into an infected space risks spreading infection.
  222. The correct answer is A (Obtain emergent contrast CT and consult ophthalmology and otolaryngology urgently): New proptosis, restricted eye movement, and vision changes suggest orbital cellulitis or abscess, a vision and life threatening complication requiring emergent imaging and specialist involvement. Oral decongestants, nasal spray, compresses, and outpatient allergy testing are appropriate for uncomplicated sinusitis but dangerously inadequate once orbital extension is suspected.
  223. The correct answer is B (A macrolide antibiotic such as azithromycin given orally): Macrolides are a standard alternative for treating streptococcal pharyngitis in patients with penicillin allergy, providing reliable activity against group A Streptococcus. Vancomycin is reserved for more resistant or severe infections and is not first line oral therapy. Metronidazole and mupirocin lack adequate activity against this organism, and fluoroquinolones are not preferred agents for routine pharyngitis.
  224. The correct answer is A (Secure the airway in a controlled setting before further examination): Airway management takes priority in suspected epiglottitis because agitation or oral examination can precipitate complete airway obstruction, so airway control in a controlled setting, ideally the operating room, comes first. Radiographs, direct oral exam, and supine positioning risk provoking laryngospasm, and outpatient referral is inappropriate for an airway emergency.
  225. The correct answer is B (Start intravenous antipseudomonal antibiotics and obtain imaging of disease extent): Treatment failure in malignant otitis externa warrants escalation to intravenous antipseudomonal therapy along with imaging such as CT or MRI to evaluate for skull base extension. Antifungal therapy is not indicated since Pseudomonas is bacterial, and stopping antibiotics or using steroids alone would allow the infection to progress toward serious intracranial complications.
  226. The correct answer is C (Transthoracic echocardiogram, proceeding to transesophageal study if nondiagnostic): Echocardiography is central to diagnosing endocarditis, typically starting with transthoracic imaging and proceeding to transesophageal echocardiogram if the initial study is nondiagnostic or suspicion remains high. Abdominal ultrasound, pulmonary function tests, colonoscopy, and ankle brachial index are unrelated to evaluating cardiac valves for vegetations.
  227. The correct answer is C (Remove the catheter and obtain a repeat set of blood cultures): Staphylococcus aureus bacteremia from a central line generally requires catheter removal along with repeat cultures to confirm clearance, since Staphylococcus aureus is rarely a contaminant and carries high risk of endocarditis and metastatic seeding. Continuing to use the line, withholding antibiotics, or empiric antifungal therapy without evidence are inappropriate given the virulence of this organism.
  228. The correct answer is C (A nucleos(t)ide analog such as tenofovir or entecavir): Tenofovir and entecavir are preferred first line agents for chronic hepatitis B due to their potency and high barrier to resistance. Ribavirin is used in combination regimens for other viral infections, not hepatitis B monotherapy. Fluconazole, acyclovir, and metronidazole target fungal, herpes, and anaerobic or protozoal infections respectively, not hepatitis B.
  229. The correct answer is D (Percutaneous catheter drainage performed to achieve adequate source control): Larger pyogenic liver abscesses, generally over about 5 centimeters, benefit from percutaneous catheter drainage in addition to antibiotics to achieve source control. Lobectomy is reserved for cases refractory to less invasive drainage. Antifungal therapy and radiation have no role in typical bacterial abscesses, and observation alone risks incomplete resolution and rupture.
  230. The correct answer is B (Pursue urgent biliary decompression such as ERCP without further delay): Failure to improve with antibiotics and fluids in cholangitis signals the need for urgent biliary decompression, most often via ERCP, to relieve obstruction and achieve source control. Switching oral antibiotics or simply repeating cultures does not address the underlying obstruction, and discharge or antifungal therapy would be dangerously inappropriate in this deteriorating patient.
  231. The correct answer is D (Cytomegalovirus, given the classic linear esophageal ulcer pattern seen): Linear or large shallow esophageal ulcers in an advanced HIV patient are characteristic of CMV esophagitis, warranting empiric ganciclovir while biopsy confirmation is obtained. Candida typically produces plaques rather than linear ulcers. HSV causes small punched out ulcers, while genital HSV and HPV are not esophageal pathogens, and EBV mononucleosis does not typically cause this endoscopic pattern.
  232. The correct answer is E (Supportive care with oral rehydration and infection control precautions): Cruise ship outbreaks of acute watery diarrhea are frequently caused by norovirus, managed with supportive oral rehydration and strict infection control to limit spread, without a need for invasive procedures. Colonoscopy, empiric vancomycin, surgical evaluation, and antifungal therapy are not appropriate first steps for this typical self limited viral outbreak pattern.
  233. The correct answer is A (Initiate prophylaxis within 72 hours if indicated by risk assessment): Nonoccupational postexposure prophylaxis should begin as soon as possible and within 72 hours of a high risk exposure based on assessed risk, since efficacy declines the longer treatment is delayed. Waiting for testing results or the partner's status, or delaying weeks, would miss the window during which prophylaxis is effective.
  234. The correct answer is E (A single intramuscular dose of benzathine penicillin G): A single intramuscular dose of benzathine penicillin G is the standard and preferred treatment for primary syphilis. Azithromycin has documented resistance concerns and is not first line. Fluconazole and acyclovir target fungal and herpes infections respectively, and vancomycin has no activity against the syphilis spirochete.
  235. The correct answer is E (Begin empiric oral antibiotics without first requiring a urine culture): In an otherwise healthy woman with classic symptoms of uncomplicated cystitis, empiric oral antibiotics can be started based on history and urinalysis without waiting for culture. CT urogram, admission, cystoscopy, and delaying treatment for culture results are unnecessary and would delay appropriate outpatient care.
  236. The correct answer is B (Start empiric intravenous antibiotics after obtaining synovial fluid culture): A synovial fluid white count in this range with neutrophil predominance is highly concerning for septic arthritis, requiring prompt empiric intravenous antibiotics once cultures are obtained to prevent joint destruction. Steroid injection is contraindicated in suspected infection, and NSAIDs alone, discharge, or awaiting MRI would dangerously delay treatment.
  237. The correct answer is A (Image guided percutaneous bone biopsy sent for microbiologic culture): When blood cultures are negative in suspected vertebral osteomyelitis, image guided biopsy is recommended to identify the causative organism and guide targeted, prolonged antibiotic therapy. Starting broad empiric therapy without an organism risks inadequate or overly broad treatment, and antifungal therapy or surgery are not appropriate as default next steps without further data.
  238. The correct answer is C (Vasopressor support, with norepinephrine as the preferred first agent): When hypotension persists despite adequate fluid resuscitation in septic shock, vasopressor therapy, with norepinephrine as the preferred first agent, should be initiated to restore perfusion pressure. Continued fluid boluses alone risk volume overload, and corticosteroids, renal replacement therapy, and empiric antifungals are not first line steps at this specific decision point.
  239. The correct answer is D (Remove the tampon and begin supportive care with antibiotics): Toxic shock syndrome associated with tampon use requires immediate removal of the foreign material along with aggressive supportive care and antistaphylococcal antibiotics. Antihistamines, discharge, antifungal therapy, and delayed outpatient follow up all fail to address a life threatening, rapidly progressive condition.
  240. The correct answer is B (Treat for acute rheumatic fever and plan secondary antibiotic prophylaxis): This presentation is classic for acute rheumatic fever, requiring anti-inflammatory treatment and long term secondary antibiotic prophylaxis to prevent recurrence and further cardiac damage. Reassurance, antifungal therapy, physical therapy alone, or a repeat strep test without treatment would fail to address this significant post-streptococcal complication.
  241. The correct answer is A (Contact precautions with a gown and gloves for every interaction): Multidrug resistant organisms spread by direct or indirect contact require contact precautions, including gown and gloves, to prevent transmission between patients. Airborne and droplet precautions target respiratory pathogens transmitted through the air, and hand hygiene alone or protective environment measures do not adequately address contact transmitted organisms.
  242. The correct answer is E (Wash the wound and report immediately for risk assessment and prophylaxis): Prompt wound care followed by immediate reporting allows timely risk assessment, source patient testing, and initiation of postexposure prophylaxis if indicated, since delays reduce prophylactic effectiveness. Waiting until the end of a shift, ignoring the injury, self medicating, or only documenting without evaluation all risk missing the window for effective intervention.
  243. The correct answer is E (Intravenous artesunate given as the preferred first line therapy): Severe falciparum malaria with organ dysfunction such as altered mental status requires intravenous artesunate as first line therapy given its rapid parasite clearance and mortality benefit. Oral chloroquine is inadequate given resistance and severity, doxycycline alone is insufficient as monotherapy, and delaying treatment or withholding antimalarials would risk fatal outcomes.
  244. The correct answer is D (Gas production from anaerobic bacterial fermentation within necrotic tissue): Clostridium perfringens produces gas as a byproduct of anaerobic fermentation within devitalized tissue, generating the crepitus and radiographic gas seen in clostridial myonecrosis. This is not related to blood draw technique, an unrelated pulmonary process, routine postoperative air, or a distant bowel perforation, since the gas originates directly at the site of active anaerobic infection.
  245. The correct answer is A (Blunting the inflammatory mediator release triggered by bacterial lysis): Adjunctive dexamethasone given before or with antibiotics blunts the inflammatory cascade released as bacteria are killed, which has been shown to reduce complications such as hearing loss, particularly in pneumococcal meningitis. It does not enhance antibiotic penetration, prevent allergic reactions, shorten antibiotic duration, or have direct antibacterial activity.
  246. The correct answer is E (Reconsider the diagnosis and evaluate for primary central nervous system lymphoma): Failure to improve on appropriate empiric toxoplasmosis therapy after about two weeks should prompt reevaluation, since positive serology only indicates prior exposure and primary CNS lymphoma is an important alternative diagnosis in this population. Continuing unchanged therapy, switching to antituberculous drugs without evidence, stopping all treatment, or adding another antiparasitic without reassessment would not address a possible incorrect diagnosis.
  247. The correct answer is A (Postinfectious autoimmune encephalitis triggered by the earlier HSV infection): A relapse of neurologic symptoms weeks after successfully treated HSV encephalitis is a recognized pattern of postinfectious autoimmune encephalitis, often anti-NMDA receptor mediated, distinct from viral relapse. Simply extending acyclovir would not address an autoimmune process, and this pattern is not an expected normal finding, a compliance issue, or coincidental bacterial meningitis.
  248. The correct answer is E (Ongoing mechanical cord compression can cause irreversible injury regardless of antibiotics): When there is significant mass effect with new neurologic deficits like saddle anesthesia, mechanical decompression is urgently needed because antibiotics cannot relieve physical compression on neural tissue, and delay risks permanent injury. Not all epidural abscesses require surgery, especially without significant deficits, antibiotics do cross into the central nervous system to some degree, and MRI is a reliable diagnostic tool for this condition.
  249. The correct answer is B (Chronic sinusitis often involves structural obstruction or biofilm rather than acute infection): Chronic sinusitis frequently reflects anatomic obstruction, biofilm formation, allergic or inflammatory disease rather than a simple recurring acute bacterial infection, which is why repeated antibiotic courses often fail and evaluation for structural or inflammatory causes becomes important. The other options either overgeneralize resistance or dismiss antibiotics inaccurately rather than identifying the actual underlying issue.
  250. The correct answer is A (Carriers have a low risk of transmitting infection or developing rheumatic complications): Chronic streptococcal carriers are generally at low risk for spreading infection to others or developing rheumatic fever, so routine eradication therapy is not typically recommended outside specific circumstances such as outbreaks. The other statements about resistant coinfection, antibiotic penetration, immune suppression, or rapid spontaneous resolution are not accurate explanations for this management approach.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Mastoiditis, a complication that can progress to intracranial extension): Postauricular swelling and ear protrusion following otitis media are classic signs of mastoiditis, which can progress to serious intracranial complications such as abscess or venous sinus thrombosis if not promptly imaged and treated. The other listed conditions do not produce postauricular swelling with ear displacement and would not require this level of urgent evaluation.
  2. The correct answer is B (A perivalvular abscess extending directly into the cardiac conduction system): New conduction abnormalities in the setting of prosthetic valve endocarditis strongly suggest perivalvular abscess extending toward the conduction system, an emergency that often requires surgical intervention. This finding is not a benign incidental change, typical antibiotic side effect, unrelated coronary disease, or an expected normal postoperative finding.
  3. The correct answer is D (Colonization or specimen contamination rather than a true catheter infection): Mixed skin flora in low counts from a chronically catheterized patient more often reflects colonization or specimen contamination rather than true infection, and the fever source should be reassessed rather than assumed to be urinary. This finding does not confirm bacteremia, obstruction requiring surgery, or fungal infection without further supporting evidence.
  4. The correct answer is C (Portal hypertension leading to splenic sequestration of circulating platelets): Cirrhosis leads to portal hypertension, causing splenomegaly and sequestration of platelets within the enlarged spleen, which is the most common mechanism of thrombocytopenia in chronic liver disease. Direct viral platelet destruction, universal autoimmune disease, or B12 deficiency are not the primary mechanisms, and antiviral related marrow suppression would only apply if therapy were currently being given and does not explain baseline findings before treatment.
  5. The correct answer is E (A helminthic parasite such as a liver fluke acquired through diet): Eosinophilia combined with a liver lesion after eating raw freshwater fish strongly suggests a parasitic helminthic infection such as a liver fluke, which triggers eosinophilic inflammatory responses. Bacterial, viral hepatitis, skin flora, and respiratory viral pathogens do not typically produce prominent eosinophilia and are not linked to this specific exposure history.
  6. The correct answer is C (Idiopathic HIV associated esophageal ulceration as a diagnosis of exclusion): When common infectious causes have been excluded by biopsy in advanced HIV disease, idiopathic HIV associated esophageal ulcers become an important diagnosis of exclusion, often responding to corticosteroid therapy. Reflux disease, normal mucosa, generic medication effects, and dental extension are not consistent with biopsy proven ulceration after infectious causes have been ruled out.
  7. The correct answer is B (Immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome unmasking a previously subclinical infection): A falling viral load with rising CD4 count alongside new inflammatory symptoms is the hallmark of IRIS, in which the recovering immune system reacts strongly to an existing or subclinical infection. Treatment failure and nonadherence would show a rising viral load rather than improvement, and this is not a routine or universal expected reaction to effective therapy.
  8. The correct answer is C (Chancroid caused by an infection with Haemophilus ducreyi bacteria): Painful ulcers with ragged, undermined borders and tender suppurative inguinal lymphadenopathy are classic for chancroid, distinguishing it from the painless, clean based ulcer of primary syphilis. Contact dermatitis and fixed drug eruption do not typically produce suppurative lymphadenopathy, and Behcet disease usually involves recurrent oral and genital aphthous lesions without this infectious lymph node pattern.
  9. The correct answer is B (Refer the patient for penicillin desensitization followed by penicillin treatment): Penicillin remains the only reliably effective therapy for syphilis in pregnancy, including for preventing congenital syphilis, so an allergic pregnant patient should undergo desensitization rather than receive an alternative agent. Doxycycline is contraindicated in pregnancy, azithromycin has resistance and efficacy concerns for congenital prevention, withholding treatment risks fetal harm, and fluconazole has no activity against syphilis.
  10. The correct answer is D (Prompt partner treatment reduces reinfection risk and interrupts ongoing transmission): Expedited partner therapy for chlamydia is recommended because treating partners promptly, without waiting for their own test results, reduces the risk of reinfecting the index patient and interrupts further community transmission. The other statements dismissing partner testing, treatment benefit, or the need for any therapy contradict standard sexually transmitted infection control practice.
  11. The correct answer is E (Behavioral factors such as spermicide use or delayed postcoital voiding): When structural abnormalities have been excluded, modifiable behavioral factors such as spermicide use, diaphragm use, or delayed postcoital voiding are important contributors to recurrent cystitis that should be addressed. Malignancy, a missed anatomic anomaly, lifelong intravenous therapy, and inevitable kidney failure are not supported conclusions from this clinical picture.
  12. The correct answer is C (Salmonella species, given the known predisposition seen in sickle cell disease): Patients with sickle cell disease have an increased predisposition to Salmonella osteomyelitis due to impaired splenic function and bowel wall microinfarction allowing bacterial translocation, making it an important consideration alongside the more universally common Staphylococcus aureus. Pseudomonas is more associated with puncture wounds, and Bartonella, Coxiella, and Cryptococcus are not classically linked to sickle cell related bone infection.
  13. The correct answer is A (Empiric antifungal coverage should be considered given risk factors and deterioration): In patients with risk factors for invasive candidiasis who deteriorate despite broad spectrum antibacterial therapy and vasopressor support, empiric antifungal coverage should be considered rather than waiting for confirmed culture results, since Candida is a genuine cause of septic shock. Dismissing candidemia as a contaminant, avoiding antifungals during vasopressor use, or assuming bacterial coverage is always adequate would risk missing this treatable diagnosis.
  14. The correct answer is A (The syndrome is driven by circulating toxin effects rather than bloodstream invasion): Toxic shock syndrome results from circulating superantigen toxin effects rather than requiring the bacteria themselves to invade the bloodstream, so localized infection with toxin production can cause systemic shock despite negative blood cultures. The other options incorrectly assume that bacteremia is required for the diagnosis or blame culture technique rather than the actual toxin mediated mechanism.
  15. The correct answer is C (Recurrent streptococcal infections substantially raise the risk of worsening valve damage): Long term secondary prophylaxis after acute rheumatic fever, particularly with carditis, is used because subsequent streptococcal infections substantially raise the risk of progressive valvular damage, and prevention is far more effective than treating each recurrence. Prophylaxis does not replace ongoing cardiac monitoring, does not target unrelated infections, and a prior episode does not guarantee protection from recurrence without prophylaxis.
  16. The correct answer is D (The disease is generally self limited as circulating immune complexes clear): Poststreptococcal glomerulonephritis is typically self limited, with complement levels normalizing over several weeks as the immune complex mediated process resolves, which is a key feature distinguishing it from other complement consuming glomerular diseases. The other statements incorrectly claim complement never changes, that persistence always means chronic disease, or that dialysis is required for normalization, none of which reflect the typical clinical course.
  17. The correct answer is C (Soap and water hand hygiene combined with contact precautions for affected patients): Because Clostridioides difficile forms spores resistant to alcohol based sanitizers, soap and water hand washing combined with contact precautions is essential to physically remove spores and prevent transmission. Relying solely on alcohol sanitizer, discontinuing isolation, using masks alone, or focusing only on air exchange would not adequately address this spore forming, contact transmitted organism.
  18. The correct answer is D (Dormant liver hypnozoites reactivating after the initial blood stage was cleared): Plasmodium vivax and Plasmodium ovale form dormant liver hypnozoites that can reactivate months later even after successful treatment of the blood stage infection, which is why radical cure with an agent like primaquine or tafenoquine is needed to prevent relapse. Universal resistance, reinfection alone, no need for additional therapy, and an incorrect original diagnosis do not account for this well recognized biologic relapse pattern.
  19. The correct answer is A (Serology is often negative early in illness, and delayed treatment raises mortality): Rocky Mountain spotted fever serology is frequently negative in the first several days of illness, so empiric doxycycline is started based on clinical suspicion alone because delayed treatment is strongly associated with increased mortality. The other options incorrectly suggest serology is immediately reliable or that treatment timing does not matter, contradicting the urgency required in this disease.
  20. The correct answer is A (Consider PET-CT imaging to identify an occult focus of disease): When standard evaluation is unrevealing in fever of unknown origin, advanced imaging such as PET-CT can help localize an occult infectious, malignant, or inflammatory source not identified by initial testing. Stopping the workup, starting empiric antibiotics without a target, assuming a psychogenic cause, or simply repeating the same tests would not move the diagnostic evaluation forward.
  21. The correct answer is A (Sensitivity, the ability to correctly identify patients who truly have disease): sensitivity is the proportion of patients who truly have the disease whom the test correctly identifies as positive. Specificity instead measures correct identification of disease-free patients. Predictive values depend on disease prevalence rather than reflecting the test alone. Prevalence simply describes how common the disease is in the population studied.
  22. The correct answer is C (Specificity, which measures correct identification of children who are truly disease-free): specificity is calculated only among patients confirmed disease-free and reflects how well the test correctly labels them as negative. Sensitivity uses diseased patients instead, and predictive values shift with disease prevalence. Incidence describes new disease occurrence, not test performance, so it does not fit this scenario.
  23. The correct answer is B (Positive predictive value, which shifts substantially with disease prevalence): PPV depends heavily on the prevalence of disease in the tested population, so it changes substantially between a high-prevalence emergency department and a low-prevalence outpatient clinic. Sensitivity and specificity are intrinsic properties of the test and stay constant across populations. Likelihood ratios and the ROC curve are also independent of prevalence.
  24. The correct answer is D (Duty to warn or protect an identifiable, specifically threatened victim): when a patient makes a credible, specific threat against an identifiable person, most jurisdictions permit or require breaking confidentiality to protect that third party. This exception exists specifically to prevent serious foreseeable harm, unlike the other listed principles, which do not address protecting a named potential victim.
  25. The correct answer is E (A durable power of attorney naming her chosen health care agent): this document formally names a specific surrogate to make medical decisions if the patient loses capacity. A living will states treatment preferences but names no person, a DNR addresses resuscitation only, a POLST is a medical order set, and informed consent applies to a single decision, not future surrogacy.
  26. The correct answer is B (Ability to understand, appreciate, reason through, and communicate a choice): decisional capacity is decision-specific and rests on these four functional elements, regardless of an underlying diagnosis like dementia. A psychiatric diagnosis, disagreement with the physician, a cognitive test score, or a judge's ruling are neither necessary nor sufficient for determining capacity at the bedside.
  27. The correct answer is A (The patient's legally recognized spouse or domestic partner): most state surrogate hierarchy statutes place a spouse or legal partner above adult children, parents, or friends when no advance directive exists. The attending physician guides decisions but does not serve as the legal surrogate, and informal friends rank below family members in this hierarchy.
  28. The correct answer is D (A Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle of small, iterative improvement testing): testing a small change on a limited scale, studying results, adjusting, and repeating before wider rollout is the hallmark of a PDSA cycle. Root cause analysis investigates past events, FMEA anticipates failure points prospectively, DMAIC follows a more structured statistical framework, and value stream mapping visualizes workflow rather than testing changes iteratively.
  29. The correct answer is C (Approximately 90 percent of the diseased patients test positive): sensitivity equals true positives divided by all diseased patients, which is 90 divided by 100, or 90 percent. The other percentages either miscalculate this ratio or confuse it with specificity, which would instead use the 190 correctly negative results out of 200 disease-free patients.
  30. The correct answer is E (About 5 patients would need to be treated): the absolute risk reduction is 50 percent minus 30 percent, or 20 percent, and NNT equals 1 divided by the absolute risk reduction, giving 1 divided by 0.20, which equals 5. The other options result from miscalculating the absolute risk reduction or inverting the wrong figure.
  31. The correct answer is D (A relative risk reduction of about 50 percent): relative risk reduction equals the absolute risk reduction divided by the baseline risk, so 1 percent divided by 2 percent equals 50 percent. The 1 percent and 2 percent options confuse relative reduction with absolute values, and 99 percent or 200 percent misapply the formula entirely.
  32. The correct answer is B (A positive likelihood ratio of approximately 8): the positive likelihood ratio equals sensitivity divided by one minus specificity, or 0.80 divided by 0.10, which equals 8. The other values result from using sensitivity or specificity alone, inverting the formula, or multiplying figures that should instead be divided.
  33. The correct answer is E (Confounding variable linked to both coffee use and lung cancer): smoking is associated with both the exposure, coffee drinking, and the outcome, lung cancer, without being caused by coffee, which is the classic definition of a confounder. It is not on the causal pathway between coffee and cancer, so it is not a mediator, and it does not change the strength of association differently across subgroups, so it is not an effect modifier.
  34. The correct answer is A (Recall bias arising from differential memory between the two groups): mothers of affected infants are more motivated to remember and report past exposures than mothers of healthy infants, systematically distorting reported exposure rates in a case-control study. Lead-time and length-time bias relate to screening and survival, Berkson bias relates to hospital-based selection, and publication bias concerns which studies get published, none of which fit this scenario.
  35. The correct answer is C (The Swiss cheese model, where aligned system failures allow harm through): each safety layer has gaps, and this event occurred because the holes in the checklist, marking, and time-out aligned on the same occasion. Blaming one individual, assuming a single culpable person, or claiming any single tool eliminates all error misrepresents how layered safety systems actually function.
  36. The correct answer is E (Root cause analysis focused on underlying system-level factors): this structured method examines contributing systemic factors like labeling and staffing rather than assigning individual blame. Punitive actions against the nurse, public shaming, litigation, or concealing the event from the family do not identify or fix the underlying system vulnerabilities that allowed the error.
  37. The correct answer is B (Disclose the error to the patient with explanation and apology): ethical guidelines and most institutional policies require transparent disclosure of errors regardless of whether harm occurred or whether the patient is aware. Concealing the error, waiting for harm to appear, informing only risk management, or disclosing only if asked all violate the physician's duty of honesty.
  38. The correct answer is A (He likely retains decisional capacity for this specific choice): he demonstrates understanding, appreciation, reasoning, and a clear choice consistent with his values, which is the standard for decisional capacity regardless of his psychiatric diagnosis. Capacity is decision-specific and is not automatically negated by mental illness, disagreement with the recommendation, or requiring a court order for every refusal.
  39. The correct answer is C (Healthy worker effect arising from selection into employment): employed workers are generally healthier than the general population, which includes people too sick to work, so worker cohorts often show artificially lower mortality even when exposed to hazards. Recall bias, lead-time bias, confounding by indication, and immortal time bias describe different distortions that do not match this employment-based selection pattern.
  40. The correct answer is D (Drug Y shows a more favorable benefit to harm ratio overall): dividing NNH by NNT gives 5 for Drug X and 10 for Drug Y, showing Drug Y causes relatively less harm per unit of benefit even though more patients must be treated to prevent one stroke. Judging either drug by NNT or NNH alone, as the other options do, ignores this important tradeoff.
  41. The correct answer is C (Renal hypoperfusion causing avid sodium and water reabsorption.): The low FeNa, elevated BUN to creatinine ratio, and hyaline casts all indicate prerenal azotemia in which intact tubules respond to hypoperfusion by reabsorbing sodium avidly. Nephrotoxic injury (B) and interstitial or glomerular disease (C, D, E) would not produce this classic low FeNa pattern with preserved tubular function.
  42. The correct answer is B (Acute tubular necrosis from aminoglycoside toxicity): Aminoglycosides cause direct proximal tubular injury, producing muddy brown granular casts and a FeNa above 2 percent from impaired tubular sodium reabsorption. Prerenal azotemia (A) would show a low FeNa, interstitial nephritis (C) typically shows eosinophils and white cell casts, and obstruction (D) or glomerulonephritis (E) do not fit this drug exposure and sediment.
  43. The correct answer is C (Markedly elevated serum creatine kinase with myoglobinuria): Rhabdomyolysis is confirmed by a markedly elevated CK along with a dipstick positive for blood but no RBCs seen, since the assay detects myoglobin. Urine sodium (A) and ANA (D) do not confirm this diagnosis, low haptoglobin with schistocytes (B) suggests hemolysis rather than muscle injury, and eosinophils (E) suggest interstitial nephritis.
  44. The correct answer is D (Hepatorenal syndrome from splanchnic vasodilation and renal vasoconstriction): Progressive renal failure in cirrhosis with a bland sediment that fails to improve with albumin and diuretic withdrawal is characteristic of hepatorenal syndrome, driven by splanchnic vasodilation triggering renal vasoconstriction. Acute tubular necrosis (A) usually follows a clear nephrotoxic insult, and the other options do not match this clinical pattern.
  45. The correct answer is E (Cholesterol embolization syndrome from arterial catheter manipulation): The delayed onset after an arterial procedure, livedo reticularis, blue toes with intact pulses, and eosinophilia are classic for cholesterol embolization from catheter-induced plaque disruption. Contrast nephropathy (A) occurs within days, and the other options do not explain the skin findings and eosinophilia together.
  46. The correct answer is A (Stage G3a chronic kidney disease): By KDIGO criteria, G3a covers an eGFR of 45 to 59 mL/min/1.73m2, and an eGFR of 52 confirmed on two occasions three months apart falls squarely in this range. G2 (C) covers 60 to 89, G3b (B) covers 30 to 44, G4 (D) covers 15 to 29, and G5 (E) covers below 15, none of which match an eGFR of 52.
  47. The correct answer is C (Native arteriovenous fistula created from the patient's own vessels): Native fistulas have the lowest rates of infection and thrombosis compared to grafts or catheters because they use the patient's own vessel, which matures and resists infection better. Catheters (A, E) carry the highest infection risk, grafts (B) have higher thrombosis rates than fistulas, and peritoneal catheters (D) are a different modality entirely.
  48. The correct answer is D (ACE inhibitor to reduce intraglomerular pressure and proteinuria): ACE inhibitors reduce efferent arteriolar resistance, lowering intraglomerular pressure and proteinuria, which has the strongest evidence for slowing CKD progression in proteinuric patients. Diuretics (A) and calcium channel blockers (B) help blood pressure but lack this specific renoprotective mechanism, severe protein restriction (C) is not standard practice, and beta-blockers (E) lack comparable renal protective evidence.
  49. The correct answer is B (Acute interstitial nephritis from a drug hypersensitivity reaction): Fever, rash, eosinophiluria, and rising creatinine after starting a new medication like a proton pump inhibitor are the classic triad and sediment findings of acute interstitial nephritis. Acute tubular necrosis (A) shows granular casts rather than eosinophils, and the other options do not fit this hypersensitivity pattern.
  50. The correct answer is A (Class IV, diffuse proliferative lupus nephritis): Diffuse endocapillary proliferation involving more than half of glomeruli defines class IV lupus nephritis, the most severe and common proliferative form. Class II (B) shows only mesangial changes, class V (C) is membranous without this proliferative pattern, class I (D) is nearly normal, and class VI (E) represents advanced sclerosis rather than active proliferation.
  51. The correct answer is C (Minimal change disease with diffuse foot process effacement): A normal-appearing biopsy on light microscopy in a child with nephrotic syndrome is the hallmark of minimal change disease, with foot process effacement only visible on electron microscopy. FSGS (A) and membranous nephropathy (B) show visible structural lesions on light microscopy, and IgA (D) and MPGN (E) show mesangial or proliferative changes.
  52. The correct answer is D (HIV-associated focal segmental glomerulosclerosis): Collapsing glomerulopathy with segmental sclerosis in the setting of untreated HIV is characteristic of HIV-associated nephropathy, a distinct and aggressive form of FSGS. Minimal change disease (A) shows a normal-appearing biopsy, membranous nephropathy (B) shows subepithelial deposits, and IgA (C) and anti-GBM (E) have different characteristic biopsy findings.
  53. The correct answer is E (Anti-phospholipase A2 receptor antibody): Anti-PLA2R antibodies are found in the majority of primary membranous nephropathy cases and are highly specific for this diagnosis. Anti-dsDNA (A) is associated with lupus, anti-GBM (B) with Goodpasture syndrome, ANCA (C) with vasculitis, and ASO (D) with post-streptococcal disease, none of which are specific for primary membranous nephropathy.
  54. The correct answer is A (IgA nephropathy with synpharyngitic hematuria): Gross hematuria occurring within one to two days of an upper respiratory infection, recurring with subsequent illnesses, is the classic synpharyngitic pattern of IgA nephropathy. Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (B) has a much longer latency of one to three weeks, and the other options do not follow this recurrent infection-associated timing.
  55. The correct answer is B (Anti-glomerular basement membrane antibody): Linear IgG deposition along the basement membrane with pulmonary hemorrhage and rapidly progressive renal failure defines anti-GBM disease, caused by antibodies against type IV collagen. PLA2R (A) is linked to membranous nephropathy, anti-Smith (C) to lupus, ANCA (D) to pauci-immune vasculitis, and anti-topoisomerase (E) to scleroderma, none of which produce this linear pattern.
  56. The correct answer is D (Primary aldosteronism from an adrenal source): Elevated aldosterone with suppressed renin activity, hypertension, and hypokalemia is the classic biochemical signature of primary aldosteronism. Renal artery stenosis (A) causes elevated renin, pheochromocytoma (B) presents with catecholamine excess symptoms, Cushing syndrome (C) has cortisol-driven features, and licorice ingestion (E) suppresses both aldosterone and renin rather than elevating aldosterone.
  57. The correct answer is E (Escherichia coli from the periurethral flora): E. coli accounts for the large majority of uncomplicated urinary tract infections in young women due to ascension from periurethral colonization. Staphylococcus saprophyticus (B) is the second most common cause but less frequent overall, and the remaining organisms (A, C, D) are more typical of complicated or healthcare-associated infections.
  58. The correct answer is A (Calcium oxalate stones from excess urinary oxalate): Calcium oxalate stones account for the majority of kidney stones in the general population, more than struvite, uric acid, cystine, or calcium phosphate stones combined. The other stone types (B through E) are each less common and often associated with specific predisposing conditions like infection or metabolic disorders.
  59. The correct answer is B (Renal vein thrombosis related to urinary protein loss): Nephrotic syndrome, particularly membranous nephropathy, causes a hypercoagulable state from urinary loss of antithrombotic proteins, predisposing to renal vein thrombosis presenting with flank pain and hematuria. Pyelonephritis (A) presents with fever and infectious symptoms, and the other options do not specifically relate to the hypercoagulable nephrotic state.
  60. The correct answer is C (Bladder outlet obstruction causing back-pressure on both kidneys): BPH causes bladder outlet obstruction that leads to increased pressure transmitted retrograde to both kidneys, producing bilateral hydronephrosis and renal dysfunction. The other mechanisms (A, B, D, E) describe glomerular, vascular, or toxic processes that do not explain obstruction-related bilateral hydronephrosis.
  61. The correct answer is E (Microalbuminuria detected on urine testing): Microalbuminuria is the earliest detectable marker of diabetic nephropathy, preceding declines in GFR, overt proteinuria, or elevated creatinine. Reduced eGFR (A), gross hematuria (B), nephrotic-range proteinuria (C), and elevated creatinine (D) all represent later stages of kidney damage.
  62. The correct answer is A (PKD1, the most common cause of autosomal dominant disease): PKD1 mutations account for the majority of autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease cases and are associated with earlier onset of renal failure. PKHD1 (B) causes the recessive form typically presenting in infancy, while TSC2, VHL, and NPHS1 (C, D, E) are associated with distinct genetic syndromes rather than typical ADPKD.
  63. The correct answer is B (PSA screening should involve shared decision-making given overdiagnosis risk): Current guidelines recommend individualized shared decision-making for PSA screening because of the risk of overdiagnosis and overtreatment of indolent cancers. Universal screening (A) and no role for screening (C) are both overly absolute, and digital rectal exam alone (D) or PSA eliminating biopsy need (E) do not reflect accurate screening practice.
  64. The correct answer is C (Alpha-1 adrenergic receptor blockade relaxing prostatic smooth muscle): Tamsulosin selectively blocks alpha-1 adrenergic receptors in the prostate and bladder neck, relaxing smooth muscle and improving urinary flow. 5-alpha reductase inhibition (A) describes finasteride's mechanism, and the remaining options (B, D, E) describe mechanisms of other unrelated drug classes.
  65. The correct answer is D (Limit total correction to 8 to 10 mEq/L in 24 hours): Severe chronic hyponatremia must be corrected slowly, generally no more than 8 to 10 mEq/L in 24 hours, to avoid osmotic demyelination syndrome. The other options describe correction rates or strategies that are either far too rapid or clinically inappropriate.
  66. The correct answer is A (Intravenous calcium gluconate to stabilize the cardiac membrane): In hyperkalemia with EKG changes, calcium gluconate is given first to stabilize the cardiac membrane and prevent arrhythmia, even though it does not lower potassium levels. Insulin-dextrose (D) and other therapies (B, C, E) address the potassium level itself but do not provide the immediate cardiac membrane stabilization needed first.
  67. The correct answer is B (An anion gap of 28, elevated and consistent with the process): The anion gap equals sodium minus the sum of chloride and bicarbonate, so 140 minus 112 equals 28, which is well above the normal range of 8 to 12 and confirms a high anion gap metabolic acidosis. The other listed values do not match the correct calculation from the given electrolytes.
  68. The correct answer is C (Expected PaCO2 is 21 to 25, so the value indicates a concurrent respiratory acidosis): Winter's formula predicts expected PaCO2 as 1.5 times bicarbonate plus 8, plus or minus 2, giving 21 to 25 for a bicarbonate of 10; since the measured PaCO2 of 32 exceeds this range, an additional respiratory acidosis is present. The other options miscalculate or misinterpret the expected compensation.
  69. The correct answer is D (136 mEq/L after applying the correction factor): Corrected sodium equals measured sodium plus 1.6 times the difference between glucose and 100, divided by 100, which gives 128 plus 1.6 times 5, or 128 plus 8, equaling 136 mEq/L. The other values do not reflect the correct calculation using the given glucose and correction factor.
  70. The correct answer is E (An intact parathyroid hormone level): Measuring intact PTH distinguishes PTH-mediated hypercalcemia, such as primary hyperparathyroidism, from PTH-independent causes like malignancy, where PTH is typically suppressed.   71. The correct answer is B (Intravenous calcium gluconate given promptly): Acute symptomatic hypocalcemia with tetany requires prompt intravenous calcium gluconate for rapid correction and symptom relief.   72. The correct answer is C (Phosphate binders such as sevelamer taken with meals): Phosphate binders taken with meals reduce intestinal absorption of dietary phosphate and are the appropriate next step when diet alone fails to control hyperphosphatemia in dialysis patients.   73. The correct answer is D (Refeeding syndrome causing an intracellular phosphate shift): Refeeding after malnutrition triggers insulin release that drives phosphate into cells, causing severe hypophosphatemia with weakness and respiratory compromise, characteristic of refeeding syndrome. Tumor lysis (A) and hungry bone syndrome (B) occur in different clinical contexts, and rhabdomyolysis (C) and renal wasting (E) do not fit this feeding-related timeline.
  74. The correct answer is E (Intravenous calcium gluconate to antagonize magnesium toxicity): Calcium gluconate directly antagonizes the neuromuscular and cardiac effects of magnesium toxicity and should be given immediately in symptomatic hypermagnesemia. Furosemide (A) may help eliminate magnesium but is not the immediate priority, and the other options do not address the acute toxicity as directly.
  75. The correct answer is A (Magnesium, which is low from proton pump inhibitor use): Long-term proton pump inhibitor use is a recognized cause of hypomagnesemia through impaired intestinal magnesium absorption, and low magnesium causes refractory hypokalemia that will not correct until magnesium is repleted. The other electrolytes (B, C, D, E) are not the classic mechanism linking PPI use to refractory hypokalemia.
  76. The correct answer is C (Urine chloride below 20 mEq/L reflecting volume contraction): A low urine chloride in the setting of vomiting-induced metabolic alkalosis indicates volume contraction and predicts that the alkalosis will correct with saline administration, defining it as chloride-responsive. An elevated urine chloride (A) would instead suggest a chloride-resistant cause, and the other findings (B, D, E) do not confirm chloride responsiveness.
  77. The correct answer is D (Chronic respiratory acidosis with partial metabolic compensation): The markedly elevated bicarbonate of 32 alongside a high PaCO2 and only mildly acidemic pH indicates a chronic respiratory acidosis with renal metabolic compensation, typical of COPD. Acute processes (A, E) would show less compensatory bicarbonate elevation, and options B and C do not match this combination of values.
  78. The correct answer is E (Concurrent respiratory alkalosis and high anion gap metabolic acidosis): Salicylate toxicity classically causes a mixed disorder with primary respiratory alkalosis from direct respiratory center stimulation plus a high anion gap metabolic acidosis from salicylate and lactate accumulation, explaining the near-normal pH with low PaCO2 and low bicarbonate. The other single-disorder options cannot account for both the very low PaCO2 and low bicarbonate together.
  79. The correct answer is A (Glomerular origin such as glomerulonephritis): Dysmorphic red blood cells and red blood cell casts are specific markers of glomerular bleeding, reflecting red cell passage through damaged glomerular capillaries and tubules. Bladder tumors, stones, BPH, and renal cell carcinoma (B, C, D, E) typically cause nonglomerular bleeding with normal-appearing red cells and no casts.
  80. The correct answer is B (Stress incontinence, managed initially with pelvic floor muscle exercises): Leakage specifically with increased intra-abdominal pressure like coughing, without urgency, defines stress incontinence, and pelvic floor muscle exercises are first-line therapy. Urge incontinence (A) involves leakage with urgency, and the other options describe different incontinence types that do not match this presentation.
  81. The correct answer is D (Non-contrast CT of the abdomen and pelvis): Non-contrast CT is the preferred initial imaging study for suspected nephrolithiasis because it reliably detects stones of nearly all compositions and accurately measures size and location. Plain radiographs (A) miss radiolucent stones, ultrasound (B) is less sensitive for small stones, and MRI (C) and IVP (E) are not first-line for this indication.
  82. The correct answer is E (CT or MR angiography of the renal arteries): CT or MR angiography provides direct noninvasive visualization of the renal arteries and is appropriate for evaluating suspected renal artery stenosis in a young patient with resistant hypertension and flash pulmonary edema. The other tests (A, B, C, D) evaluate different endocrine or structural causes rather than directly imaging the renal arteries.
  83. The correct answer is A (Emergent hemodialysis for refractory, life-threatening hyperkalemia): When fluids fail to restore urine output in rhabdomyolysis-induced AKI and hyperkalemia is severe and worsening, emergent hemodialysis is required to rapidly and definitively remove potassium. Continuing fluids alone (B), diuretics (C), or bicarbonate alone (D) will not adequately correct life-threatening hyperkalemia, and observation (E) risks fatal arrhythmia.
  84. The correct answer is B (Hepatorenal syndrome given the low FeNa without volume response): A very low FeNa, bland sediment, and lack of response to albumin and diuretic withdrawal in a cirrhotic patient are the defining diagnostic criteria for hepatorenal syndrome.   85. The correct answer is C (Needle-shaped cholesterol clefts occluding small arterioles): The delayed presentation after catheterization with livedo reticularis, eosinophilia, and hypocomplementemia is classic for cholesterol embolization, and biopsy characteristically shows needle-shaped cholesterol clefts within occluded small vessels.   86.   87. The correct answer is A (Mycophenolate mofetil or cyclophosphamide with high-dose corticosteroids): Active proliferative class IV lupus nephritis requires potent induction immunosuppression, typically mycophenolate mofetil or cyclophosphamide combined with high-dose corticosteroids, to prevent irreversible renal damage. Hydroxychloroquine alone (B), NSAIDs (C), ACE inhibitors alone (D), and observation (E) are all insufficient for active proliferative disease.
  88. The correct answer is B (Initiation of effective combination antiretroviral therapy): HIV-associated collapsing FSGS improves substantially with effective antiretroviral therapy that suppresses viral replication, the driving factor behind the glomerular injury.   89. The correct answer is C (Age-appropriate malignancy screening for an underlying solid tumor): Membranous nephropathy in an older patient with negative PLA2R testing raises concern for secondary membranous nephropathy from an underlying malignancy, warranting age-appropriate cancer screening.   90. The correct answer is D (Post-streptococcal disease has a delayed latency and typically resolves, unlike recurrent IgA nephropathy): Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis characteristically occurs two to three weeks after infection and usually resolves completely, while IgA nephropathy causes recurrent hematuria with each infection and does not show low complement. Options A, B, C, and E each misstate the latency, complement findings, or clinical course that distinguish these two conditions.
  91. The correct answer is A (Plasmapheresis combined with corticosteroids and cyclophosphamide): Anti-GBM disease with pulmonary hemorrhage is a medical emergency requiring plasmapheresis to remove circulating antibody plus corticosteroids and cyclophosphamide to prevent further antibody production.   92. The correct answer is B (Adrenal vein sampling to confirm lateralization of aldosterone excess): Adrenal vein sampling is required before adrenalectomy because CT-visualized nodules do not reliably identify the source of aldosterone excess, and nonfunctioning incidentalomas are common.   93. The correct answer is C (Emergent urinary decompression with a ureteral stent or nephrostomy tube): Infected obstructed pyelonephritis with hemodynamic instability is a urologic emergency requiring immediate decompression to relieve the obstruction and control the source of infection, since antibiotics alone cannot penetrate an obstructed system effectively. The other options (A, B, D, E) delay definitive source control and risk progression to sepsis and renal loss.
  94. The correct answer is D (Struvite stones forming from urease-producing organisms alkalinizing urine): Proteus mirabilis produces urease, which alkalinizes urine and promotes precipitation of magnesium ammonium phosphate, forming large branching struvite staghorn calculi.   95. The correct answer is E (Match urine output with isotonic fluids while monitoring electrolytes closely): Post-obstructive diuresis can cause significant volume and electrolyte depletion, so fluid replacement should match output while closely monitoring electrolytes to avoid both under- and over-resuscitation. Restricting fluids (A) or ignoring the diuresis (C) risks hypovolemia and shock, diuretics (B) would worsen losses, and dialysis (D) is not indicated for this physiologic process.
  96. The correct answer is B (SGLT2 inhibitors added for kidney and cardiovascular protection): SGLT2 inhibitors have demonstrated renal and cardiovascular protective effects independent of glycemic control in patients with diabetic kidney disease already on ACE inhibitor therapy.   97. The correct answer is C (Screening with MR angiography given the positive family history of aneurysm): Intracranial aneurysm screening with MR angiography is recommended for ADPKD patients with a family history of aneurysm or subarachnoid hemorrhage due to their increased risk.   98. The correct answer is D (Concurrent metabolic alkalosis, high anion gap metabolic acidosis, and respiratory alkalosis): The near-normal pH with a low PaCO2, near-normal bicarbonate, and elevated anion gap indicates three simultaneous processes, an underlying metabolic alkalosis from vomiting, a high anion gap metabolic acidosis from sepsis, and a respiratory alkalosis, that together mask each other's effect on pH.   99.   100. The correct answer is A (Emergent hemodialysis to definitively remove excess potassium): Severe hyperkalemia with sine wave changes on EKG requires temporizing measures followed promptly by hemodialysis, the only definitive treatment that actually removes potassium from the body.   101. The correct answer is B (Focal seizure with impaired awareness featuring automatisms and postictal confusion): automatisms like lip smacking and hand fumbling with impaired awareness and postictal confusion are hallmarks of a focal seizure with impaired awareness, formerly called complex partial seizure. Generalized tonic-clonic seizures involve bilateral convulsive movements and loss of consciousness from onset. Absence seizures are brief staring spells without postictal confusion. Simple partial and myoclonic seizures do not typically cause automatisms with impaired awareness.
  102. The correct answer is D (Transient ischemic attack with full resolution and no infarct on imaging): a focal neurologic deficit that fully resolves within minutes to hours without imaging evidence of infarction defines a TIA.   103. The correct answer is A (Chronic hypertension, the leading modifiable risk factor for stroke overall): hypertension is the single most important modifiable risk factor for both ischemic and hemorrhagic stroke, and its treatment produces the largest reduction in stroke incidence. Homocysteine, low activity, mild alcohol use, and sleep apnea contribute to risk but carry far less population impact than hypertension. Controlling blood pressure remains the cornerstone of primary stroke prevention.
  104. The correct answer is C (Subarachnoid hemorrhage causing sudden maximal intensity headache within seconds): a sudden thunderclap headache reaching maximum intensity within seconds, especially with meningismus, is the classic presentation of subarachnoid hemorrhage from aneurysm rupture. Migraine and tension headaches build gradually rather than instantly. Bacterial meningitis usually has a more subacute onset with fever, and cluster headache is periorbital and recurrent rather than a single catastrophic event.
  105. The correct answer is E (Cavernous sinus thrombosis with bilateral spread of cranial nerve palsies): bilateral spread of ophthalmoplegia and multiple cranial neuropathies following sinus infection reflects septic thrombosis spreading through the cavernous sinus, which drains both orbits.   106. The correct answer is B (Unilateral throbbing headache accompanied by nausea and photophobia during attacks): this combination is the defining clinical picture of migraine. Bilateral pressure without associated symptoms suggests tension headache.   107. The correct answer is A (Severe unilateral periorbital pain with ipsilateral tearing, ptosis, and restlessness): cluster headache produces intense unilateral periorbital pain with autonomic features such as lacrimation, ptosis, and nasal congestion on the same side.
