  108. The correct answer is D (C6 nerve root, affecting the thumb, index finger, and wrist extension): the C6 nerve root supplies sensation to the thumb and index finger, innervates wrist extensors, and mediates the brachioradialis reflex, so deficits here localize to C6 radiculopathy. C4 affects shoulder sensation, C7 affects the middle finger and triceps reflex, C8 affects the little finger and finger flexors, and T1 affects intrinsic hand muscles, none matching this pattern.
  109. The correct answer is C (Lumbar disc herniation causing nerve root irritation and radicular leg pain): a positive straight leg raise reproducing radicular pain below the knee is a classic sign of nerve root tension from disc herniation, most commonly at L4-S1. Sacroiliac dysfunction and facet arthropathy cause more localized back pain without this radicular tension sign. Piriformis syndrome and compression fracture do not typically produce a positive straight leg raise in this manner.
  110. The correct answer is E (Lumbar spinal stenosis causing neurogenic claudication relieved by forward flexion): neurogenic claudication with bilateral leg discomfort relieved by forward flexion, which enlarges the spinal canal, is the hallmark of lumbar spinal stenosis. Peripheral arterial disease causes claudication relieved by rest regardless of posture. Deep venous thrombosis, hip osteoarthritis, and diabetic amyotrophy do not produce this posture-dependent relief pattern.
  111. The correct answer is A (Elevated cerebrospinal fluid protein with a normal white blood cell count): albuminocytologic dissociation, meaning high protein without a corresponding pleocytosis, is the classic CSF finding in Guillain-Barre syndrome. A high white count with low glucose suggests bacterial infection, xanthochromia suggests subarachnoid hemorrhage, and oligoclonal bands are associated with multiple sclerosis rather than GBS.
  112. The correct answer is B (Phalen maneuver, reproducing symptoms with one minute of wrist flexion): sustained wrist flexion reproducing median nerve symptoms is the Phalen maneuver, used to support a diagnosis of carpal tunnel syndrome. Tinel percussion involves tapping over the nerve rather than sustained flexion. Spurling test assesses cervical radiculopathy, Lasegue sign assesses lumbar nerve root irritation, and Froment sign evaluates ulnar nerve function.
  113. The correct answer is E (Symmetric distal sensory loss in a stocking-glove distribution over time): diabetic peripheral neuropathy typically produces a length-dependent, symmetric sensory loss starting distally in the feet and progressing proximally in a stocking-glove pattern. Asymmetric proximal weakness suggests diabetic amyotrophy, sudden foot drop suggests mononeuropathy, facial numbness is not typical, and single nerve root loss suggests radiculopathy rather than diffuse polyneuropathy.
  114. The correct answer is D (Postherpetic neuralgia persisting in the distribution of a healed shingles rash): persistent pain in a dermatomal distribution beyond three months after a herpes zoster outbreak defines postherpetic neuralgia. Trigeminal neuralgia is unrelated to prior rash and involves brief electric shock-like pain. Complex regional pain syndrome usually follows limb trauma, central post-stroke pain follows a cerebrovascular event, and fibromyalgia is diffuse rather than dermatomal.
  115. The correct answer is C (Peripheral facial nerve palsy involving the forehead and lower face equally): involvement of the forehead indicates a lower motor neuron lesion of the facial nerve itself, as seen in Bell palsy, because the forehead receives bilateral cortical input and is spared in central lesions. Cortical stroke and internal capsule infarcts spare the forehead. Multiple sclerosis and myasthenia gravis do not typically present with this classic peripheral facial pattern including forehead weakness.
  116. The correct answer is A (Obese woman of childbearing age with daily headache and visual blurring): idiopathic intracranial hypertension classically occurs in obese women of reproductive age and presents with daily headache, transient visual obscurations, and papilledema. The other demographic and symptom combinations describe different conditions such as subarachnoid hemorrhage, epilepsy, dementia, or meningitis rather than IIH.
  117. The correct answer is D (Administer intravenous thrombolytic therapy after confirming eligibility on imaging): within the appropriate treatment window and absent contraindications, intravenous thrombolysis is the guideline-recommended intervention to improve outcomes in acute ischemic stroke. Aspirin alone is insufficient as initial therapy in this window, heparin is not first-line acute therapy, and endarterectomy and statin initiation are not immediate interventions for hyperacute stroke.
  118. The correct answer is B (Obtain carotid imaging, an echocardiogram, and cardiac rhythm monitoring): identifying the source of a TIA requires evaluating for carotid stenosis, cardioembolic sources, and arrhythmias such as atrial fibrillation. Lumbar puncture and EEG address seizure or infectious causes, muscle biopsy addresses myopathy, and sleep or thyroid studies do not address the vascular and cardioembolic etiologies most relevant to TIA.
  119. The correct answer is E (Obtain a head CT scan and start empiric antibiotics immediately): focal deficits or papilledema raise concern for mass effect, so imaging should precede lumbar puncture to avoid herniation, while antibiotics should not be delayed for imaging or CSF results. Proceeding directly to LP risks herniation, and delaying antibiotics for any reason increases mortality in bacterial meningitis.
  120. The correct answer is C (A central cause requiring urgent evaluation for posterior circulation stroke): a normal head impulse test combined with direction-changing nystagmus is a red flag pattern on the HINTS exam suggesting a central lesion such as posterior circulation stroke, warranting further stroke workup. Peripheral causes like vestibular neuronitis and BPPV typically show an abnormal head impulse test with unidirectional nystagmus instead.
  121. The correct answer is A (Acetylcholine receptor antibody testing performed on a serum sample): this serologic test is the most specific and widely used confirmatory study for myasthenia gravis given the classic fatigable ocular and bulbar weakness. Nerve conduction studies and muscle biopsy evaluate peripheral nerve or muscle disease rather than the neuromuscular junction, and MRI or spinal fluid studies do not confirm this diagnosis.
  122. The correct answer is C (MRI of the brain evaluated for demyelinating white matter lesions): the presence and number of white matter lesions on brain MRI at the time of optic neuritis is the strongest predictor of subsequent conversion to multiple sclerosis. Visual evoked potentials and fundoscopy assess the optic nerve itself but not future MS risk, and antinuclear antibody or genetic testing are not established predictors in this setting.
  123. The correct answer is B (Thyroid function, vitamin B12, and metabolic panel with neuroimaging): a standard dementia workup screens for reversible contributors such as hypothyroidism, B12 deficiency, and metabolic derangements, alongside structural imaging to exclude mass lesions or hydrocephalus. Brain biopsy is invasive and not first-line, genetic testing is not routine, and starting medication or invasive CSF testing without basic screening skips essential initial steps.
  124. The correct answer is D (Carbidopa-levodopa, the most effective initial therapy for motor symptoms): levodopa combined with carbidopa remains the most effective initial therapy for significant motor symptoms in Parkinson disease. Diphenhydramine, haloperidol, and baclofen are not appropriate first-line Parkinson treatments, and haloperidol can actually worsen parkinsonism by blocking dopamine receptors. Amitriptyline has no established role in initial motor symptom control.
  125. The correct answer is E (Empiric intravenous antibiotics combined with consideration of surgical drainage): brain abscess management requires prompt intravenous antibiotics targeted to likely organisms plus neurosurgical drainage for larger or symptomatic lesions. Oral antibiotics alone are inadequate for this severity, observation delays necessary treatment, and steroids or anticonvulsants alone do not address the underlying infection.
  126. The correct answer is A (Spinal immobilization combined with urgent imaging of the entire spine): after trauma with suspected spinal cord injury, immediate immobilization and urgent imaging are essential to prevent further neurologic injury and guide management. Mobilization or discharge without evaluation risks catastrophic worsening, and long-term high-dose steroids are not recommended given an unfavorable risk-benefit profile in acute spinal cord injury.
  127. The correct answer is D (Beginning empiric intravenous acyclovir while confirmatory testing is pending): because untreated HSV encephalitis carries high mortality, empiric acyclovir should be started immediately based on clinical suspicion rather than delayed for PCR confirmation. Antibiotics and antifungals do not treat viral encephalitis, and steroids alone do not address the underlying infection.
  128. The correct answer is C (Creatine kinase, electromyography, and muscle biopsy performed together): this combination of elevated muscle enzymes, characteristic electromyographic changes, and biopsy findings of inflammatory infiltrate establishes the diagnosis of polymyositis. Nerve conduction studies alone assess peripheral nerves rather than muscle, and chest imaging, thyroid testing, or lumbar puncture do not evaluate primary muscle pathology.
  129. The correct answer is B (Postictal Todd paralysis occurring as a known consequence of seizures): transient focal weakness following a seizure that resolves within hours is characteristic of Todd paralysis rather than a new stroke. Recurrent TIA and cardioembolic stroke would not typically be temporally tied to a seizure in this way, and conversion disorder or hypoglycemia do not explain the clear postictal timing described.
  130. The correct answer is E (Bilateral spread of infection through shared venous sinus communication): progression to the contralateral eye occurs because the cavernous sinuses communicate through shared venous channels, allowing infection to spread across the midline, a feature not seen in simple orbital cellulitis. Fever, eyelid swelling, sinus tenderness, and leukocytosis can all occur in ordinary orbital cellulitis and do not distinguish the two conditions.
  131. The correct answer is C (Cauda equina syndrome requiring emergent imaging and surgical decompression): saddle anesthesia, urinary retention, and bilateral leg weakness are red flag findings for cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency requiring urgent imaging and decompression to prevent permanent deficits. Lumbar strain, sacroiliac dysfunction, piriformis syndrome, and facet arthropathy do not cause this combination of bowel, bladder, and bilateral neurologic findings.
  132. The correct answer is A (Prominent fatigable ocular and bulbar weakness occurring without sensory loss): myasthenic crisis characteristically spares sensation and produces fluctuating, fatigable weakness concentrated in ocular and bulbar muscles, unlike the ascending, areflexic, sensory-involved pattern of Guillain-Barre syndrome. Antecedent infection and absent reflexes are more typical of GBS, and elevated CSF protein is a GBS finding rather than a myasthenia finding.
  133. The correct answer is E (Acute onset with a fluctuating course and impaired attention): delirium is defined by an acute change in mental status with a fluctuating course and impaired attention, distinguishing it from the gradual, stable decline of underlying dementia. Chronic progression and a stable baseline describe dementia rather than delirium, and preserved attention or absence of illness would argue against delirium entirely.
  134. The correct answer is D (Tremor prominent with action and posture rather than at rest): essential tremor characteristically worsens with voluntary movement and sustained posture and improves at rest, opposite to the resting tremor of Parkinson disease. Cogwheel rigidity, shuffling gait, asymmetric resting tremor, and masked facies are all features of Parkinson disease rather than essential tremor.
  135. The correct answer is B (Lumbar puncture performed to look for cerebrospinal fluid xanthochromia): a small subarachnoid hemorrhage can be missed on CT after the first few hours, so lumbar puncture looking for xanthochromia is the appropriate next step to definitively exclude the diagnosis. Discharge, repeat imaging in a week, migraine prophylaxis, and spine MRI all fail to promptly address the ongoing risk of a missed aneurysmal bleed.
  136. The correct answer is A (Vestibular neuronitis causing acute peripheral vestibular dysfunction and vertigo): an abnormal head impulse test with unidirectional nystagmus and no skew deviation is the expected peripheral pattern on the HINTS exam, consistent with vestibular neuronitis rather than stroke. Cerebellar infarction, lateral medullary syndrome, basilar occlusion, and vertebral dissection are central causes that typically produce a normal head impulse test with direction-changing nystagmus instead.
  137. The correct answer is C (A normal creatine kinase level despite significant muscle weakness): steroid-induced myopathy classically causes proximal weakness without pain and with a normal creatine kinase, unlike statin myopathy which often presents with muscle pain and elevated creatine kinase. Presence of weakness, symmetric distribution, and improvement after stopping the drug can occur with either condition and do not reliably distinguish them.
  138. The correct answer is D (Dissemination of demyelinating lesions in both time and anatomic space): separate clinical episodes affecting different neurologic sites at different points in time, supported by MRI lesions of different ages in different locations, satisfies the core diagnostic principle of dissemination in time and space used for multiple sclerosis. Wallerian degeneration, watershed infarction, and length-dependent axonal loss describe unrelated pathologic processes, and Uhthoff phenomenon alone refers to heat-related symptom worsening, not diagnostic criteria.
  139. The correct answer is E (Elevated opening pressure on lumbar puncture with normal fluid composition): confirming idiopathic intracranial hypertension requires demonstrating elevated cerebrospinal fluid opening pressure with otherwise normal fluid studies, after a mass lesion has been excluded by imaging. Elevated white cells, low glucose, oligoclonal bands, and positive blood cultures would instead suggest infectious or inflammatory causes rather than IIH.
  140. The correct answer is B (Epidural hematoma causing a lens-shaped collection confined by suture lines): a lucid interval followed by rapid deterioration and a lens-shaped hyperdensity confined by suture lines is the classic presentation of epidural hematoma, typically from middle meningeal artery injury. Subdural hematoma instead crosses suture lines and appears crescent-shaped. Diffuse axonal injury, subarachnoid hemorrhage, and contusion do not produce this specific imaging and clinical pattern.
  141. The correct answer is C (Lichen sclerosus causing thin white atrophic vulvar and perianal plaques): the classic 'cigarette paper' or figure-of-eight white atrophic plaques around the vulva and anus in an older woman are characteristic of lichen sclerosus. Erosive lichen planus typically involves painful vaginal erosions, candidiasis causes erythema with satellite lesions rather than atrophic white plaques, Paget disease presents as a red weeping plaque, and vulvar intraepithelial neoplasia usually appears pigmented or raised rather than thin and white.
  142. The correct answer is A (Continue breastfeeding on the affected side and start oral dicloxacillin): lactational mastitis is treated with continued breastfeeding or milk removal alongside a penicillinase-resistant penicillin or similar antibiotic, since milk stasis worsens infection and stopping feeding is unnecessary and unhelpful. Vancomycin and stopping breastfeeding are excessive for typical mastitis, MRI is not indicated without concerning features for malignancy, and drainage is reserved for abscess formation, not simple mastitis.
  143. The correct answer is E (Meeting two of three Rotterdam criteria confirms polycystic ovary syndrome): the Rotterdam criteria require two of three features, oligo/anovulation, clinical or biochemical hyperandrogenism, and polycystic ovaries on ultrasound, which this patient meets with irregular cycles, hirsutism/acne, and polycystic ovarian morphology. Elevated FSH with low estradiol suggests ovarian failure, elevated 17-hydroxyprogesterone points to adrenal hyperplasia, a low LH:FSH ratio suggests hypothalamic amenorrhea, and elevated prolactin suggests a pituitary adenoma, none of which fit this normal-prolactin, normal-thyroid presentation.
  144. The correct answer is B (Routine follow-up transvaginal ultrasound in six to twelve weeks): a simple, unilocular, anechoic cyst under 5 to 7 cm in a premenopausal woman is almost always a functional cyst and can be safely followed with repeat imaging rather than intervention. Surgery, oncology referral, and needle aspiration are unnecessary for a clearly benign-appearing simple cyst, and oral contraceptives do not reliably shrink existing cysts.
  145. The correct answer is D (Uterine leiomyomas distorting the uterine contour and enlarging the uterus): heavy bleeding with an enlarged, irregularly contoured, nontender uterus is the classic presentation of uterine fibroids.   146. The correct answer is A (Endometriosis causing cyclic pelvic pain, dyspareunia, and dysmenorrhea): the combination of severe dysmenorrhea, deep dyspareunia, and cyclic pelvic pain with cul-de-sac and uterosacral tenderness is the classic triad of endometriosis. Primary dysmenorrhea lacks the deep dyspareunia and exam findings, pelvic inflammatory disease typically presents with fever and cervical motion tenderness, and interstitial cystitis or irritable bowel syndrome do not explain the uterosacral nodularity found on exam.
  147. The correct answer is C (Transvaginal ultrasound or endometrial biopsy to evaluate the endometrial lining): any postmenopausal bleeding warrants prompt evaluation of the endometrium to exclude endometrial cancer, especially in a woman with obesity and diabetes, both established risk factors.   148. The correct answer is E (Co-testing with Pap cytology and HPV testing every five years): for average-risk women aged 30 to 65, co-testing with cytology and HPV every five years, or HPV testing alone every five years, is a preferred current screening strategy. Annual cytology or HPV testing alone yearly is more frequent than recommended, cytology alone every ten years is too infrequent, and screening should not stop simply because she is under 40.
  149.   150.   151. The correct answer is E (Propylthiouracil during the first trimester, switching to methimazole afterward): propylthiouracil is preferred in the first trimester because methimazole carries a higher risk of specific birth defects during organogenesis, then switching to methimazole after the first trimester reduces the risk of propylthiouracil-associated liver toxicity. Continuing methimazole throughout, using propylthiouracil for the whole pregnancy, radioactive iodine, and beta blockers alone are all inappropriate or unsafe choices.
  152. The correct answer is D (An ectopic pregnancy that must be excluded before other diagnoses): a positive pregnancy test with pelvic pain, spotting, and a history of pelvic inflammatory disease without a confirmed intrauterine pregnancy is a classic ectopic pregnancy presentation that must always be ruled out first given its potential for rupture and hemorrhage. Threatened abortion, cyst rupture, molar pregnancy, and round ligament pain are less urgent considerations that should not be assumed until ectopic pregnancy is excluded.
  153. The correct answer is A (A urine or serum pregnancy test to exclude pregnancy first): in any reproductive-age woman with abnormal uterine bleeding, pregnancy must be excluded first because it changes the entire diagnostic and management pathway, including safety of imaging and biopsy. Ultrasound, biopsy, coagulation studies, and CA-125 are all reasonable subsequent steps but should follow confirmation of pregnancy status.
  154. The correct answer is C (Avoid systemic hormone therapy given her prior thromboembolism history): a personal history of venous thromboembolism is a contraindication to systemic estrogen-containing hormone therapy because it further increases clot risk. Starting combined or estrogen-only therapy, using high-dose transdermal estrogen, or pairing therapy with anticoagulation all inappropriately expose her to unnecessary thrombotic risk when nonhormonal alternatives exist.
  155. The correct answer is B (A progestin-only pill or an etonogestrel subdermal implant): progestin-only methods do not carry the theoretical concerns about reduced milk supply associated with estrogen and are considered safe and effective during breastfeeding. Combined pills, the vaginal ring, and the patch all contain estrogen and are generally avoided in early lactation, while fertility awareness methods are unreliable in the early postpartum period with irregular cycling.
  156. The correct answer is D (Nitrofurantoin is best avoided close to delivery due to hemolysis risk): nitrofurantoin used near term carries a theoretical risk of neonatal hemolysis, particularly in glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase deficient infants, so alternative agents are often preferred close to delivery. Fluoroquinolones are generally avoided in pregnancy due to cartilage toxicity concerns, sulfa drugs carry kernicterus risk near term, tetracyclines affect fetal bone and teeth throughout pregnancy, and untreated bacteriuria in pregnancy requires treatment.
  157. The correct answer is A (Peripartum cardiomyopathy presenting with new left ventricular systolic dysfunction): new heart failure symptoms with a newly reduced ejection fraction arising in the last month of pregnancy through several months postpartum, without prior cardiac disease, defines peripartum cardiomyopathy. Amniotic fluid embolism presents acutely at delivery with collapse, pulmonary embolism causes right heart strain rather than reduced left ventricular function, a preexisting cardiomyopathy would have prior history, and postpartum thyroiditis causes high-output rather than low ejection fraction failure.
  158. The correct answer is C (Between 24 and 28 weeks gestation using a glucose challenge test): universal screening for gestational diabetes with a 50 gram glucose challenge test is standard practice between 24 and 28 weeks gestation in average-risk women. Screening at the first visit is reserved for women with significant risk factors to detect preexisting diabetes, waiting for symptoms or screening postpartum misses the window for intervention, and 36 weeks is too late for the standard screening test.
  159. The correct answer is E (Azithromycin given as a single one gram oral dose): azithromycin as a single dose is the recommended first-line treatment for chlamydia in pregnancy because doxycycline and tetracyclines are avoided due to fetal bone and teeth effects, and fluoroquinolones like ofloxacin and levofloxacin are generally avoided in pregnancy. This makes azithromycin both effective and the safest recommended option in this clinical setting.
  160. The correct answer is B (Scheduled NSAIDs started at the onset of menstrual symptoms): NSAIDs are first-line therapy for primary dysmenorrhea because they reduce prostaglandin-mediated uterine cramping and are effective for most patients when started at symptom onset. Endometrial ablation and GnRH agonists are reserved for refractory cases or specific pathology, diagnostic laparoscopy is not needed as an initial step in typical primary dysmenorrhea, and opioids are inappropriate first-line therapy for this condition.
  161. The correct answer is D (Triple assessment combining exam, imaging, and core needle biopsy): the standard evaluation of a palpable breast mass combines clinical examination, imaging such as mammography and ultrasound, and core needle biopsy for tissue diagnosis, since no single component reliably excludes cancer alone. Reassurance based on exam features alone is unsafe, MRI alone is not the standard initial test, prolonged observation delays diagnosis, and biopsy without prior imaging loses valuable localization and characterization information.
  162. The correct answer is A (Starting intravenous magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis right away): magnesium sulfate is the standard of care for seizure prophylaxis in preeclampsia with severe features, which this patient has given her severe blood pressure elevation, refractory headache, and thrombocytopenia. Nifedipine alone treats blood pressure but not seizure risk, aspirin is used for prevention earlier in pregnancy rather than acute treatment, furosemide is not indicated for cerebral edema in this context, and levetiracetam is not the standard agent for eclampsia prophylaxis.
  163. The correct answer is C (Acute fatty liver of pregnancy causing hypoglycemia and coagulopathy): the combination of hypoglycemia, markedly elevated ammonia, and coagulopathy with only mildly elevated transaminases and normal platelets favors acute fatty liver of pregnancy over HELLP syndrome, which typically shows more prominent hemolysis, elevated liver enzymes, and thrombocytopenia. Cholestasis causes pruritus without liver failure, viral hepatitis does not typically cause this coagulopathy and hypoglycemia pattern, and gallstone pancreatitis or Budd-Chiari syndrome do not fit this specific metabolic picture.
  164. Repeating imaging in three months delays necessary care, fine-needle aspiration risks tumor seeding and is not standard, empiric chemotherapy without tissue diagnosis is inappropriate, and CA-125 elevation in this clinical context should not be dismissed as false positive.
  165.   166.   167. Laparotomy and salpingostomy are more invasive than needed for a candidate suitable for medical therapy, expectant management alone is less reliable than active treatment at this hCG level, and dilation and curettage would not appropriately manage a confirmed ectopic pregnancy.
  169. The correct answer is B (Benefits generally outweigh risks when started within ten years of menopause): current evidence supports the 'timing hypothesis,' where hormone therapy started within ten years of menopause or before age 60 generally has a favorable risk-benefit profile for symptom relief. Risks do not uniformly outweigh benefits for all ages, therapy is not reserved only for women over 65, benefits are not identical regardless of when therapy starts, and nonhormonal therapy is not universally preferred over hormone therapy in appropriate candidates.
  170. The correct answer is E (Migraine with aura combined with smoking raises her stroke risk): migraine with aura combined with smoking, especially in a woman over 35, substantially increases the risk of ischemic stroke with estrogen-containing contraceptives, making combined hormonal methods contraindicated. Age alone does not preclude hormonal contraception, migraine without aura is not an absolute contraindication, smoking clearly does increase estrogen-related vascular risk, and progestin-only methods do not carry the same elevated stroke risk as combined estrogen-containing methods.
  171. The correct answer is D (Small cell lung carcinoma frequently secretes ectopic antidiuretic hormone causing this syndrome): Small cell lung carcinoma arises from neuroendocrine cells and frequently secretes ectopic antidiuretic hormone, producing SIADH with hyponatremia and concentrated urine. Adenocarcinoma and squamous cell carcinoma are not typically associated with this paraneoplastic pattern. Large cell neuroendocrine carcinoma can rarely cause paraneoplastic syndromes but small cell is the classic association. Bronchioloalveolar carcinoma, a subtype of adenocarcinoma, is not linked to SIADH.
  172.   173.   187. The correct answer is B (Identification of the large binucleate Reed-Sternberg cell confirms this diagnosis on histology): The presence of Reed-Sternberg cells, large binucleate cells with prominent nucleoli, on lymph node biopsy is diagnostic of classic Hodgkin lymphoma. Acute promyelocytic leukemia is characterized by a t(15;17) translocation. Acute lymphoblastic leukemia, hairy cell leukemia, and chronic lymphocytic leukemia are not defined by the BCR-ABL fusion.
  191.   192. The correct answer is C (Tumor lysis syndrome from rapid malignant cell breakdown describes this exact laboratory pattern): Tumor lysis syndrome occurs when rapid cell death releases intracellular contents, causing hyperkalemia, hyperphosphatemia, secondary hypocalcemia, and hyperuricemia, typically after treatment of bulky, chemosensitive tumors. SIADH causes hyponatremia rather than this electrolyte pattern. Hypercalcemia of malignancy causes elevated rather than low calcium. Disseminated intravascular coagulation primarily affects coagulation parameters, and checkpoint inhibitor adrenal insufficiency presents with different findings such as hyponatremia and hypotension.
  193. The correct answer is B (Tumor secretion of parathyroid hormone related peptide best explains this humoral hypercalcemia mechanism): Squamous cell carcinomas commonly cause humoral hypercalcemia of malignancy through ectopic secretion of parathyroid hormone related peptide, which mimics PTH action on bone and kidney. Direct osteolytic invasion is more typical of bone metastases from breast cancer or myeloma rather than this humoral mechanism. Ectopic vitamin D activation is characteristic of lymphoma, not squamous cell lung cancer. Calcitonin excess and interleukin-6 alone are not the primary mechanisms in this scenario.
  194. The correct answer is C (Smoking cessation counseling combined with pharmacotherapy has the greatest impact on future cancer risk): Smoking cessation is the single most effective intervention to reduce future cancer risk in smokers, and counseling combined with pharmacotherapy significantly improves quit rates. Annual chest x-ray and sputum cytology have not been shown to reduce lung cancer mortality and are not recommended screening tools. Multivitamin supplementation and increased fiber intake have not demonstrated significant impact on cancer risk reduction in this context.
  195. The correct answer is E (CT-guided percutaneous or bronchoscopic biopsy is the most appropriate next step for tissue diagnosis): A suspicious lung nodule without evidence of distant spread requires tissue sampling via image guided or bronchoscopic biopsy to confirm malignancy and guide treatment planning. Empiric radiation without a diagnosis is inappropriate before histologic confirmation. Repeat imaging in 12 months would delay diagnosis in a nodule with concerning features. Tumor markers and PET alone cannot provide definitive tissue diagnosis.
  196. The correct answer is E (Core needle biopsy of the mass is the appropriate next diagnostic step before treatment): A suspicious breast mass with concerning mammographic features requires core needle biopsy to obtain tissue diagnosis before any treatment decisions are made. Mastectomy without confirmed pathology is inappropriate. Clinical follow-up alone would delay diagnosis of a likely malignant lesion. MRI can supplement staging but does not replace tissue diagnosis, and empiric hormonal therapy is not appropriate without a confirmed diagnosis.
  197. The correct answer is A (The Bethesda system for thyroid cytopathology categorizes results and guides this specific decision): The Bethesda system standardizes thyroid fine needle aspiration cytology results into diagnostic categories that guide clinical management, including repeat biopsy or surgical referral for indeterminate results. Gleason grading applies to prostate cancer, Fuhrman grading to renal cell carcinoma, Nottingham grading to breast cancer, and Bismuth-Corlette classification to cholangiocarcinoma location, none of which apply to thyroid cytology.
  198. The correct answer is D (The next screening colonoscopy should be performed in ten years for this patient): For an average risk patient with a completely normal screening colonoscopy, guidelines recommend repeating colonoscopy in 10 years. One and 3 year intervals are used for patients with polyps or other higher risk findings. A 15 year interval is longer than standard guidelines recommend, and stopping screening entirely after one normal exam is not appropriate for an average risk patient still within the recommended screening age range.
  199. The correct answer is C (Endoscopic ultrasound with fine needle aspiration best obtains tissue while assessing vascular involvement): Endoscopic ultrasound allows close visualization of the pancreas and surrounding vasculature, enabling safe fine needle aspiration of pancreatic head masses while assessing vascular involvement for staging. Percutaneous approaches are less favorable near major vessels and carry risk of tumor seeding. Diagnostic laparoscopy without sampling does not provide tissue diagnosis, and blind percutaneous aspiration lacks the precision needed near critical structures. Repeat imaging alone delays necessary tissue diagnosis.
  200. The correct answer is E (Ultrasound combined with alpha-fetoprotein testing every six months is the recommended surveillance strategy): Patients with cirrhosis are recommended to undergo hepatocellular carcinoma surveillance with abdominal ultrasound and alpha-fetoprotein testing every 6 months to detect early, potentially curable tumors. Annual CT and 5 year MRI intervals are too infrequent or resource intensive for standard surveillance. Liver biopsy every 6 months is invasive and not the surveillance standard. Checking liver enzymes alone does not reliably detect early hepatocellular carcinoma.
  201. The correct answer is B (Triple phase contrast enhanced CT of the abdomen is most useful to characterize this lesion): Triple phase CT allows characterization of renal masses by evaluating enhancement patterns across different vascular phases, helping distinguish solid enhancing renal cell carcinoma from benign cystic lesions. Plain radiographs and skeletal surveys do not adequately characterize renal masses. Ultrasound alone and non-contrast CT lack the enhancement information needed to fully characterize a solid renal lesion.
  202. The correct answer is A (Radical inguinal orchiectomy is the appropriate next management step avoiding transscrotal tumor seeding): Suspected testicular cancer is managed with radical inguinal orchiectomy, which provides both diagnosis and initial treatment while avoiding the scrotal violation and tumor seeding risk associated with transscrotal biopsy. Percutaneous or incisional biopsy through the scrotum risks local tumor spread and altered lymphatic drainage. Observation delays necessary treatment, and empiric chemotherapy without tissue diagnosis is inappropriate.
  203. The correct answer is D (Referral to a gynecologic oncologist for surgical staging is the most appropriate next step): A complex adnexal mass with ascites and markedly elevated CA-125 is highly suspicious for ovarian malignancy and warrants prompt referral to a gynecologic oncologist for appropriate surgical staging and management. Oral contraceptive trials and repeat testing in a year would inappropriately delay diagnosis and treatment. Ultrasound alone without further workup and empiric antibiotics do not address the underlying malignant process suggested by these findings.
  204. The correct answer is A (A full thickness excisional biopsy with narrow margins is preferred to obtain accurate depth): Full thickness excisional biopsy is preferred for suspicious pigmented lesions because it allows accurate measurement of Breslow depth, which is critical for melanoma staging and prognosis. Shave biopsy can transect the base of the lesion and compromise depth measurement. Fine needle aspiration and curettage do not preserve tissue architecture needed for depth assessment, and a partial punch biopsy may sample a nonrepresentative area of the lesion.
  205. The correct answer is B (Administering empiric broad spectrum antibiotics within one hour is the most appropriate immediate step): Febrile neutropenia is a medical emergency requiring prompt empiric broad spectrum antibiotics within one hour of presentation to reduce mortality from potentially rapidly progressive infection. Waiting for culture results before treatment risks clinical deterioration. Discharge with oral antibiotics is only appropriate for carefully selected low risk patients, not universally. Growth factor alone without antibiotics does not address active infection, and outpatient imaging should not delay antibiotic administration.
  206. The correct answer is D (Giving urgent high dose dexamethasone while arranging an emergent spine MRI is most appropriate): New neurologic deficits in a patient with known metastatic cancer suggest spinal cord compression, an oncologic emergency requiring immediate corticosteroids to reduce cord edema and emergent MRI to confirm the diagnosis and guide urgent treatment. Outpatient imaging within a week is far too delayed. Opioids alone and physical therapy do not address the underlying compression, and bone scan is not the appropriate initial emergent imaging study.
  207. The correct answer is C (Therapeutic thoracentesis with the fluid sent for cytology is the most appropriate initial intervention): A large symptomatic pleural effusion in a patient with known metastatic cancer should be drained by thoracentesis both to relieve dyspnea and to confirm malignant cytology. Diuretics alone will not adequately drain a malignant effusion. Empiric antibiotics are not indicated without evidence of infection, and placing a chest tube without first analyzing the fluid skips important diagnostic information. Observation with delayed imaging leaves significant dyspnea untreated.
  208. The correct answer is C (A prophylactic multi-drug antiemetic regimen given before chemotherapy is the most appropriate approach): Highly emetogenic regimens require prophylactic combination antiemetic therapy, typically including a serotonin antagonist, corticosteroid, and neurokinin-1 antagonist, given before chemotherapy to prevent nausea and vomiting. Reactive treatment after symptoms occur is far less effective than prevention. Dietary changes alone and a single antihistamine dose are inadequate for highly emetogenic regimens, and opioids are not primary antiemetic agents.
  209. The correct answer is A (A baseline echocardiogram measuring left ventricular ejection fraction should be obtained before treatment): Anthracyclines such as doxorubicin carry a dose dependent risk of cardiotoxicity, so baseline and periodic echocardiographic assessment of ejection fraction is recommended to monitor cardiac function during treatment. Pulmonary function tests are more relevant to bleomycin toxicity. Abdominal ultrasound, thyroid function panels, and renal ultrasound do not assess the cardiac risk specific to anthracycline therapy.
  210. The correct answer is E (Holding the checkpoint inhibitor and starting corticosteroids is the appropriate management for this colitis): Immune related colitis from checkpoint inhibitor therapy is managed by holding the offending agent and initiating corticosteroids to suppress the immune mediated inflammation. Continuing or increasing the checkpoint inhibitor dose would worsen the immune toxicity. Empiric antibiotics do not address an immune mediated process once infection is excluded, and switching to another checkpoint inhibitor risks a similar or worse reaction before the current toxicity resolves.
  211. The correct answer is E (Complete decongestive therapy combined with compression garments is the first line management approach): First line management of lymphedema after axillary dissection includes complete decongestive therapy, combining manual lymphatic drainage, compression bandaging, and garments, along with exercise and skin care. Surgical lymph node transfer is reserved for refractory cases, not first line therapy. Diuretics are generally ineffective for lymphedema since it is a protein rich fluid accumulation, and amputation or strict immobilization are not appropriate management strategies.
  212. The correct answer is C (Immunohistochemical staining of the biopsy specimen is the most appropriate next diagnostic step): When metastatic adenocarcinoma is found without an identifiable primary tumor, immunohistochemical staining of the biopsy helps narrow the likely tissue of origin and guide targeted treatment decisions. Empiric chemotherapy without further characterization is less tailored to the specific tumor type. Whole body radiation is not standard management for cancer of unknown primary, and repeating the same biopsy or simply observing does not add diagnostic information.
  213. The correct answer is E (PET/CT for whole body staging is the most important additional study before surgery): PET/CT is recommended before curative intent lung cancer surgery to detect occult mediastinal nodal involvement or distant metastases not visible on CT alone, which would change management. Repeat chest CT alone does not add functional metabolic information. Bone scan and ventilation perfusion scan address more limited questions and do not provide comprehensive whole body staging, and abdominal ultrasound alone is insufficient for full staging.
  214. The correct answer is B (This finding supports adding trastuzumab, which targets the HER2 receptor, to her regimen): Amplification of the HER2 growth factor receptor gene identifies patients who benefit from HER2 targeted therapy such as trastuzumab, added to standard treatment. Tamoxifen and aromatase inhibitors target hormone receptor positive disease, not HER2 amplification. Bevacizumab targets VEGF and is not standard for HER2 positive breast cancer, and imatinib targets BCR-ABL, relevant to chronic myeloid leukemia rather than breast cancer.
  215. The correct answer is C (HPV positive status is associated with a significantly better prognosis than HPV negative disease): HPV positive oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma has a notably better response to treatment and improved overall survival compared with HPV negative disease, which is often driven by tobacco and alcohol use. Identical prognosis, more distant metastases, and poor chemoradiation response are not accurate descriptions of HPV positive tumors. HPV status is clinically relevant beyond cervical cancer, including in oropharyngeal cancer prognosis.
  216. The correct answer is A (Endoscopic ultrasound combined with PET/CT best assesses depth, nodal status, and distant spread): Endoscopic ultrasound provides detailed assessment of tumor depth and regional nodal involvement, while PET/CT detects distant metastatic disease, together forming the standard staging workup for esophageal adenocarcinoma before treatment decisions. Barium swallow and chest x-ray with abdominal ultrasound lack the resolution needed for accurate staging. Endoscopy alone identifies the tumor but not depth of invasion or distant spread, and non-contrast CT alone is insufficient for complete staging.
  217. The correct answer is D (This degree of vascular encasement makes the tumor unresectable, so surgery is not offered upfront): Greater than 180 degrees of encasement of the superior mesenteric artery indicates locally advanced, unresectable disease by standard criteria, prompting consideration of systemic therapy or chemoradiation rather than upfront surgery. This finding is highly relevant to resectability, contrary to having no bearing on it. It does not confirm liver metastasis, does not mandate emergency surgery, and does not contraindicate chemotherapy, which remains an appropriate treatment option.
  218. The correct answer is E (Surgical resection of the liver metastases offers the best chance of long term survival): Patients with colorectal cancer and limited, resectable liver metastases with adequate remnant liver function can achieve long term survival, including potential cure, with surgical resection, often combined with perioperative chemotherapy. Palliative chemotherapy alone offers lower survival potential than resection in this favorable oligometastatic scenario. Whole liver radiation and observation are not standard approaches, and liver transplantation is not first line therapy for colorectal liver metastases.
  219. The correct answer is C (Active surveillance with periodic PSA testing and biopsy is the most appropriate management strategy): Low risk, low volume prostate cancer is appropriately managed with active surveillance, involving regular PSA monitoring and repeat biopsies, to avoid overtreatment while detecting any progression that would prompt definitive therapy. Immediate prostatectomy, chemotherapy, orchiectomy, and radiation without further evaluation all represent overtreatment for indolent, low risk disease and carry unnecessary treatment related morbidity.
  220. The correct answer is C (The sunburst pattern suggests osteosarcoma, while the onion skin pattern suggests Ewing sarcoma): A sunburst periosteal reaction with a metaphyseal location is classic for osteosarcoma, while an onion skin, layered periosteal reaction in a diaphyseal location is characteristic of Ewing sarcoma. The other answer choices reverse or misattribute these classic radiographic patterns, and chondrosarcoma and benign osteochondroma do not typically present with either described pattern.
  221. The correct answer is E (An empiric toxoplasmosis treatment trial with imaging response assessment most often distinguishes these entities): In HIV patients with low CD4 counts and a brain mass, empiric antitoxoplasmosis therapy with clinical and radiographic reassessment is standard, since toxoplasmosis is more common and lesions that fail to respond raise suspicion for primary CNS lymphoma, prompting biopsy. Lumbar puncture alone without imaging follow-up, serum tumor markers, chest x-ray, and unrelated skin biopsy do not reliably differentiate between these two diagnoses.
  222. The correct answer is A (Cerebrospinal fluid cytology obtained from a lumbar puncture is most useful to confirm this diagnosis): Carcinomatous meningitis is diagnosed by finding malignant cells on cerebrospinal fluid cytology obtained through lumbar puncture, especially when brain MRI is nonfocal but symptoms suggest leptomeningeal involvement. Repeat MRI without contrast, EEG, serum tumor markers alone, and visual evoked potentials do not directly confirm leptomeningeal spread of malignant cells.
  223. The correct answer is C (Emergent radiation or stenting should proceed without delay even before a biopsy is obtained): When superior vena cava syndrome causes life threatening airway compromise or severe cerebral edema, emergent treatment such as radiation or venous stenting should not be delayed for tissue diagnosis, unlike the usual approach of obtaining biopsy first in stable patients. Requiring biopsy before any treatment in this emergent scenario risks patient deterioration. Chemotherapy can still be used in appropriate cases, observation is inadequate, and anticoagulation alone does not relieve the airway obstruction.
  224. The correct answer is B (Pain that precedes a neurologic deficit is a classic early warning sign of cord compression): Back pain that worsens when supine, especially in a patient with known metastatic disease, is a classic early symptom of spinal cord compression that often precedes neurologic deficits, making urgent imaging essential to prevent permanent damage. Dismissing this pain as insignificant or purely musculoskeletal risks delayed diagnosis, and waiting until paralysis develops would result in irreversible harm. This presentation does not rule out spinal metastasis.
  225. The correct answer is D (Watchful waiting with periodic clinical monitoring is the most appropriate initial management approach): Early stage, asymptomatic chronic lymphocytic leukemia without cytopenias or bulky disease is managed with watchful waiting, since early treatment has not been shown to improve survival in this population. Immediate chemoimmunotherapy, upfront transplant, splenectomy, and radiation to all nodal regions represent overtreatment for asymptomatic early stage disease and are not standard initial management.
  226. The correct answer is A (Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome fits this pattern of weakness improving with repeated activity): Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome is a paraneoplastic disorder strongly associated with small cell lung cancer, characterized by proximal muscle weakness that improves with repeated activity, in contrast to myasthenia gravis where weakness worsens with repetition. Paraneoplastic cerebellar degeneration causes ataxia rather than this specific weakness pattern. Guillain-Barre syndrome is typically postinfectious rather than paraneoplastic, and polymyositis and myasthenia gravis with thymoma do not show this improvement with repeated activity.
  227. The correct answer is B (This positive finding strongly supports a diagnosis of primary CNS lymphoma over other causes): Detection of Epstein-Barr virus DNA in cerebrospinal fluid strongly supports a diagnosis of primary CNS lymphoma in HIV patients with low CD4 counts, since these lymphomas are typically EBV driven. Toxoplasma encephalitis, progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy, cryptococcal meningitis, and CNS tuberculoma are not associated with positive EBV DNA in cerebrospinal fluid.
  228. The correct answer is E (This pattern is most suggestive of a TP53 mutation causing Li-Fraumeni syndrome): The combination of sarcoma, breast cancer, brain tumors, and adrenocortical carcinoma occurring at young ages across generations is the classic pattern of Li-Fraumeni syndrome, caused by germline TP53 mutations. APC mutations cause colonic polyposis and colorectal cancer, not this pattern. MLH1 mutations cause Lynch syndrome with colorectal and endometrial cancer. VHL mutations cause renal cell carcinoma and hemangioblastomas, and RET mutations cause medullary thyroid carcinoma and related endocrine tumors.
  229. The correct answer is A (Surveillance should specifically target secondary breast cancer and premature coronary artery disease risk): Survivors of chest radiation for Hodgkin lymphoma are at increased long term risk for secondary breast cancer and accelerated coronary artery disease, warranting targeted surveillance such as earlier breast imaging and cardiovascular risk assessment. Peripheral neuropathy and hearing loss relate more to certain chemotherapy agents than chest radiation. Chronic kidney disease, isolated osteoporosis, and peptic ulcer disease are not the primary late effects associated with mantle field radiation.
  230. The correct answer is D (Combined chemoradiation using the Nigro protocol is the standard first line treatment approach): Localized anal squamous cell carcinoma is standardly treated with combined chemoradiation, historically termed the Nigro protocol, which achieves high cure rates while preserving anal sphincter function and avoiding the need for abdominoperineal resection in most patients. Upfront surgical resection, chemotherapy alone, observation, and radiation alone without chemotherapy are not the standard first line approach for this cancer.
  231. The correct answer is B (Adenovirus, a highly contagious virus causing watery discharge and tender preauricular nodes): rapid spread between eyes, watery discharge, and tender preauricular lymphadenopathy are classic for adenoviral conjunctivitis. Staphylococcus and Pseudomonas cause bacterial disease with purulent discharge rather than watery tearing. Chlamydia causes a more chronic, often unilateral follicular conjunctivitis. Herpes simplex typically causes unilateral disease with dendritic corneal lesions, not bilateral rapid spread.
  232. The correct answer is C (Age related macular degeneration, presenting with drusen and central visual distortion): drusen deposits and macular pigmentary changes with central distortion and preserved peripheral vision are the hallmark findings of macular degeneration. Proliferative diabetic retinopathy classically shows neovascularization and hemorrhages rather than drusen. Central retinal artery occlusion causes sudden painless total vision loss, not gradual distortion. Retinal detachment presents with flashes and floaters, and glaucoma causes peripheral field loss rather than central distortion.
  233. The correct answer is D (Retinal artery embolism arising from carotid atherosclerosis causing transient monocular blindness): this describes amaurosis fugax, transient monocular vision loss caused by microemboli, most often from carotid disease, transiently occluding retinal circulation. Vitreous hemorrhage causes floaters and persistent vision changes rather than resolving curtain loss. Angle closure is painful with a red eye. Optic nerve demyelination causes subacute painful vision loss, not brief episodes, and zoster produces a vesicular rash rather than isolated transient vision loss.
  234. The correct answer is E (Keratoconjunctivitis sicca, causing gritty irritation that worsens with prolonged visual tasks): gritty burning eyes worsening with screen use and improving with blinking is classic for dry eye disease, common with reduced blink rate during focused tasks. Bacterial conjunctivitis presents with purulent discharge. Anterior uveitis and episcleritis cause redness and pain, often with photophobia, not primarily a chronic gritty sensation. Corneal ulcer would show a visible defect and significant pain with vision changes.
  235. The correct answer is A (Fluorescein staining with slit lamp examination to reveal an epithelial defect): fluorescein dye highlights an epithelial defect under blue light, confirming a corneal abrasion at the bedside. Tonometry evaluates intraocular pressure, relevant for glaucoma, not abrasions. Dilated fundus exam assesses the posterior segment, which is unaffected here. Visual field testing and the swinging flashlight test evaluate optic nerve and retinal pathways, not superficial corneal injury.
  236. The correct answer is B (Start topical and systemic agents to promptly lower the elevated intraocular pressure): this presentation describes acute angle closure glaucoma, an emergency requiring immediate pressure-lowering therapy to prevent permanent vision loss while awaiting specialist care. Antibiotic drops do not address the pressure crisis. Dilating the pupil would worsen angle closure by increasing pupillary block. Patching and delaying treatment risks irreversible optic nerve damage, and acyclovir treats herpetic disease, not angle closure.
  237. The correct answer is C (Begin high dose systemic glucocorticoids immediately to protect remaining vision): suspected giant cell arteritis with visual symptoms requires immediate steroid initiation to prevent permanent blindness, without waiting for biopsy confirmation. Carotid ultrasound does not address the acute inflammatory threat to vision. Aspirin alone is insufficient and NSAIDs do not treat the underlying vasculitis. Delaying treatment for routine outpatient referral risks irreversible vision loss.
  238. The correct answer is D (Impaired parasympathetic pupillary constriction consistent with a benign tonic pupil): a tonic (Adie) pupil is larger, reacts poorly to light with better but sluggish accommodation, and is more noticeable in dim light, without ptosis or diplopia. Third nerve palsy from aneurysm typically causes ptosis and diplopia alongside the dilated pupil. Horner syndrome causes a smaller, not larger, pupil with ptosis. Angle closure produces a painful red eye, and optic nerve inflammation causes an afferent defect without this pattern of anisocoria.
  239. The correct answer is E (Inflammation is localized retrobulbar, occurring behind the portion of the nerve that is visible): this describes optic neuritis, where inflammation often occurs posterior to the globe so the disc can appear normal on exam despite pain, color desaturation, and a relative afferent pupillary defect. Extraocular muscle or macular involvement would not explain the afferent defect and color vision loss pattern described. Functional vision loss would not produce an objective afferent pupillary defect. Papilledema causes visible disc swelling, which is absent here.
  240. The correct answer is A (Hematogenous seeding of the eye that occurred during the episode of bacteremia): endogenous endophthalmitis occurs when bloodborne organisms seed the eye during bacteremia, producing acute pain, vision loss, and hypopyon, a risk heightened by intravenous drug use. Direct inoculation describes exogenous endophthalmitis after surgery or trauma, not this scenario. Herpes reactivation causes keratitis with a different clinical course. Immune complex disease and contiguous sinus spread do not fit the temporal link to bacteremia described.
  241. The correct answer is B (Pale, bluish, edematous nasal turbinates instead of erythematous mucosa seen with viral infection): Allergic rhinitis classically produces pale, boggy, edematous turbinates from histamine mediated vascular congestion, distinct from the erythematous mucosa of viral infection. Purulent discharge, positional facial pain, fever with myalgias, and unilateral foul discharge all point toward infectious or structural causes rather than allergy. Itchy watery eyes and clear rhinorrhea support an allergic process, and the exam finding confirms it.
  242. The correct answer is A (Age related olfactory decline caused by cumulative loss of sensory neurons over time): Presbyosmia is the most common cause of chronic olfactory decline in older adults, resulting from progressive loss of olfactory sensory neurons over time. Chronic sinusitis and Parkinson disease can cause smell loss but are less common overall causes at the population level, and this patient lacks congestion or motor symptoms. Zinc and B12 deficiency are uncommon and would typically present with other systemic clues.
  243. The correct answer is C (Presbycusis resulting from age related degeneration of cochlear hair cells bilaterally): Gradual, bilateral, symmetric high frequency sensorineural hearing loss with difficulty in noisy environments in an older adult is the classic presentation of presbycusis. Otosclerosis causes conductive hearing loss, cholesteatoma is typically unilateral with otorrhea, Meniere disease fluctuates and includes vertigo, and bilateral acoustic neuromas are rare and associated with neurofibromatosis type 2.
  244. The correct answer is D (Rapid onset drooling with refusal to lie flat or swallow secretions safely): Epiglottitis presents abruptly with high fever, drooling, dysphagia, and a tripod sitting posture reflecting impending airway obstruction, requiring urgent airway management. Croup instead features a gradual barking cough with stridor that often improves with cool air, and a mild prodromal illness with low grade fever suggests a benign viral process rather than a supraglottic emergency.
  245. The correct answer is D (Dix Hallpike maneuver to elicit rotatory nystagmus and reproduce the vertigo): Brief positional vertigo triggered by head movement is classic for benign paroxysmal positional vertigo, and the Dix Hallpike maneuver is the standard bedside test that reproduces rotatory nystagmus and symptoms. Head impulse testing evaluates vestibular neuronitis, tuning fork tests assess hearing rather than vertigo, and Romberg or Valsalva testing do not confirm this specific diagnosis.
  246. The correct answer is B (Obtain audiometry to document a characteristic low frequency sensorineural hearing loss): Meniere disease is diagnosed clinically, supported by audiometry showing fluctuating low frequency sensorineural hearing loss during episodes of vertigo with tinnitus and aural fullness. MRI is reserved for atypical or asymmetric cases to exclude a retrocochlear lesion, electronystagmography during an acute attack is impractical and unnecessary, and surgery and antivirals are not first line steps.
  247. The correct answer is C (Apply a topical vasoconstrictor and then attempt gentle anterior nasal packing): When direct compression with forward head positioning fails to control anterior epistaxis, the next step is a topical vasoconstrictor followed by anterior nasal packing. Tilting the head backward risks aspiration of blood, surgical ligation is reserved for refractory bleeding after packing fails, coagulation testing should not delay hemostatic measures, and simple discharge is unsafe with active bleeding.
  248. The correct answer is C (Malignant otitis externa requiring imaging and prolonged antipseudomonal antibiotic therapy): Diabetic and immunocompromised patients with refractory otitis externa and granulation tissue in the canal are at risk for malignant otitis externa, a skull base osteomyelitis typically caused by Pseudomonas requiring imaging and prolonged antipseudomonal antibiotics. The other options do not account for the poor response to standard therapy or the diabetic risk factor for invasive skull base infection.
  249. The correct answer is D (Peritonsillar abscess requiring prompt needle aspiration or incision and drainage): Trismus, muffled voice, and asymmetric palatal bulging with uvular deviation are hallmark findings of peritonsillar abscess, which requires prompt drainage and antibiotics. Simple pharyngitis and mononucleosis do not cause trismus or palatal asymmetry, retropharyngeal infection typically presents with neck stiffness rather than uvular deviation, and Ludwig angina involves the floor of the mouth rather than the tonsillar region.
  250. The correct answer is E (Vestibular schwannoma compressing the trigeminal nerve at the cerebellopontine angle): Unilateral progressive sensorineural hearing loss with tinnitus and ipsilateral facial numbness suggests a cerebellopontine angle mass such as vestibular schwannoma compressing the trigeminal nerve, warranting MRI before attributing symptoms to age related hearing loss. Presbycusis is bilateral and symmetric, otosclerosis causes conductive loss, and otitis media or TMJ dysfunction do not explain progressive unilateral sensorineural loss with facial numbness.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (At least five symptoms present nearly every day for two weeks minimum): major depressive disorder requires at least five of nine characteristic symptoms, including depressed mood or anhedonia, present nearly every day for a minimum of two weeks. Adjustment disorder involves a clear stressor without meeting full symptom or duration criteria, so A and B describe adjustment disorder features instead. Psychotic features (D) are not required for a standard MDD diagnosis, and prior psychotherapy failure (E) is not a diagnostic criterion at all.
  2. The correct answer is A (Male sex, older age, and access to firearms in the home): these are well-established epidemiologic risk factors for completed suicide, since men and older adults use more lethal methods and firearm access dramatically increases lethality of attempts. Women attempt suicide more often but with lower lethality methods, making B a distractor rather than a marker of completed suicide risk. Social support (C) is protective rather than a risk factor, and options D and E describe scenarios without the strongest established risk associations.
  3. The correct answer is E (This presentation meets full criteria for bipolar I disorder, manic episode): a full week of decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, grandiosity, and impulsive behavior causing significant impairment meets criteria for a manic episode, which defines bipolar I disorder. Cyclothymic disorder (A) involves milder, chronic mood fluctuations that never meet full mania or depression criteria. Mixed features of depression (B) would require depressive symptoms predominating, and generalized anxiety and persistent depressive disorder (C, D) do not match this manic symptom cluster.
  4. The correct answer is B (Restriction of caloric intake leading to significantly low body weight): anorexia nervosa is defined by energy intake restriction producing a significantly low body weight along with fear of weight gain and body image disturbance. Binge-purge behavior (A) can occur in the binge-eating/purging subtype of anorexia but is the defining feature of bulimia instead. Normal weight with fear of gaining (C) and binge eating without compensation (D) describe other eating disorder presentations, and muscularity-focused preoccupation (E) is not the core anorexia feature.
  5. The correct answer is D (Self-evaluation unduly influenced by body shape and weight): bulimia nervosa requires that self-worth be excessively tied to body shape and weight, alongside recurrent binge-purge cycles occurring at least weekly for three months. Low body weight and amenorrhea (A, B) are associated with anorexia nervosa rather than bulimia, since bulimia patients are typically normal weight. Lack of body concern (C) contradicts the diagnosis, and an age-of-onset cutoff (E) is not part of the criteria.
  6. The correct answer is A (Continued use despite persistent social or interpersonal problems it causes): substance use disorder is defined by a problematic pattern of use leading to impairment or distress, including continued use despite ongoing social or relationship problems it causes. A single intoxication episode (B) or strictly prescribed medical use (C) does not indicate a use disorder. Tolerance after one exposure (D) is not physiologically typical, and a family history alone without personal use (E) does not establish a diagnosis.
  7. The correct answer is C (Continuous signs of disturbance persisting for at least six months): schizophrenia requires continuous signs of disturbance for at least six months, including at least one month of active-phase symptoms. Symptoms lasting less than one month with recovery (A) describe brief psychotic disorder, and the one-to-six month range (B) describes schizophreniform disorder. A single brief episode (D) or symptoms only during intoxication or withdrawal (E) would not meet criteria for a primary psychotic disorder like schizophrenia.
  8. The correct answer is E (This presentation is most consistent with delusional disorder given her preserved functioning): this diagnosis involves at least one month of a fixed, non-bizarre delusion without hallucinations, disorganized thinking, or major functional impairment, matching this patient's preserved daily functioning. Schizophrenia and schizoaffective disorder (A, B) require broader symptom clusters and greater functional decline, and brief psychotic disorder (C) resolves within one month. Paranoid personality disorder (D) involves pervasive distrust without a true fixed delusion.
  9. The correct answer is B (Intrusive unwanted thoughts and repetitive behaviors performed to reduce anxiety): obsessive-compulsive disorder is defined by obsessions, which are intrusive distressing thoughts, and compulsions performed to neutralize the resulting anxiety. Generalized worry (A) describes generalized anxiety disorder, and panic attacks (C) describe panic disorder. Illness preoccupation (D) describes illness anxiety disorder, and rigid perfectionism without intrusive thoughts (E) describes obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, a distinct and ego-syntonic condition.
  10. The correct answer is D (Marked fear of a specific object or situation that is actively avoided): specific phobia involves intense, disproportionate fear of a discrete object or situation leading to avoidance behavior. Social or performance-specific fear (A) describes social anxiety disorder, and unexpected panic attacks (B) describe panic disorder. Broad multi-domain worry (C) describes generalized anxiety disorder, and fear of separation from attachment figures (E) describes separation anxiety disorder.
  11. The correct answer is C (Symptoms present for more than one month after the trauma): posttraumatic stress disorder requires symptoms persisting beyond one month following the traumatic event, distinguishing it from acute stress disorder which covers the first month. Options A and B describe timeframes consistent with acute stress disorder rather than PTSD. A one-year minimum (D) is far longer than actually required, and stating no minimum duration applies (E) is inaccurate since duration is a defining feature separating these two diagnoses.
  12. The correct answer is A (Excessive worry about multiple events, more days than not, for six months): generalized anxiety disorder requires excessive, difficult-to-control worry about multiple domains occurring more days than not for at least six months. Discrete episodes of intense fear lasting minutes (B) describe panic attacks, and worry limited to one social situation (C) describes social anxiety disorder. A two-week duration (D) is far too brief, and worry exclusively tied to substance withdrawal (E) would point to a substance-induced anxiety disorder instead.
  13. The correct answer is D (This presentation is most consistent with a diagnosis of panic disorder): recurrent unexpected panic attacks with rapid-onset physical symptoms and persistent worry about future attacks are the hallmark of panic disorder. The correct answer is D (4 to 8 mL per kilogram of predicted body weight): lung-protective ventilation for ARDS uses low tidal volumes of roughly 4 to 8 mL per kilogram of predicted body weight, typically targeting around 6 mL per kilogram, to reduce ventilator-induced lung injury.
