  138. The correct answer is E (Trochanteric bursitis causing focal lateral hip pain and point tenderness): Trochanteric bursitis presents with lateral hip pain that worsens when lying on the affected side and produces point tenderness directly over the greater trochanter. Meralgia paresthetica instead produces numbness and tingling rather than focal point tenderness. Avascular necrosis typically causes deep groin pain rather than lateral tenderness, and iliopsoas tendinitis also localizes primarily to the groin. Sacroiliac joint dysfunction refers pain toward the buttock rather than the lateral hip region.
  139. The correct answer is B (Anserine bursa located at the medial proximal tibial tendon insertion): Anserine bursitis causes tenderness over the proximal medial tibia below the joint line, where the sartorius, gracilis, and semitendinosus tendons converge and insert, and is common in obese patients with coexisting osteoarthritis. Prepatellar bursitis instead causes swelling directly over the kneecap itself. A popliteal cyst presents as posterior knee fullness rather than proximal medial tenderness. The iliotibial band and medial collateral ligament produce lateral or joint line symptoms rather than this described location.
  140. The correct answer is D (Plantar fasciitis producing pain at the medial calcaneal tuberosity insertion): Plantar fasciitis classically causes prominent start up pain with the very first steps of the day and produces point tenderness at the medial calcaneal tuberosity where the plantar fascia inserts. Achilles tendinitis instead causes posterior heel pain closer to the tendon insertion site. Tarsal tunnel syndrome produces numbness and tingling rather than this mechanical start up pain pattern. Morton neuroma causes forefoot pain between the metatarsal heads, not heel pain.
  141. The correct answer is C (HLA-B27 haplotype strongly associated with ankylosing spondylitis and related spondyloarthropathies): Inflammatory back pain that improves with exercise and worsens with rest in a young man strongly suggests ankylosing spondylitis, a condition closely linked to the HLA-B27 haplotype. HLA-DR4 is instead associated with rheumatoid arthritis rather than axial spondyloarthritis. HLA-DR3 relates to autoimmune endocrine disease and HLA-DQ2 relates to celiac disease, neither fitting this clinical presentation. HLA-B51 is linked to Behcet disease, which presents with recurrent ulcers and uveitis instead.
  142. The correct answer is C (Reactive arthritis triggered by a preceding genitourinary or enteric infection): Reactive arthritis follows genitourinary or enteric infections such as Campylobacter and classically presents with the triad of conjunctivitis, urethritis, and asymmetric arthritis weeks after the trigger. Gonococcal arthritis follows direct joint infection rather than representing a post infectious reactive process. Still disease presents with fevers and rash rather than this specific triad, and psoriatic arthritis requires preexisting psoriasis. Rheumatic fever follows streptococcal pharyngitis, not a gastrointestinal infectious trigger.
  143. The correct answer is B (Psoriatic arthritis presenting with dactylitis and characteristic fingernail changes): Dactylitis, the sausage digit appearance, combined with nail pitting and onycholysis in a patient with known psoriasis is classic for psoriatic arthritis. Rheumatoid arthritis causes symmetric small joint swelling but typically without dactylitis or nail pitting. Gout produces tophi rather than diffuse digit swelling, and osteoarthritis produces bony nodules rather than sausage shaped digits. Reactive arthritis by definition occurs without preexisting psoriatic skin disease present.
  144. The correct answer is D (Enteropathic arthritis occurring in association with inflammatory bowel disease): Enteropathic arthritis is a peripheral or axial spondyloarthropathy that occurs in patients with inflammatory bowel disease and often flares in parallel with bowel disease activity, matching this described presentation closely. Septic arthritis instead presents as a single acutely hot joint with systemic infection signs, not this chronic relapsing pattern. Fibromyalgia causes diffuse pain without objective joint swelling. Osteoarthritis and Charcot joints are both unrelated to underlying bowel disease activity.
  145. The correct answer is D (Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody testing performed as part of the workup): Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody is highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis and often becomes detectable before clinical joint symptoms even develop. Rheumatoid factor is more sensitive but considerably less specific since it appears across many other conditions as well. Antinuclear antibody testing is nonspecific and seen across numerous autoimmune diseases broadly. Anti-Smith and anti-histone antibodies are instead associated with lupus and drug induced lupus respectively, not rheumatoid arthritis.
  146. The correct answer is B (Felty syndrome occurring as a recognized complication of rheumatoid arthritis): Felty syndrome is the specific combination of rheumatoid arthritis, splenomegaly, and neutropenia, which together predispose affected patients to recurrent serious bacterial infections. Sjogren and sicca syndrome both involve dryness of mucous membranes rather than splenomegaly and neutropenia. Caplan syndrome refers instead to pulmonary nodules occurring in rheumatoid patients with coal dust or silica exposure. Reiter syndrome is an older term for reactive arthritis, unrelated to this particular triad.
  147. The correct answer is A (Folic acid supplementation intended to reduce methotrexate related toxicity risk): Folic acid supplementation is routinely prescribed alongside methotrexate to reduce mucosal, hepatic, and gastrointestinal side effects without meaningfully diminishing its efficacy for treating rheumatoid arthritis. Vitamin D and calcium address bone health concerns rather than directly reducing methotrexate toxicity. Vitamin B12 and iron sulfate address entirely separate deficiencies unrelated to the underlying mechanism of methotrexate related folate antagonism.
  148. The correct answer is C (Anti-double stranded DNA antibody titers that rise during active nephritis): Anti-double stranded DNA antibody titers correlate closely with overall lupus disease activity, particularly lupus nephritis, and are routinely used clinically to monitor for impending disease flares. Anti-centromere antibody is instead associated with limited cutaneous scleroderma rather than lupus nephritis activity. Anti-Jo-1 is a myositis specific antibody, and anti-histone antibody points toward a drug induced lupus process. Anti-Ro antibody is linked more to Sjogren overlap and neonatal lupus rather than nephritis activity specifically.
  149. The correct answer is B (Malar rash appearing across the cheeks while sparing the nasolabial folds): The malar or butterfly rash that characteristically spares the nasolabial folds is a hallmark cutaneous finding of systemic lupus erythematosus in this age group. Heliotrope rash and Gottron papules are instead specific cutaneous findings of dermatomyositis. Sausage shaped digits with nail pitting describe psoriatic arthritis rather than lupus. A salmon colored evanescent rash is instead characteristic of adult onset Still disease with quotidian fevers.
  150. The correct answer is A (Anti-histone antibody typically seen in cases of drug induced lupus): Drug induced lupus, classically triggered by hydralazine, procainamide, or isoniazid, is strongly associated with anti-histone antibodies and typically spares both the kidneys and the central nervous system unlike idiopathic disease. Anti-double stranded DNA and anti-Smith antibodies are more specific markers for idiopathic systemic lupus erythematosus instead. Anti-Scl-70 and anti-centromere antibodies are associated with systemic sclerosis subtypes rather than any form of drug induced lupus.
  151. The correct answer is B (Anti-Scl-70 (anti-topoisomerase I) antibody associated with diffuse cutaneous disease): Anti-Scl-70, also called anti-topoisomerase I antibody, is associated with diffuse cutaneous systemic sclerosis and carries a notably higher risk of progressive pulmonary fibrosis. Anti-centromere antibody is instead linked to the more limited cutaneous form with comparatively less lung involvement. Anti-Ro and anti-Jo-1 relate respectively to Sjogren overlap and inflammatory myositis, and anti-U1 RNP is linked instead to mixed connective tissue disease.
  152. The correct answer is A (Anti-centromere antibody strongly associated with the limited cutaneous CREST pattern): This constellation of findings describes CREST syndrome, the limited cutaneous form of systemic sclerosis, which is most strongly associated with anti-centromere antibody positivity. Anti-Scl-70 instead correlates with the diffuse cutaneous subtype and a greater risk of internal organ involvement. Anti-dsDNA and anti-La antibodies are respectively associated with lupus and Sjogren syndrome rather than limited scleroderma. Anti-RNA polymerase III is linked to diffuse scleroderma with heightened renal crisis risk.
  153. The correct answer is A (Polyarteritis nodosa affecting medium sized vessels while sparing the lungs): Polyarteritis nodosa is a medium vessel vasculitis that classically spares the lungs while causing mononeuritis multiplex, new onset hypertension, and characteristic microaneurysms visible on angiography. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis and microscopic polyangiitis typically involve the lungs and kidneys along with ANCA positivity, unlike this case. Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis occurs specifically in patients with a history of asthma and eosinophilia. Takayasu arteritis instead affects the aorta and its large branches, not medium vessels.
  154. The correct answer is C (Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis presenting with asthma and eosinophilia): Eosinophilic granulomatosis with polyangiitis, formerly called Churg-Strauss syndrome, occurs in patients with a preceding history of asthma and allergic disease and features marked eosinophilia along with vasculitic neuropathy and purpura. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis lacks this strong asthma and eosinophilia association typically seen here. Giant cell arteritis instead affects older patients with cranial symptoms, and Kawasaki disease occurs primarily in young children. Polyarteritis nodosa does not feature prominent eosinophilia as a hallmark finding.
  155. The correct answer is A (Giant cell arteritis presenting with jaw claudication and threatened vision loss): Giant cell arteritis presents with temporal headache, jaw claudication, markedly elevated inflammatory markers, and can cause sudden irreversible vision loss, making immediate high dose corticosteroids essential even before biopsy confirmation is obtained. Polymyalgia rheumatica can coexist with this condition but does not alone explain jaw claudication and vision loss. Trigeminal neuralgia, cluster headache, and migraine do not cause vision loss accompanied by this degree of elevated sedimentation rate.
  156. The correct answer is C (Takayasu arteritis affecting the aorta and its major branch vessels): Takayasu arteritis is a large vessel vasculitis that typically affects young women and involves the aorta along with its major branches, producing pulse deficits and limb claudication as described. Giant cell arteritis instead occurs in older patients and primarily involves cranial vessels rather than the aorta. Kawasaki disease affects young children with coronary artery involvement specifically. Buerger disease relates to tobacco use in smaller vessels, and fibromuscular dysplasia is a distinct noninflammatory arterial disorder.
  157. The correct answer is D (Granulomatosis with polyangiitis typically associated with cytoplasmic ANCA positivity): Granulomatosis with polyangiitis affects the upper airways, lungs, and kidneys, and is associated with cytoplasmic ANCA directed against proteinase 3, matching this presentation closely. Microscopic polyangiitis is instead more associated with perinuclear ANCA against myeloperoxidase and lacks granulomatous upper airway disease. Eosinophilic granulomatosis requires a preceding history of asthma and eosinophilia. Goodpasture syndrome involves anti-glomerular basement membrane antibody rather than ANCA, and IgA vasculitis typically presents in children instead.
  158. The correct answer is C (Heliotrope rash of the eyelids together with Gottron papules of the knuckles): Heliotrope rash of the eyelids together with Gottron papules over the knuckles are the classic and highly specific cutaneous findings of dermatomyositis accompanying the described proximal muscle weakness. Malar rash is instead characteristic of lupus rather than dermatomyositis specifically. Sclerodactyly describes systemic sclerosis, livedo reticularis is seen in vasculitis and antiphospholipid syndrome, and erythema nodosum is linked to sarcoidosis and inflammatory bowel disease rather than myositis.
  159. The correct answer is B (Relapsing polychondritis affecting cartilaginous structures throughout the body): Relapsing polychondritis causes recurrent inflammation of cartilaginous structures including the ears, notably sparing the earlobe which lacks cartilage entirely, and can also involve the nose and airway leading to saddle nose deformity and hoarseness. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis can cause saddle nose deformity too but not this episodic bilateral ear chondritis pattern. Gouty tophi and cellulitis do not typically spare the earlobe or cause airway hoarseness. Lupus does not classically cause cartilage destruction like this.
  160. The correct answer is D (Behcet disease presenting with recurrent oral ulcers, genital ulcers, and uveitis): Behcet disease is classically associated with recurrent oral and genital ulcers, uveitis, and a positive pathergy test, and is more prevalent along the historic Silk Road region including the Mediterranean and Middle East. Reactive arthritis instead presents with the conjunctivitis, urethritis, and arthritis triad rather than this specific ulcer pattern. Lupus and Sjogren syndrome do not typically cause genital ulcers or a positive pathergy response. Relapsing polychondritis targets cartilage rather than mucosal ulcers.
  161. The correct answer is B (Jones criteria used specifically for diagnosing acute rheumatic fever): The Jones criteria are used specifically to diagnose acute rheumatic fever, which follows streptococcal pharyngitis and presents with migratory polyarthritis, carditis, chorea, subcutaneous nodules, and erythema marginatum. The ACR and SLICC criteria are instead used for rheumatoid arthritis and lupus respectively, not rheumatic fever. CASPAR criteria classify psoriatic arthritis, and the modified New York criteria are used for ankylosing spondylitis, none of which fit this post streptococcal childhood presentation.
  162. The correct answer is E (Anti-Ro (SSA) and anti-La (SSB) antibodies typically seen in Sjogren syndrome): Sjogren syndrome, characterized by dry eyes and dry mouth resulting from lymphocytic infiltration of exocrine glands, is most closely associated with anti-Ro (SSA) and anti-La (SSB) antibody positivity. Anti-Scl-70 and anti-centromere antibodies instead relate to systemic sclerosis subtypes rather than sicca symptoms. Anti-Jo-1 and anti-Mi-2 are myositis specific antibodies, and anti-dsDNA with anti-Smith are lupus specific markers rather than sicca specific ones.
  163. The correct answer is A (Fibromyalgia presenting with chronic widespread pain and normal laboratory testing): Fibromyalgia presents with chronic widespread pain, fatigue, and poor quality sleep without any objective joint inflammation or elevated inflammatory markers on testing. Polymyalgia rheumatica instead causes proximal stiffness along with an elevated sedimentation rate, unlike the entirely normal labs described here. Rheumatoid arthritis and polymyositis both produce objective joint swelling or measurable muscle weakness with abnormal findings, and Still disease presents with fevers and a rash, none of which match this presentation.
  164. The correct answer is C (Adult onset Still disease presenting with quotidian fevers and high ferritin): Adult onset Still disease presents with quotidian spiking fevers, a transient salmon colored rash, arthralgias, and markedly elevated ferritin after infection and malignancy have been reasonably excluded. Lupus typically shows a fixed malar rash and positive autoantibodies rather than this evanescent rash pattern. Rheumatoid arthritis lacks the characteristic daily fever spikes seen here, and reactive arthritis and polyarteritis nodosa do not produce this specific rash and ferritin combination.
  165. The correct answer is B (FBN1 fibrillin-1 gene mutation classically responsible for Marfan syndrome): Marfan syndrome results from mutations in the FBN1 gene encoding fibrillin-1, producing the classic tall stature, lens dislocation, and aortic root dilation described in this vignette. COL1A1 mutations instead cause osteogenesis imperfecta, and COL5A1 mutations cause classic Ehlers-Danlos syndrome, both with clearly different clinical features than described here. TNXB mutations relate to a rare Ehlers-Danlos subtype, and HFE mutations cause hereditary hemochromatosis, entirely unrelated to this presentation.
  166. The correct answer is D (Ehlers-Danlos syndrome typically presenting with hyperextensible skin and joint hypermobility): Ehlers-Danlos syndrome is characterized by skin hyperextensibility, joint hypermobility with recurrent dislocations, easy bruising, and a high Beighton hypermobility score as described in this vignette. Marfan syndrome instead features tall stature along with aortic and lens findings rather than skin hyperextensibility as the dominant trait. Osteogenesis imperfecta primarily causes bone fragility, homocystinuria causes a Marfanoid habitus with thrombosis risk, and pseudoxanthoma elasticum causes calcification changes rather than joint hypermobility.
  167. The correct answer is D (Colchicine or an oral NSAID given promptly for acute symptom control): Acute gout flares are treated with colchicine, NSAIDs, or corticosteroids for symptom control rather than with urate lowering therapy started during the attack itself. Allopurinol, febuxostat, probenecid, and pegloticase are all urate lowering agents that can worsen or prolong an acute flare if started during it, and are instead reserved for chronic management once the flare has fully resolved.
  168. The correct answer is E (HLA-B*5801 genetic testing performed before starting allopurinol specifically): HLA-B*5801 testing is recommended before starting allopurinol in patients of Southeast Asian descent, particularly Han Chinese, Korean, or Thai ancestry, because this allele confers a markedly increased risk of severe allopurinol hypersensitivity syndrome. Renal ultrasound, electrocardiogram, colonoscopy, and bone density scanning are not part of the standard pre-allopurinol workup used to screen for this specific genetic risk factor.
  169. The correct answer is B (Arthrocentesis performed with synovial fluid analysis along with therapeutic drainage): Arthrocentesis both confirms the underlying diagnosis by identifying calcium pyrophosphate crystals and provides pain relief by decompressing the swollen joint, making it the appropriate first step here. Colchicine can help symptomatically but skips this important diagnostic step and misses formally ruling out infection. Allopurinol has no established role in pseudogout management, MRI is unnecessary for this diagnosis, and antibiotics are inappropriate without any evidence suggesting infection.
  170. MRI is reserved for cases when radiographs appear normal but clinical suspicion remains high for early disease, rather than CT, bone scan, PET, or ultrasound, none of which serve as the standard initial test for suspected sacroiliitis.
  171. The correct answer is B (Complete blood count along with liver enzyme and renal function testing): Before starting methotrexate, baseline complete blood count, liver function testing, and renal function testing are obtained because the drug can cause bone marrow suppression, hepatotoxicity, and is cleared renally, requiring dose adjustment. Uric acid, lipid panel, thyroid function, glucose, and protein electrophoresis are not part of the standard baseline panel used specifically to monitor for methotrexate related toxicity.
  172. The correct answer is D (Screening specifically for latent tuberculosis infection prior to starting therapy): Screening for latent tuberculosis is mandatory before starting a TNF inhibitor because these agents can reactivate latent infection into active, potentially disseminated tuberculosis disease. Colonoscopy, routine pulmonary function testing, echocardiography, and HLA-B27 testing are not required screening steps specifically tied to initiating TNF inhibitor therapy in this clinical context.
  173. The correct answer is C (High dose corticosteroids combined with either mycophenolate or cyclophosphamide therapy): Proliferative lupus nephritis requires aggressive induction therapy combining high dose corticosteroids with mycophenolate mofetil or cyclophosphamide in order to prevent progression toward end stage renal disease. NSAIDs, hydroxychloroquine alone, topical steroids, and colchicine with allopurinol are all clearly insufficient for controlling this degree of active proliferative nephritis on their own.
  174. The correct answer is A (Annual dilated ophthalmologic examination performed to screen for retinal toxicity): Hydroxychloroquine can cause an irreversible retinopathy with prolonged use, so a baseline ophthalmologic examination followed by annual screening after five years of therapy is the recommended monitoring approach. Audiometry, nerve conduction studies, pulmonary function testing, and bone marrow biopsy are not part of the standard hydroxychloroquine toxicity monitoring protocol recommended for these patients.
  175. The correct answer is B (Angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors started immediately regardless of baseline renal function): Angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors are the treatment of choice for scleroderma renal crisis and should be started immediately regardless of baseline renal function in order to prevent irreversible kidney damage from occurring. Beta blockers and thiazide diuretics are not first line therapy for this specific hypertensive emergency. Calcium channel blockers primarily target Raynaud phenomenon rather than this crisis, and NSAIDs can actually worsen renal function further.
  176. The correct answer is C (Dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers chosen for their vasodilating properties): Dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers such as nifedipine are first line pharmacologic therapy for Raynaud phenomenon because they promote peripheral vasodilation and meaningfully reduce the frequency and severity of vasospastic episodes. Beta blockers can actually worsen Raynaud symptoms through their vasoconstrictive properties. ACE inhibitors, loop diuretics, and corticosteroids are not considered standard first line therapy for this particular vasospastic condition.
  177. The correct answer is D (Antiviral therapy specifically targeting his underlying chronic hepatitis B infection): When polyarteritis nodosa is associated with underlying hepatitis B infection, antiviral therapy targeting the virus itself is essential alongside carefully limited immunosuppression, since treating the underlying infection is central to controlling the vasculitis and allows immunosuppression to eventually be tapered. Prophylactic antibiotics, splenectomy, plasma exchange alone, and iron chelation are not standard components of managing hepatitis B associated polyarteritis nodosa in current practice.
  178. The correct answer is A (Starting high dose corticosteroid therapy without any further delay whatsoever): High dose corticosteroids should be started immediately whenever giant cell arteritis is clinically suspected, especially with visual symptoms present, because vision loss can quickly become permanent while biopsy sensitivity remains adequate for one to two weeks after steroids begin. Delaying treatment risks irreversible blindness in this scenario. Methotrexate, genetic testing, and TNF inhibitors are not appropriate as immediate first steps in this true ophthalmologic emergency.
  179. The correct answer is D (High dose corticosteroids combined with either rituximab or cyclophosphamide therapy): Severe organ threatening ANCA associated vasculitis with pulmonary hemorrhage and rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis requires induction therapy with high dose corticosteroids combined with rituximab or cyclophosphamide to rapidly control inflammation and prevent irreversible organ damage. Hydroxychloroquine with low dose steroids, NSAIDs with colchicine, topical steroids, and antibiotics are all clearly inadequate for this genuinely life threatening presentation requiring aggressive treatment.
  180. The correct answer is E (Muscle biopsy demonstrating the characteristic pattern of inflammatory changes): Muscle biopsy showing perifascicular atrophy along with an inflammatory infiltrate confirms the diagnosis of dermatomyositis and helps distinguish it from other possible causes of weakness and elevated creatine kinase. Bone marrow biopsy, skin biopsy of unaffected skin, and lumbar puncture do not directly evaluate for active muscle inflammation in this context.
  181. The correct answer is D (Age and risk appropriate malignancy screening tailored specifically to this patient): Dermatomyositis in adults carries a meaningfully increased risk of occult malignancy, so age and risk appropriate cancer screening should be pursued promptly at diagnosis rather than skipped entirely or reserved only for symptomatic patients later on. Routine whole body PET scanning for every single patient is not standard practice given cost and generally low diagnostic yield. Genetic counseling does not substitute for appropriate cancer screening in this specific clinical context.
  182. The correct answer is B (Arthrocentesis performed with synovial fluid Gram stain and culture testing): Emergent arthrocentesis with synovial fluid Gram stain, culture, cell count, and crystal analysis is essential before starting antibiotics whenever clinically possible, because it confirms the diagnosis and identifies the causative organism to guide targeted therapy. Starting antibiotics without aspiration first can sterilize the fluid and obscure the underlying diagnosis. Plain radiographs, delayed outpatient follow up, and oral antibiotics are all inappropriate for this true surgical emergency.
  183. The correct answer is A (Multiple mucosal sites including the cervix, urethra, throat, and rectum): In disseminated gonococcal infection, culturing all relevant mucosal sites including the cervix, urethra, throat, and rectum increases the overall yield of identifying Neisseria gonorrhoeae, since blood and joint fluid cultures are frequently negative in this arthritis dermatitis syndrome. Cerebrospinal fluid, bone marrow, sputum, and stool cultures are not routinely useful for establishing this particular diagnosis in practice.
  184. The correct answer is E (Serum ferritin level along with a transferrin saturation percentage measurement): Arthropathy involving the second and third metacarpophalangeal joints with hook shaped osteophytes is a classic radiographic pattern for hemochromatosis, so serum ferritin and transferrin saturation should be checked to confirm underlying iron overload. Uric acid, antinuclear antibody, rheumatoid factor, and anti-CCP testing do not identify the underlying iron storage disease responsible for this particular characteristic joint pattern.
  185. The correct answer is E (High serum calcium occurring together with a high parathyroid hormone level): Primary hyperparathyroidism is confirmed by finding elevated serum calcium in the setting of an inappropriately elevated or normal parathyroid hormone level, which together drive the bone resorption seen radiographically in this patient. Suppressed parathyroid hormone accompanying high calcium instead suggests a non-parathyroid cause of hypercalcemia. Any of the low calcium patterns listed are inconsistent with a diagnosis of primary hyperparathyroidism.
  186. The correct answer is B (Cortical thickening demonstrating a mixed lytic and sclerotic imaging pattern): Paget disease of bone characteristically shows cortical thickening with a mixed lytic and sclerotic pattern along with overall bone enlargement, matching the isolated elevated alkaline phosphatase and tibial bowing described in this vignette.   187. The correct answer is D (Core decompression surgery performed to relieve elevated intraosseous pressure early): Core decompression is an appropriate joint preserving surgical procedure for early stage avascular necrosis before femoral head collapse has occurred, aiming to relieve intraosseous pressure and improve local blood flow to the area. Total hip replacement is generally reserved for more advanced cases with collapse already present. Corticosteroids can actually be a cause of avascular necrosis rather than a treatment for it, and antibiotics are irrelevant since this is not an infectious process.
  188. The correct answer is C (Oral NSAID therapy started with careful attention to gastrointestinal risk factors): After acetaminophen and topical therapy have failed, oral NSAIDs represent the appropriate next step in the stepwise management of osteoarthritis, with careful attention paid to gastrointestinal and cardiovascular risk factors. Surgery is generally reserved for more advanced or refractory disease that has failed conservative measures. Long term opioids are avoided as early therapy given their limited benefit and dependence risk, and systemic corticosteroids are not standard treatment for osteoarthritis.
  189. The correct answer is E (Anterior cruciate ligament, which is specifically assessed by these two tests): A positive anterior drawer test and Lachman test are both specific for anterior cruciate ligament tears, which commonly occur during noncontact pivoting injuries sustained by athletes during competition. Valgus and varus stress testing assess the medial and lateral collateral ligaments respectively, and the McMurray test is used specifically to evaluate for meniscal injury.
  190. The correct answer is A (Surgical spinal fusion pursued given the significant severity of this curve): A Cobb angle of 45 degrees or greater in a skeletally immature patient generally warrants surgical spinal fusion, because curves of this magnitude carry a genuinely high risk of continued progression well into adulthood. Observation and bracing alone are typically reserved for meaningfully smaller curves than this. Physical therapy alone does not correct significant structural curves of this size, and scoliosis at this magnitude does not spontaneously resolve simply with continued growth.
  191. The correct answer is C (Anti-U1 ribonucleoprotein (RNP) antibody testing performed as part of the workup): High titer anti-U1 ribonucleoprotein antibody is the recognized serologic hallmark of mixed connective tissue disease and is essential for confirming this diagnosis in a patient presenting with overlapping lupus, scleroderma, and myositis features together.   192.   193. The correct answer is C (Complete blood count along with thyroid function testing and inflammatory markers): A basic workup including complete blood count, thyroid function testing, and inflammatory markers such as erythrocyte sedimentation rate helps reasonably exclude other treatable causes of widespread pain like hypothyroidism, anemia, or inflammatory arthritis before formally diagnosing fibromyalgia. Muscle biopsy, bone marrow biopsy, nerve conduction studies, and PET scanning are not part of the standard basic fibromyalgia workup and are instead reserved for specific alternative clinical concerns when present.
  194. The correct answer is A (A short tapering course of oral or intra-articular corticosteroid therapy): When NSAIDs are contraindicated by renal impairment and colchicine is unsafe due to a significant drug interaction, corticosteroids given orally or intra-articularly become the appropriate option for controlling an acute gout flare.   195. The correct answer is C (Synovial fluid aspiration performed with Gram stain and culture testing): Because gout and septic arthritis can genuinely coexist and present with very similar features, synovial fluid aspiration with Gram stain, culture, and crystal analysis is essential to rule out infection before attributing symptoms solely to a gout flare. Empiric colchicine without first ruling out infection risks masking a truly septic joint. Serum uric acid can remain normal during an acute flare and does not exclude infection, and antibiotics without cultures or radiographs do not adequately evaluate for it.
  196. The correct answer is B (Perform arthrocentesis first, then carefully tailor treatment based on the results): In an immunosuppressed patient presenting with acute monoarthritis, arthrocentesis should be performed promptly to analyze cell count, Gram stain, culture, and crystals, since chronic steroid use can blunt the typical inflammatory response and effectively mask an underlying infection. Starting antibiotics or simply assuming a flare or gout without fluid analysis risks missing a truly septic joint or delaying appropriate therapy. MRI does not replace the diagnostic value of direct joint fluid analysis in this urgent situation.
  197. The correct answer is D (Anti-CCP positivity still meaningfully supports an underlying rheumatoid arthritis diagnosis here): Anti-CCP antibody is highly specific for rheumatoid arthritis and can remain positive even when rheumatoid factor itself is negative, so the overall clinical picture combined with anti-CCP positivity still strongly supports the diagnosis. Rheumatoid factor negativity alone does not exclude rheumatoid arthritis since a meaningful subset of patients remain seronegative for this particular marker. Osteoarthritis does not produce prolonged morning stiffness or strong autoantibody positivity, and nothing here specifically points toward lupus instead.
  198. The correct answer is A (Prophylactic low dose aspirin combined with heparin throughout the entire pregnancy): In antiphospholipid antibody positive patients with a documented history of pregnancy loss, prophylactic low dose aspirin combined with heparin throughout pregnancy meaningfully reduces the risk of recurrent obstetric complications and thrombosis, even without any prior clot. Warfarin is teratogenic and should be avoided during pregnancy. Discontinuing all lupus medications risks triggering a disease flare, and waiting until a clot actually occurs ignores established evidence based prevention strategies for this specific high risk population.
  199. The correct answer is E (An isolated vasculopathy of the pulmonary arteries occurring independent of skin fibrosis): In limited cutaneous systemic sclerosis, pulmonary arterial hypertension often arises from an isolated vasculopathy affecting the pulmonary arteries that can develop independently of the degree of skin or lung fibrosis present, unlike diffuse disease where interstitial fibrosis predominates instead. Skin fibrosis does not directly compress the pulmonary vessels themselves. Pulmonary embolism and left heart failure are not the typical underlying mechanism in this particular scleroderma associated pattern, and aspiration would produce a distinctly different pulmonary clinical picture.
  200. The correct answer is E (A fused, osteoporotic spine functions like a long lever prone to unstable fracture): The fused, often osteoporotic spine seen in longstanding ankylosing spondylitis behaves like a long rigid lever, so even seemingly minor trauma can produce unstable, genuinely high risk fractures through the fused segments with potential for spinal cord injury. NSAID use is not the primary mechanism driving this particular fracture risk. Muscle weakness and disc herniation do not adequately explain this specific fracture pattern, and fusion actually reduces flexibility rather than increasing it, contributing directly to the injury risk.
  201. The correct answer is A (TNF inhibitors are recognized to potentially unmask or worsen demyelinating disease): TNF inhibitors are recognized to potentially unmask or worsen underlying demyelinating disease such as multiple sclerosis, so new optic neuritis and white matter changes occurring in a patient on this therapy should prompt discontinuation and prompt neurology evaluation. This is not an expected benign effect, nor is it simply attributable to methotrexate use. Rheumatoid arthritis flares affect joints rather than causing optic neuritis, and this particular neurologic association is well documented rather than absent.
  202. The correct answer is A (Secondary (AA) amyloidosis occurring as a result of chronic underlying inflammation): Chronic uncontrolled inflammatory disease such as rheumatoid arthritis can lead to secondary AA amyloidosis, in which amyloid protein deposits within the kidney and characteristically shows the classic apple green birefringence with Congo red staining under polarized light. Lupus nephritis would instead show a different immune complex deposition pattern on biopsy examination. Methotrexate is not classically associated with amyloid deposition, and minimal change disease along with diabetic nephropathy do not produce this specific Congo red staining pattern.
  203. The correct answer is D (Anti-melanoma differentiation-associated gene 5 (MDA5) antibody linked to lung dominant disease): Anti-MDA5 antibody is associated with amyopathic or hypomyopathic dermatomyositis featuring rapidly progressive interstitial lung disease alongside relatively mild muscle involvement, closely matching this particular presentation. Anti-Jo-1 is instead part of the antisynthetase syndrome with more prominent myositis symptoms and mechanic hands. Anti-centromere and anti-Scl-70 relate to systemic sclerosis subtypes, and anti-dsDNA is specific to lupus, none of which explain this specific lung dominant myositis pattern described here.
  204. The correct answer is D (Rising ANCA titers accompanied by new symptoms suggest an impending disease relapse): A rising ANCA titer accompanied by new clinical symptoms such as sinusitis and hematuria in a patient with known granulomatosis with polyangiitis strongly suggests an impending relapse and warrants prompt reevaluation along with likely treatment escalation. ANCA titers do carry genuine clinical relevance when interpreted alongside symptoms, though an isolated titer rise without symptoms is not always an automatic trigger for treatment. Rituximab reduces but does not eliminate relapse risk, and this presentation is far more consistent with vasculitis activity than an unrelated infection.
  205. The correct answer is A (Pulmonary arterial hypertension recognized as a leading cause of overall mortality): Pulmonary arterial hypertension is a well recognized and leading cause of mortality in mixed connective tissue disease, so new progressive exertional dyspnea in this population should prompt urgent evaluation for this complication with echocardiography and additional targeted workup. Osteoarthritis, deconditioning, gout, and plantar fasciitis do not typically present with progressive dyspnea and would not explain this important red flag symptom occurring in a patient with this specific overlap syndrome.
  206. The correct answer is B (Avascular necrosis of the femoral head resulting from his chronic steroid use): Chronic corticosteroid use is a major recognized risk factor for avascular necrosis of the femoral head, which frequently shows normal early plain radiographs, making MRI necessary to detect early stage disease before femoral head collapse actually occurs. Osteoarthritis, bursitis, muscle strain, and referred lumbar pain do not carry this same strong association with steroid exposure and would not be the priority diagnosis to exclude in this specific clinical context.
  207. The correct answer is E (Reactivation tuberculosis remains a key concern despite her prior negative screening result): TNF inhibitors increase the risk of reactivating latent tuberculosis, and a prior negative screening result obtained years earlier does not rule out subsequent exposure or later reactivation, so cavitary lung disease accompanied by constitutional symptoms in this setting should prompt urgent tuberculosis evaluation. Assuming this is only a rheumatoid nodule or routine pneumonia risks missing an infectious process that TNF inhibition can predispose to and worsen, and a single prior screening does not confer any ongoing lasting protection.
  208. The correct answer is C (MRI of the sacroiliac joints performed specifically to detect early inflammation): MRI of the sacroiliac joints can detect early inflammatory changes such as bone marrow edema well before structural damage becomes visible on plain radiographs, making it the appropriate next step in a patient with strong clinical suspicion for axial spondyloarthritis and normal early films. A bone scan lacks the specificity that MRI offers for this particular purpose. Assuming exclusion based on normal radiographs alone would miss early nonradiographic disease, and starting biologic therapy without confirming the diagnosis skips necessary evaluation.
  209. The correct answer is E (Mucosa associated lymphoid tissue (MALT) lymphoma requiring prompt biopsy evaluation): Patients with longstanding Sjogren syndrome carry an increased risk of eventually developing MALT lymphoma, and new persistent unilateral parotid enlargement accompanied by lymphadenopathy should prompt biopsy to formally exclude this serious complication. Simple viral parotitis and sialolithiasis typically present quite differently and do not explain associated lymphadenopathy occurring alongside them. Dehydration causes transient rather than persistent asymmetric swelling, and artificial saliva products are not known to cause any glandular enlargement.
  210. The correct answer is C (Thrombosis occurring as a result of secondary antiphospholipid syndrome): The combination of positive antiphospholipid antibodies, recurrent early pregnancy loss, and an acute thrombotic event occurring in a lupus patient is characteristic of secondary antiphospholipid syndrome, in which an underlying hypercoagulable state drives arterial and venous thrombosis including stroke. True cerebral vasculitis is considerably less common and would not fully explain this particular antibody and pregnancy loss pattern together. Libman-Sacks endocarditis can contribute emboli but does not fully account for her overall serologic profile, and hypertension or hypoglycemia do not explain these findings.
  211. The correct answer is E (An interleukin-17 inhibitor effective for treating both his skin and joint disease): Interleukin-17 inhibitors are effective for treating both axial and peripheral psoriatic arthritis as well as extensive plaque psoriasis, making this class an appropriate choice after NSAID and TNF inhibitor failure in a patient with significant accompanying skin involvement. Switching NSAIDs is unlikely to succeed given his documented prior resistance to this drug class. Long term oral corticosteroids are generally avoided in psoriatic disease due to rebound flare risk upon withdrawal. Colchicine and hydroxychloroquine are not established effective therapies for either condition.
  212. The correct answer is B (Evolution toward or genuine coexistence with an underlying giant cell arteritis process): New jaw claudication accompanied by markedly elevated inflammatory markers despite adequate polymyalgia rheumatica treatment strongly suggests evolution toward or genuine coexistence with giant cell arteritis, which shares an underlying pathophysiology and frequently overlaps clinically with polymyalgia rheumatica. A simple relapse of polymyalgia rheumatica alone does not typically produce jaw claudication symptoms. Steroid myopathy instead causes proximal weakness rather than jaw pain, and a dental infection or nonadherence would not explain this specific new symptom pattern with worsening markers.
  213. The correct answer is C (Tracheobronchial cartilage collapse resulting from chronic inflammatory cartilage destruction): Relapsing polychondritis can cause progressive destruction and eventual collapse of the tracheobronchial cartilage, leading to airway obstruction and stridor that represents a genuinely life threatening emergency requiring urgent airway management in this setting. Anaphylaxis, stroke related vocal cord paralysis, foreign body aspiration, and viral laryngitis are all unrelated to the well established cartilage destroying pathophysiology of this specific disease and would not be the most direct explanation here.
  214. The correct answer is D (Systemic immunosuppression such as azathioprine or a biologic agent added promptly): Severe ocular involvement in Behcet disease, particularly posterior uveitis with retinal vasculitis threatening vision, requires systemic immunosuppression such as azathioprine or biologic therapy like a TNF inhibitor to help prevent permanent vision loss, going well beyond colchicine which mainly addresses mucocutaneous symptoms. Topical drops alone are insufficient for this degree of posterior segment disease. NSAIDs do not adequately control disease of this severity, and this presentation represents a genuine vision threatening emergency rather than something to simply observe.
  215. The correct answer is E (Bull's eye maculopathy resulting from hydroxychloroquine related retinal toxicity): After many years of cumulative hydroxychloroquine use, new central visual symptoms raise significant concern for bull's eye maculopathy, a form of irreversible retinal toxicity that becomes increasingly likely with cumulative dose and duration of therapy, making urgent ophthalmologic evaluation essential here. Cataracts and diabetic retinopathy are less specifically tied to her particular medication history and presentation described. Optic neuritis and dry eye syndrome do not typically produce this central bull's eye pattern associated with long term hydroxychloroquine exposure.
  216. The correct answer is B (Chest wall restriction resulting from severely limited thoracic cage expansion capacity): In advanced ankylosing spondylitis, fusion of the costovertebral joints limits overall chest wall expansion, producing a restrictive pattern on pulmonary function testing while preserving diffusion capacity since the lung parenchyma itself remains largely undiseased. True interstitial fibrosis would typically reduce diffusion capacity instead of preserving it. Pulmonary embolism produces an entirely different physiologic testing pattern, diaphragmatic paralysis is not a typical feature of ankylosing spondylitis, and NSAID use does not cause emphysema.
  217. The correct answer is D (Continuing hydroxychloroquine throughout the entire pregnancy is generally considered safe and recommended): Hydroxychloroquine is considered safe throughout pregnancy, and its continuation is generally recommended because stopping the medication meaningfully increases the risk of a lupus flare, which itself poses greater overall risk to both mother and fetus than the medication does. It should not simply be stopped because of pregnancy alone. Not all lupus medications carry an equal risk profile, so blanket discontinuation advice is inaccurate here, and substantial reassuring evidence actually supports its continued use during pregnancy.
  218. The correct answer is A (Neonatal congenital heart block resulting from transplacental maternal antibody transfer): Anti-Ro (SSA) antibodies can cross the placenta and damage the developing fetal cardiac conduction system, causing congenital heart block, which is why dedicated fetal echocardiographic monitoring is recommended throughout pregnancy in anti-Ro positive mothers. Neural tube defects instead relate to folate deficiency rather than this particular antibody. Fetal hydantoin syndrome and fetal alcohol syndrome relate to specific drug and alcohol exposures respectively, and congenital rubella syndrome results from viral infection, none of which are linked to anti-Ro antibody transfer.
  219. The correct answer is E (Starting allopurinol at a low dose and titrating slowly with close monitoring): In patients with renal impairment or a history of only a mild prior reaction, allopurinol can often be cautiously reintroduced starting at a low dose with slow titration and close ongoing monitoring, since renal impairment affects drug clearance and requires dosing adjustment. Permanently avoiding urate lowering therapy entirely leaves recurrent flares and progressive tissue damage unaddressed. Starting at the maximum dose or reusing the exact dose that previously caused a rash both increase the risk of a severe hypersensitivity reaction.
  220. The correct answer is B (Immediate blood pressure control using an ACE inhibitor with prompt renal function recheck): New severe hypertension occurring in a diffuse systemic sclerosis patient must be treated as possible scleroderma renal crisis until proven otherwise, requiring immediate blood pressure control with an ACE inhibitor along with urgent recheck of her renal function, since any delay can lead to irreversible kidney failure. Routine follow up or outpatient referral is far too slow for this potential true emergency. A beta blocker alone is not first line therapy for this specific crisis, and increasing immunosuppression alone does not address the acute hypertensive emergency itself.
  221. The correct answer is B (Stage 3, full-thickness loss with visible fat): Full-thickness skin loss with visible subcutaneous fat but no exposed bone, tendon, or muscle defines a stage 3 pressure injury. Stage 1 has intact skin, and stage 2 involves only partial-thickness loss with exposed dermis, both less severe than this wound. Stage 4 requires exposed bone, tendon, or muscle, which is not present here. Unstageable wounds have a base obscured by slough or eschar, which is not described in this case.
  222. The correct answer is D (Compression therapy with multilayer bandaging or stockings): Venous ulcers result from chronic venous insufficiency and edema, so compression therapy is the cornerstone of treatment to improve venous return and healing. Surgical debridement, systemic antibiotics, and casting are not standard first-line measures for uncomplicated venous ulcers. Corticosteroids would impair wound healing and are not indicated.
  223. The correct answer is A (Ability to understand, appreciate, and reason about the choice): Decisional capacity requires the patient to understand relevant information, appreciate its personal significance, reason through options, and communicate a choice. A cognitive screening score alone does not establish capacity, and disagreeing with the physician does not indicate incapacity. Legal guardianship documentation and absence of psychiatric diagnosis are not required elements of a capacity assessment for a specific decision.
  224. The correct answer is E (The adult son, based on legal next-of-kin priority): Most state default surrogate hierarchies prioritize legal next-of-kin such as an adult child over more distant relatives, regardless of relationship closeness. The niece would typically only serve as surrogate if no closer legal relative existed. Physicians and ethics committees do not serve as default surrogates, and court-appointed guardianship is not the first step when a legal next-of-kin is available.
  225. The correct answer is C (Calcium channel blockers and calcium supplements): Both calcium channel blockers like verapamil and calcium supplements are well recognized causes of constipation in older adults through effects on gut motility. SSRIs, ACE inhibitors, beta-2 agonists, and loop diuretics are not typically associated with significant constipation. This patient's regimen directly implicates the calcium-related medications as the cause.
  226.   227. The correct answer is B (No antibiotic treatment, since she is asymptomatic): Asymptomatic bacteriuria is common in older adults and should not be treated with antibiotics unless the patient has localized urinary symptoms or is undergoing an invasive urologic procedure. Treating bacteriuria without symptoms promotes antibiotic resistance without clinical benefit. Repeated cultures and empiric intravenous or oral antibiotics are not indicated in this asymptomatic scenario.
  228. The correct answer is E (Stress incontinence from pelvic floor weakness): Leakage triggered by increased intra-abdominal pressure such as coughing or sneezing, without urgency, is the classic presentation of stress incontinence from weakened pelvic floor support. Urge incontinence typically presents with a sudden strong urge to void, and overflow incontinence involves continuous dribbling from bladder overdistension. Mixed and functional incontinence involve additional features not described here.
  229. The correct answer is D (Gradual onset with preserved consciousness): Dementia typically develops gradually over months to years with a clear sensorium, unlike delirium, which has an acute onset and altered consciousness. Fluctuating attention and acute onset over hours to days are hallmarks of delirium, not dementia. Disorganized speech with hallucinations and reversibility are also more characteristic of delirium than progressive dementia.
  230. The correct answer is C (Presbycusis from age-related cochlear degeneration): Symmetric high-frequency sensorineural hearing loss with difficulty understanding speech in noisy environments is the classic presentation of presbycusis. Otosclerosis and cerumen impaction cause conductive rather than sensorineural loss. Meniere disease typically presents with episodic vertigo and fluctuating hearing loss, and acoustic neuroma usually causes asymmetric, unilateral hearing loss.
  231. The correct answer is E (Its strong anticholinergic effects raise fall and confusion risk): Diphenhydramine has potent anticholinergic properties that increase the risk of sedation, confusion, and falls in older adults, which is why Beers criteria flag it as potentially inappropriate. It does not require renal monitoring, interact with grapefruit juice, cause hyperkalemia, or require therapeutic drug level monitoring.
  232. The correct answer is A (Multifactorial risk from medication, cardiovascular, and muscular factors): This patient has several concurrent risk factors, including sedating medication, orthostatic hypotension, and muscle weakness, illustrating the multifactorial nature of falls in older adults. None of the other options accurately capture that multiple independent contributors are present, since the scenario describes medication, cardiovascular, and muscular issues together rather than a single isolated cause.
  233. The correct answer is D (Repositioning at least every two hours on a standard mattress): Standard guidelines recommend repositioning immobile patients at least every two hours on a standard support surface to relieve pressure and prevent injury progression. Waiting for symptoms, repositioning only every four or eight hours, or repositioning only after incontinence episodes all allow prolonged unrelieved pressure that increases injury risk.
  234. The correct answer is B (Acute onset with fluctuation, inattention, plus disorganized thinking or altered consciousness): The Confusion Assessment Method requires acute onset with a fluctuating course and inattention, plus either disorganized thinking or an altered level of consciousness, to diagnose delirium. The other option combinations omit required core features or substitute unrelated criteria such as family history or infection findings that are not part of the CAM algorithm.
  235. The correct answer is C (Early mobilization and weight bearing within a day of surgery): Early mobilization after hip fracture repair reduces complications such as pneumonia, deconditioning, and delirium while improving functional outcomes. Prolonged bed rest and delayed physical therapy worsen deconditioning and complication risk. Prophylactic long-term opioids and prolonged catheterization increase risks of delirium and infection rather than improving recovery.
  236. The correct answer is A (Ask him to explain the risks and reasoning behind his choice): Capacity assessment requires evaluating the patient's understanding, appreciation, and reasoning for the specific decision at hand, not simply his diagnosis or a screening score. A dementia diagnosis alone does not equal incapacity, and decisions should not be deferred automatically to family members without a proper individualized assessment.
  237. The correct answer is E (Mini Nutritional Assessment): The Mini Nutritional Assessment is a validated tool specifically designed to screen older adults for malnutrition risk in outpatient and long-term care settings. The Confusion Assessment Method screens for delirium, the Katz Index measures functional independence, the Timed Up and Go assesses mobility and fall risk, and the Geriatric Depression Scale screens for depressive symptoms, none of which assess nutritional status.
  238. The correct answer is D (Montreal Cognitive Assessment, which is sensitive to mild deficits): The Montreal Cognitive Assessment is more sensitive than other brief tools for detecting mild cognitive impairment and executive dysfunction. The Geriatric Depression Scale and Katz scale assess mood and function rather than cognition, the Glasgow Coma Scale measures consciousness after acute injury, and the Braden Scale evaluates pressure injury risk, none of which screen for cognitive impairment.
  239. The correct answer is B (Urinalysis to rule out infection or hematuria): A urinalysis is an inexpensive, noninvasive initial test that can identify infection, hematuria, or other reversible contributors to new incontinence before pursuing more invasive testing. Urodynamic studies, cystoscopy, pelvic MRI, and renal ultrasound are more specialized tests reserved for cases that remain unclear after basic initial evaluation.
  240. The correct answer is C (Report suspected elder mistreatment to adult protective services): Clinicians are mandated reporters who must report reasonable suspicion of elder mistreatment to adult protective services, even without a direct disclosure from the patient. Waiting for explicit disclosure, confronting the caregiver directly, or simply documenting without reporting could place the patient at continued risk. Physical therapy referral does not address the underlying safety concern.
  241. The correct answer is A (Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia): Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia is the recommended first-line treatment for chronic insomnia in older adults because it addresses underlying behavioral and cognitive contributors without medication risks. Benzodiazepines, diphenhydramine, sedating antipsychotics, and long-term zolpidem all carry increased risks of falls, confusion, and dependence in this population and are not preferred first-line options.
  242. The correct answer is E (Titrate opioids to control pain under the principle of double effect): The principle of double effect supports appropriately titrating opioids to relieve suffering even if there is a foreseen but unintended risk of hastening death, as long as the intent is symptom control. Withholding treatment, using only as-needed dosing, stopping opioids for NSAIDs alone, or jumping straight to sedation would leave pain undertreated or bypass appropriate stepwise management.
  243. The correct answer is B (Convene an ethics consultation to clarify substituted judgment): When surrogates disagree and no documented wishes exist, an ethics consultation helps clarify the patient's likely preferences through substituted judgment and mediates the conflict. Deferring to birth order, unilateral physician decision-making, defaulting to maximal treatment, or picking a legal next-of-kin without discussion all bypass appropriate shared decision-making processes.
  244. The correct answer is D (Superimposed delirium precipitated by the new medication): Acute agitation, fluctuating attention, and disorganized speech following a new medication in a patient with baseline dementia strongly suggests superimposed delirium rather than simple disease progression. Dementia progression is gradual, not acute, sundowning does not typically produce this degree of disorganization, and pseudodementia and primary psychiatric disease do not explain the temporal link to the new medication.
  245.   246.   247. The correct answer is E (Refer for surgical or wound specialist evaluation of the wound): A deep pressure injury with exposed bone that is worsening despite standard care warrants specialist evaluation for advanced wound management or possible surgical intervention.   248. The correct answer is B (Overflow incontinence from anticholinergic-induced urinary retention): In a man with underlying prostatic obstruction, adding an anticholinergic medication can precipitate urinary retention and overflow incontinence, presenting as constant dribbling with a distended bladder.   249. The correct answer is D (Depression presenting with pseudodementia): Recent bereavement, low mood, weight loss, and poor effort on cognitive testing are classic features suggesting depression-related pseudodementia rather than a primary dementia process. Alzheimer disease and vascular dementia typically show consistent effort with genuine cognitive deficits, normal age-related decline would not include poor effort or mood symptoms, and frontotemporal dementia usually presents with behavioral or personality changes rather than this pattern.
  250. The correct answer is C (Topical medications to lower intraocular pressure prevent further nerve damage): This presentation is consistent with open-angle glaucoma, and topical intraocular pressure lowering agents are the mainstay treatment to slow optic nerve damage progression. Glaucoma classically causes peripheral vision loss first, not central vision loss, and existing nerve damage is not reversible even with pressure control. Anti-VEGF injections treat macular degeneration, and glaucoma of this type is usually gradual and painless rather than sudden and painful.
